=== py4-01 | Debugging: closures and late binding
--- teach
The advanced course ended with binary search: throw away half the range at every step until the answer is pinned down. This expert course goes back inside Python itself. First comes the machinery serious code is built on (closures, descriptors, class hooks and coroutines; each gets its own lesson, so the names need not mean anything yet). Then typing, testing and designing code so it is easy to change. Last come hard problems that need a real algorithm.

It opens with a bug. You make several functions that should each do something different, and they all do the same thing. To see why, you need to know exactly how a closure remembers.

**A reminder: what a closure is.** You met closures in the decorators lesson. A function defined inside another function can keep using the outer function's variables, even after the outer function has returned. The inner function together with the variables it remembers is a **closure**.

```python
def make_greeter(greeting):
    def greet(name):
        return f"{greeting}, {name}"
    return greet

hello = make_greeter("hello")
print(hello("ada"))     # hello, ada
```

By the time `hello("ada")` runs, `make_greeter` has already finished. Yet `greet` still knows `greeting`. Two rules about *how* it remembers cause most closure bugs.

**Rule 1: a closure keeps the variable, not the value.** Think of a variable as a labeled box. A closure does not take a photo of what is in the box when the function is made. It keeps the box itself, and opens it to look inside *each time the function runs*. Looking a name up when the function runs, instead of when it was made, is called **[[late binding|late-binding-name]]**.

```python
def make():
    n = 1
    def show():
        return n
    n = 2              # change the box after show was made
    return show

print(make()())        # 2, not 1
```

`show` was made while `n` was 1. But it only looks inside the box when it is called, and by then the box holds 2.

**Where it bites: functions made in a loop.** Here a loop makes three small functions with a **[[lambda|lambda-recap]]**. Each one should return the loop number it was made in:

```python
funcs = [lambda: i for i in range(3)]
print([f() for f in funcs])     # [2, 2, 2], not [0, 1, 2]
```

There is only **one** box called `i`. The loop puts 0 in it, then 1, then 2. All three lambdas share that one box. When you finally call them, the loop is over and the box holds 2, so every one of them says 2.

**Fix A: a default argument.** Default values are **[[worked out once, when the function is defined|defaults-once]]**. So a default can take a snapshot of the value right now:

```python
funcs = [lambda i=i: i for i in range(3)]
print([f() for f in funcs])     # [0, 1, 2]
```

Read `lambda i=i: i` aloud as "a function with a parameter called `i`, whose default is the value the loop's `i` has at this moment". Each lambda now has its own `i`, filled in when it was made.

**Fix B: a factory function.** A **factory** is a function whose job is to make and return another function. Every call to a function gets brand-new local variables, so every call to the factory makes a fresh box:

```python
def make(i):
    return lambda: i        # this i belongs to this one call of make

funcs = [make(i) for i in range(3)]
print([f() for f in funcs])     # [0, 1, 2]
```

Both fixes work. The factory is the cleaner one, because it does not add an extra parameter a caller could fill in by mistake.

This bug turns up most often when a program makes one **[[event handler|event-handlers]]** per button in a loop: every button ends up doing what the last one should.

**Rule 2: assigning to a name makes it local.** Reading an outer variable is fine, as `greet` did with `greeting`. *Assigning* to it is different. Before a function runs, Python scans its body. Any name that is assigned anywhere inside it (with `=`, `+=` and friends) is treated as that function's own **local** variable for the *whole* body.

```python
def make_counter():
    count = 0
    def increment():
        count += 1          # crashes
        return count
    return increment

make_counter()()
# UnboundLocalError: cannot access local variable 'count' where it is not associated with a value
```

`count += 1` means `count = count + 1`. That assignment makes `count` local to `increment`. So the right-hand side tries to read the *local* `count`, which has no value yet. Python stops with **[[UnboundLocalError|unbound-local]]**: a local variable was read before anything was put in it.

The fix is one line. `nonlocal count` tells Python "`count` is not mine; it is the box belonging to the function around me":

```python
def make_counter():
    count = 0
    def increment():
        nonlocal count
        count += 1
        return count
    return increment

c = make_counter()
print(c(), c(), c())    # 1 2 3
```

Each call to `make_counter()` makes a new `count` box, so two counters never share a count.

**How to debug it: look inside the closure.** When several functions "all return the same thing", suspect a shared variable. You can check. A function made inside another function has a `__closure__`: a tuple of **[[cells|closure-cells]]**, the boxes it remembers. `.cell_contents` shows what is in a box right now:

```python
funcs = [lambda: i for i in range(3)]
print(funcs[0].__closure__[0].cell_contents)                 # 2
print(funcs[0].__closure__[0] is funcs[2].__closure__[0])    # True: one shared box
```

`is` asks "is this the very same object?". `True` here proves the first and last function share one box. After the factory fix the same test prints `False`.

**Watch out:** the bug hides if you test each function *inside* the loop, right after making it. At that moment the box still holds the "right" value, so everything looks fine. It only goes wrong later, once the loop has moved on. Always test the functions after the loop has finished, the way the real program will call them.

::: context late-binding-name What "binding" means
To **bind** a name is to connect it to a value. *Late* binding means Python makes that connection as late as possible: at the moment the line runs, not when the function was written or created.

It is the same reason a function can use a variable that is only created further down the file, as long as the variable exists by the time the function is called. Python does not check names in advance; it looks them up at run time.

Late binding is a feature, not a flaw. It is what lets `make_counter` update its count. It only becomes a bug when you expected a snapshot and got a live box instead.
:::

::: context lambda-recap A lambda is a one-line function
You met `lambda` in the sorting lesson. `lambda x: x * 2` is a small function with no name. It is the same as writing:

```python
def double(x):
    return x * 2
```

A lambda holds one expression and returns its value automatically. `lambda: i` takes no arguments at all and returns `i`.

Lambdas follow exactly the same closure rules as `def` functions. They only look different; they remember variables the same way.
:::

::: context defaults-once The mutable-default lesson, turned into a tool
In the intermediate course, a list used as a default argument surprised you: it was created once, when the `def` ran, and then shared by every call.

That same fact, "defaults are worked out once, at definition time", is exactly what fixes late binding. `lambda i=i: i` copies the value of `i` into the default at the moment the lambda is made. The behavior that caused one bug is the cure for another. Knowing *when* Python evaluates each thing is most of expert debugging.
:::

::: context event-handlers Buttons, handlers and ground stations
An **event** is something that happens while a program runs: a click, a key press, a message arriving. An **event handler** is the function the program calls when that event happens.

A mission-control screen might make one button per command from a list: "open valve", "arm", "abort". If each button's handler is a lambda made in the loop, every button can end up sending the last command in the list. A button labeled "open valve" that sends "abort" is a serious bug, and it is exactly this one. The task's `make_handlers` is a small model of it.
:::

::: context unbound-local Why Python decides "local" in advance
Python reads a whole function before it runs it, and decides once which names are local. It does not decide line by line. So an assignment on the *last* line of a function makes the name local on the *first* line too.

That is why the error is so confusing: the line that crashes looks like it only reads `count`. The cause is the assignment hidden inside `+=`.

There are two keywords to override the rule. `nonlocal x` means "the `x` of the function around me". `global x` means "the `x` at the top level of the file". Reaching for `global` is usually a sign the design needs rethinking; `nonlocal` inside a closure is normal.
:::

::: context closure-cells One shared box versus a box each
A **cell** is Python's name for the box a closure keeps. Functions made in a loop without a fix all point at one cell. With a factory, each call makes its own cell.

```svg

```

`__closure__` is a tuple because a closure can remember several variables: one cell per variable, in a fixed order.
:::
--- task
**Bug report:** three functions in this file misbehave. Fix all three.

1. `make_multipliers(4)` should return four functions, where the function at position `k` multiplies its argument by `k` (so the first multiplies by 0, the last by 3). Instead, all of them multiply by 3.
2. `make_handlers(["save", "open"])` should return a dictionary mapping each name to a function that returns `"clicked <name>"`. Instead, every handler says `"clicked open"`.
3. `make_counter()` should return a function that counts 1, 2, 3… each time it is called, but calling it crashes with `UnboundLocalError`. Two counters must count separately.
--- starter
def make_multipliers(n):
    return [lambda x: i * x for i in range(n)]


def make_handlers(names):
    handlers = {}
    for name in names:
        handlers[name] = lambda: f"clicked {name}"
    return handlers


def make_counter():
    count = 0

    def increment():
        count += 1
        return count

    return increment
--- solution
def make_multipliers(n):
    def times(k):
        return lambda x: k * x
    return [times(i) for i in range(n)]


def make_handlers(names):
    handlers = {}
    for name in names:
        handlers[name] = lambda name=name: f"clicked {name}"
    return handlers


def make_counter():
    count = 0

    def increment():
        nonlocal count
        count += 1
        return count

    return increment
--- hint
Bugs 1 and 2 are late binding: every function shares one loop variable. Call one of the multipliers' `__closure__[0].cell_contents`, or compare two of their cells with `is`, to see it.
--- hint
Take a snapshot of the value when each function is made: either a default argument (`lambda name=name: ...`) or a small factory function defined inside `make_multipliers` that takes `k` and returns `lambda x: k * x`.
--- hint
Bug 3 is Rule 2: `count += 1` assigns to `count`, which makes it local to `increment`. Add `nonlocal count` as the first line inside `increment`.
--- check case | Each multiplier uses its own k
[f(10) for f in make_multipliers(4)]
=> [0, 10, 20, 30]
--- check case | Each handler remembers its own name
{name: h() for name, h in make_handlers(["save", "open", "quit"]).items()}
=> {"save": "clicked save", "open": "clicked open", "quit": "clicked quit"}
--- check case | The counter counts
(lambda c: [c(), c(), c()])(make_counter())
=> [1, 2, 3]
--- check test | Two counters are independent
(lambda a, b: (a(), a(), b()) == (1, 2, 1))(make_counter(), make_counter())

+++ practice | Adders made in a loop
--- task
Write `make_adders(amounts)`. It returns a list of functions, one for each number in `amounts`, in the same order. The function at position `k` takes one number and returns that number plus `amounts[k]`.

The functions must give the right answers when they are called later, after the whole list has been built. For example, `[f(100) for f in make_adders([1, 5, 10])]` gives `[101, 105, 110]`. An empty list of amounts gives `[]`.
--- starter
def make_adders(amounts):
    return []
--- solution
def make_adders(amounts):
    def adder(amount):
        return lambda x: x + amount
    return [adder(a) for a in amounts]
--- hint
One lambda per amount, written straight into a comprehension, shares a single loop variable with all the others. Call the functions after the list is built to see what that does.
--- hint
Give each function a box of its own: a small factory inside `make_adders` that takes one amount and returns `lambda x: x + amount`. Call it once for each amount.
--- check case | Each adder adds its own amount
[f(100) for f in make_adders([1, 5, 10])]
=> [101, 105, 110]
--- check case | No amounts, no functions
make_adders([])
=> []
--- check case | Negative and repeated amounts
[f(0) for f in make_adders([-3, 7, 7])]
=> [-3, 7, 7]
--- check test | Two lists of adders do not interfere
(lambda a, b: (a[0](1), b[0](1)) == (3, 11))(make_adders([2]), make_adders([10]))

+++ practice | A running average
--- task
Write `make_averager()`. It returns a function `add(x)`. Each call adds the number `x` and returns the average of every number added so far, as a `float`.

For example, if `avg = make_averager()`, then `avg(10)` gives `10.0`, `avg(20)` gives `15.0` and `avg(0)` gives `10.0`.

Do not keep a list of the numbers. Keep only a running total and a count, in variables of `make_averager`, and change them from inside `add`. Two averagers must never share their numbers.
--- starter
def make_averager():
    def add(x):
        return x
    return add
--- solution
def make_averager():
    total = 0
    count = 0

    def add(x):
        nonlocal total
        nonlocal count
        total += x
        count += 1
        return total / count

    return add
--- hint
`add` has to change two variables that belong to `make_averager`. Assigning to them inside `add` makes them local to `add`, unless you say otherwise.
--- hint
Start `total = 0` and `count = 0` in `make_averager`. Inside `add`, write `nonlocal total` and `nonlocal count` first, then update both and return `total / count`.
--- check case | The average after each number
(lambda avg: [avg(10), avg(20), avg(0)])(make_averager())
=> [10.0, 15.0, 10.0]
--- check test | Two averagers keep their own numbers
(lambda a, b: (a(4), a(8), b(100)) == (4.0, 6.0, 100.0))(make_averager(), make_averager())
--- check case | Negative numbers
(lambda avg: [avg(-2), avg(-4)])(make_averager())
=> [-2.0, -3.0]
--- check test | The average is always a float
type(make_averager()(3)) is float
--- check source absent | Does not keep a list of every number
\.append\(

+++ practice | A decorator that stops after n calls
--- task
Write a decorator with an argument, `limit_calls(n)`. A function decorated with `@limit_calls(n)` works normally for its first `n` calls, and returns whatever it returns. From call number `n + 1` on, it does not run the function at all. Instead it raises `RuntimeError` with the message `"<name> was called more than <n> times"`, where `<name>` is the function's `__name__`. For example, after two calls, a third call to a function named `abs` decorated with `@limit_calls(2)` raises `RuntimeError("abs was called more than 2 times")`.

- Each decorated function counts its own calls.
- Use `@functools.wraps(func)` on the wrapper, and pass every argument through with `*args, **kwargs`.
- Keep the count in a variable of the enclosing function and change it with `nonlocal`.
--- starter
import functools


def limit_calls(n):
    def decorator(func):
        return func
    return decorator
--- solution
import functools


def limit_calls(n):
    def decorator(func):
        calls = 0

        @functools.wraps(func)
        def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
            nonlocal calls
            if calls >= n:
                raise RuntimeError(f"{func.__name__} was called more than {n} times")
            calls += 1
            return func(*args, **kwargs)

        return wrapper
    return decorator
--- hint
This is the three-layer shape of `repeat` from the decorators lesson: `limit_calls(n)` returns `decorator`, and `decorator(func)` returns `wrapper`. The count belongs in `decorator`, so each decorated function gets its own.
--- hint
Set `calls = 0` inside `decorator`, before `def wrapper`. In the wrapper, write `nonlocal calls` first. If `calls >= n`, raise the error before calling `func`; otherwise add one and `return func(*args, **kwargs)`.
--- check case | The first calls work normally
(lambda f: [f(-1), f(-2)])(limit_calls(2)(abs))
=> [1, 2]
--- check test | The call after the limit raises RuntimeError
(lambda f: (f(1), f(2), raises(RuntimeError, lambda: f(3)))[2])(limit_calls(2)(abs))
--- check case | The message names the function and the limit
(lambda f: (f(5), (lambda g: (exec("try:\n    f(6)\nexcept RuntimeError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g["msg"])[1])({"f": f}))[1])(limit_calls(1)(abs))
=> "abs was called more than 1 times"
--- check test | Over the limit, the function does not run at all
(lambda log: (lambda f: (f(), f(), raises(RuntimeError, f), len(log)))(limit_calls(2)(lambda: log.append(1)))[3] == 2)([])
--- check test | Each decorated function counts on its own
(lambda f, g: (f(1), raises(RuntimeError, lambda: f(1)), g(-4))[2] == 4)(limit_calls(1)(abs), limit_calls(1)(abs))
--- check test | Keeps the name and passes keyword arguments through
(lambda f: f.__name__ == "sorted" and f([3, 1, 2], reverse=True) == [3, 2, 1])(limit_calls(5)(sorted))
--- check test | A limit of 0 refuses the very first call
raises(RuntimeError, lambda: limit_calls(0)(abs)(1))
--- check source | Keeps the count with nonlocal
\bnonlocal\b

+++ practice | A cycle of states
--- task
Write `make_cycle(states)`. It returns a function that takes no arguments. Each call returns the next item of `states`, and after the last one it starts again from the first. For `make_cycle(["off", "on"])`, five calls give `"off"`, `"on"`, `"off"`, `"on"`, `"off"`.

Get these cases right:

- If `states` is empty, `make_cycle` itself raises `ValueError` straight away, not later when the function is called.
- With one state, every call returns that state.
- If the caller changes their list after making the cycle, the cycle does not change. Take a copy with `list(states)` when the cycle is made. That also lets `states` be a tuple or a string.
- Two cycles made from the same list move on independently.
--- starter
def make_cycle(states):
    position = 0

    def next_state():
        return states[position]

    return next_state
--- solution
def make_cycle(states):
    if not states:
        raise ValueError("make_cycle needs at least one state")
    states = list(states)
    position = 0

    def next_state():
        nonlocal position
        state = states[position]
        position = (position + 1) % len(states)
        return state

    return next_state
--- hint
The closure keeps the variable `states`, which points at the caller's own list. Point that variable at a copy before you make `next_state`, and check for an empty list before that too.
--- hint
Inside `next_state`, say `nonlocal position`, remember the state at the current position, then move on with `position = (position + 1) % len(states)`. The remainder `%` takes it back to 0 after the last state.
--- check case | Cycles round and round
(lambda c: [c() for _ in range(5)])(make_cycle(["off", "on"]))
=> ["off", "on", "off", "on", "off"]
--- check test | An empty list is refused when the cycle is made
raises(ValueError, lambda: make_cycle([]))
--- check case | One state repeats
(lambda c: [c(), c(), c()])(make_cycle(["idle"]))
=> ["idle", "idle", "idle"]
--- check test | Changing the list afterwards changes nothing
(lambda s: (lambda c: (s.append("C"), s.__setitem__(0, "Z"), [c(), c(), c()])[2])(make_cycle(s)))(["A", "B"]) == ["A", "B", "A"]
--- check test | Two cycles from one list move independently
(lambda s: (lambda a, b: (a(), a(), b()) == ("x", "y", "x"))(make_cycle(s), make_cycle(s)))(["x", "y", "z"])
--- check case | A tuple works too
(lambda c: [c(), c(), c(), c()])(make_cycle(("r", "g", "b")))
=> ["r", "g", "b", "r"]

+++ practice | Debug: id makers that share a count
--- task
`make_id_maker(prefix)` should return a function that hands out ids `"<prefix>-1"`, `"<prefix>-2"`, `"<prefix>-3"` and so on, one per call. So `make_id_maker("T")` gives a function whose calls return `"T-1"`, `"T-2"`, `"T-3"`.

Someone got an `UnboundLocalError` and "fixed" it with `global`. Now every id maker shares one count: after a `"T"` maker has handed out two ids, a brand-new `"R"` maker starts at `"R-3"`. Fix it so each id maker counts on its own, starting at 1, with no global count left in the file.
--- starter
count = 0


def make_id_maker(prefix):
    def next_id():
        global count
        count += 1
        return prefix + "-" + str(count)

    return next_id
--- solution
def make_id_maker(prefix):
    count = 0

    def next_id():
        nonlocal count
        count += 1
        return prefix + "-" + str(count)

    return next_id
--- hint
Where does the count live now? There is one `count` for the whole file, so every id maker changes the same box.
--- hint
Move `count = 0` inside `make_id_maker`, so each call to it makes a fresh box, and change `global count` to `nonlocal count`.
--- check case | One id maker counts 1, 2, 3
(lambda m: [m(), m(), m()])(make_id_maker("T"))
=> ["T-1", "T-2", "T-3"]
--- check test | Two id makers count separately
(lambda t, r: (t(), t(), r(), t()) == ("T-1", "T-2", "R-1", "T-3"))(make_id_maker("T"), make_id_maker("R"))
--- check test | A new id maker starts at 1, whatever the others did
(lambda a: (a(), a(), make_id_maker("B")())[2] == "B-1")(make_id_maker("A"))
--- check source absent | No global count
\bglobal\b

+++ practice | Alarms for many sensors
--- task
A ground station watches several sensors. Write two functions.

- `make_alarm(limit, times)` returns a function `check(reading)`. `check` returns `True` when the last `times` readings in a row, including this one, were all above `limit`, and `False` otherwise. A reading equal to `limit` is not above it, and it breaks the run. For `make_alarm(100, 3)`, the readings 101, 105 and 102 give `False`, `False`, `True`, and a fourth high reading gives `True` again.
- `build_alarms(config)` takes a dict from each sensor's name to a `(limit, times)` pair. It returns a dict from each name to its own `check` function, one for each entry of `config`.

Every alarm keeps its own run of high readings, and uses its own limit and times.
--- starter
def make_alarm(limit, times):
    def check(reading):
        return reading > limit
    return check


def build_alarms(config):
    alarms = {}
    for name, (limit, times) in config.items():
        alarms[name] = lambda reading: reading > limit
    return alarms
--- solution
def make_alarm(limit, times):
    run = 0

    def check(reading):
        nonlocal run
        if reading > limit:
            run += 1
        else:
            run = 0
        return run >= times

    return check


def build_alarms(config):
    return {name: make_alarm(limit, times) for name, (limit, times) in config.items()}
--- hint
`check` needs a memory: how many high readings in a row it has seen. Keep that number in `make_alarm` and change it with `nonlocal`. Add one for a high reading, go back to 0 otherwise, and compare with `times`.
--- hint
`make_alarm` is already a factory. In `build_alarms`, call it once for each entry instead of writing a lambda in the loop, and every sensor gets its own limit, times and run.
--- check case | Three high readings in a row set it off
(lambda c: [c(r) for r in [101, 105, 102, 150, 100, 101]])(make_alarm(100, 3))
=> [False, False, True, True, False, False]
--- check case | With times 1, every high reading sets it off
(lambda c: [c(r) for r in [5, 6, 4]])(make_alarm(5, 1))
=> [False, True, False]
--- check test | Each alarm uses its own limit and times
(lambda a: [a["temp"](r) for r in (90, 91)] == [False, True] and [a["volts"](r) for r in (30, 31, 32)] == [False, False, True])(build_alarms({"temp": (80, 2), "volts": (20, 3)}))
--- check test | Alarms keep separate runs
(lambda a: (a["x"](9), a["y"](9), a["x"](9)) == (False, False, True))(build_alarms({"x": (0, 2), "y": (0, 2)}))
--- check case | No sensors, no alarms
build_alarms({})
=> {}

=== py4-02 | Descriptors: how @property really works
--- teach
Last lesson showed exactly how a closure finds its variables. This one shows how Python finds an **attribute**: what really happens when you write `obj.attr`. In the intermediate course you used `@property` to check a value whenever it was set. Here you build the machinery underneath `@property` yourself, and then use it to write a checker once and reuse it on every field.

**Step 1: every object has its own dictionary.** When you write `self.x = 3`, Python stores it in a dictionary that belongs to that one object, called `__dict__`. The built-in `vars(obj)` shows the same dictionary.

```python
class Point:
    def __init__(self, x, y):
        self.x = x
        self.y = y

p = Point(3, 4)
print(p.__dict__)     # {'x': 3, 'y': 4}
print(vars(p))        # {'x': 3, 'y': 4}
```

**Step 2: a class has attributes too.** A name set directly in the class body, not on `self`, is a **[[class attribute|class-vs-instance]]**. It belongs to the class and is shared by every object made from it. It does not appear in any object's `__dict__`.

```python
class Ship:
    fleet = "Artemis"          # class attribute: one, shared
    def __init__(self, name):
        self.name = name       # instance attribute: one per object

a = Ship("Orion")
print(a.fleet)        # Artemis   (found on the class)
print(vars(a))        # {'name': 'Orion'}
```

**Step 3: a descriptor answers for the attribute.** Picture a hotel. Usually, when you ask for something, you go and get it from your own room. But for a few things the hotel has put a receptionist at the front desk, and anyone who asks for *those* gets their answer from the receptionist instead.

A **[[descriptor|descriptor-word]]** is that receptionist: an object with a `__get__` method, stored as a class attribute. When you read `obj.attr`, Python first checks the *class* for an attribute of that name. If it finds a descriptor there, Python calls its `__get__` and hands you whatever it returns.

```python
class Loud:
    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        return "HELLO"

class Thing:
    greeting = Loud()     # a descriptor, stored on the CLASS

print(Thing().greeting)   # HELLO   (Loud.__get__ was called)
```

`__get__(self, obj, owner)` gets three things:

- `self`: the descriptor itself (the `Loud` object).
- `obj`: the object the attribute was read from. When it is read from the class itself, as in `Thing.greeting`, there is no object, so `obj` is `None`. By convention the descriptor then returns itself.
- `owner`: the class (`Thing`).

**Step 4: `__set__` catches assignments.** Give a descriptor a `__set__(self, obj, value)` method too, and every `obj.attr = value` calls it instead of storing the value directly. A descriptor with `__set__` is a **data descriptor**, and it **[[wins over the object's own dictionary|lookup-order]]**: Python always asks it first.

**Step 5: `__set_name__` tells it its name.** When the class is created, Python calls `__set_name__(self, owner, name)` on each descriptor once, passing the name it was assigned to. For `username = NonEmpty()`, `name` is `"username"`. The descriptor saves it as `self.name` so it can use it later.

**Step 6: where the value lives.** Not on the descriptor! There is one descriptor per *class*, shared by every object. Each object's value goes in that object's own `__dict__`, under the attribute's name, **[[written straight into the dictionary|why-dict-direct]]**: `obj.__dict__[self.name] = value`.

Put together, here is a reusable "must not be empty" checker. With `@property` you would write the same getter and setter again for every field. A descriptor writes it once:

```python
class NonEmpty:
    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name
    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        return obj.__dict__[self.name]
    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if not value:
            raise ValueError(f"{self.name} must not be empty")
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value

class User:
    username = NonEmpty()
    email = NonEmpty()
    def __init__(self, username, email):
        self.username = username      # goes through NonEmpty.__set__
        self.email = email

u = User("ada", "ada@example.com")
print(vars(u))       # {'username': 'ada', 'email': 'ada@example.com'}
User("", "x@example.com")
# ValueError: username must not be empty
```

The check runs both when the object is made and on any later assignment such as `u.email = ""`.

**Checking for a number.** `isinstance(value, (int, float))` is true when `value` is an `int` *or* a `float`: a tuple of types means "any of these". So `isinstance(2.5, (int, float))` is `True` and `isinstance("5", (int, float))` is `False`. One surprise: in Python **[[True counts as a whole number|bool-is-int]]**, so `isinstance(True, int)` is `True`. A careful number check also rejects `bool`.

**Step 7: a descriptor with only `__get__`.** Without `__set__` it is a **non-data descriptor**, and now the object's own `__dict__` wins over it. That allows a neat trick: the first time the attribute is read, `__get__` computes the value and stores it in the object's `__dict__` under the *same name*. From then on Python finds the stored value first and never calls `__get__` again. The standard library's `functools.cached_property` works exactly this way, to **[[compute once, then remember|cached-property]]**:

```python
from functools import cached_property

class Report:
    @cached_property
    def summary(self):
        print("working...")
        return "all systems go"

r = Report()
print(r.summary)    # prints working... and then all systems go
print(r.summary)    # all systems go   (no "working..." this time)
print(vars(r))      # {'summary': 'all systems go'}
```

`@cached_property` above a method works like any decorator: `summary = cached_property(summary)`. The class attribute `summary` is then the descriptor, and it is given the method to call.

This one rule is how `@property` itself works: `property` is a data descriptor built into Python. Even plain **[[methods|methods-are-descriptors]]** are found this way.

**Watch out:** storing the value on the descriptor, as `self.value = value` inside `__set__`. It looks fine with one object. But every object shares the one descriptor, so they all share one value: make a product priced 1, then one priced 2, and the first product's price is now 2 as well. Always store in `obj.__dict__`.

::: context class-vs-instance One descriptor, many objects
A class attribute lives on the class, once. Each object has its own `__dict__` for its own data. A descriptor is a class attribute, so every object uses the same descriptor, but each object's value lives in that object's dictionary.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 160" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="100" y="10" width="160" height="50" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="180" y="30" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">class Product</text>
  <text x="180" y="48" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">price = Positive()</text>
  <line x1="150" y1="60" x2="90" y2="98" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <line x1="210" y1="60" x2="270" y2="98" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <rect x="15" y="98" width="150" height="50" rx="6" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="90" y="118" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">pen.__dict__</text>
  <text x="90" y="137" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">{'price': 1.5}</text>
  <rect x="195" y="98" width="150" height="50" rx="6" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <text x="270" y="118" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">cup.__dict__</text>
  <text x="270" y="137" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">{'price': 3}</text>
</svg>
```

Changing a class attribute changes it for everyone at once, which is useful for shared settings and dangerous for anything that should be per object.
:::

::: context descriptor-word Why "descriptor"
The object *describes* how an attribute behaves: what happens when it is read, set, or looked up on the class. The attribute itself is just a name; the descriptor is the set of rules behind it.

The methods `__get__`, `__set__` and `__set_name__` are **special methods** (the double-underscore kind, sometimes called "dunder" methods, from "double underscore"), like `__repr__` and `__eq__` in the intermediate course. You never call them yourself; Python calls them at the right moment.
:::

::: context lookup-order The order Python searches in
When you read `obj.attr`, Python checks in this order:

1. A **data descriptor** (has `__set__`) named `attr` on the class. If there is one, its `__get__` answers.
2. The object's own `__dict__`. If `attr` is there, that value is the answer.
3. A **non-data descriptor** (only `__get__`), or a plain class attribute, on the class.
4. Otherwise, `AttributeError`.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 80" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <g font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">
    <rect x="5" y="20" width="100" height="40" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="55" y="37">1. data</text><text x="55" y="52">descriptor</text>
    <rect x="130" y="20" width="100" height="40" rx="6" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="180" y="37">2. obj.__dict__</text><text x="180" y="52">entry</text>
    <rect x="255" y="20" width="100" height="40" rx="6" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="305" y="37">3. non-data</text><text x="305" y="52">descriptor</text>
  </g>
  <g stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"><line x1="105" y1="40" x2="124" y2="40"/><line x1="230" y1="40" x2="249" y2="40"/></g>
  <polygon points="129,40 121,36 121,44" fill="#1f2a44"/><polygon points="254,40 246,36 246,44" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="76" font-size="11" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">the first one found answers</text>
</svg>
```

Data descriptors come first so a checker cannot be skipped by a value sitting in the dictionary. Non-data descriptors come after the dictionary, which is what lets a cached value hide the descriptor.
:::

::: context why-dict-direct Why not write obj.price = value?
Inside `__set__`, it is tempting to store the value with `setattr(obj, self.name, value)` or `obj.price = value`. But that *is* an assignment to the attribute, so Python calls `__set__` again, which assigns again, which calls `__set__` again, forever, until Python gives up with `RecursionError`.

Writing into `obj.__dict__[self.name]` is a plain dictionary assignment. It does not go through the descriptor, so it breaks the loop. Reading works the same way: `__get__` reads the dictionary directly, not `obj.price`.
:::

::: context bool-is-int True and False are secretly 1 and 0
In Python, `bool` is a subclass of `int`: `True` behaves like 1 and `False` like 0. So `isinstance(True, int)` is `True`, and `True + True` is `2`.

That means a check like "is it an `int` or `float` greater than 0?" would accept `True` as the number 1. A price of `True` is almost certainly a bug, so a careful check adds `or isinstance(value, bool)` to its list of things to reject.
:::

::: context cached-property Compute once, then remember
Some values take real work to compute: parsing a large file, loading a star catalog, fitting a curve to thousands of sensor readings. If the answer never changes for that object, there is no point computing it every time it is read.

`cached_property` computes on the first read and stores the result in the object's `__dict__`. Later reads are ordinary dictionary look-ups. Deleting it (`del r.summary`) removes the stored value, so the next read computes it again.

Only use it for values that really cannot change. If the data underneath changes, the cached answer quietly goes stale.
:::

::: context methods-are-descriptors Even methods are descriptors
Every function has a `__get__` method, so a function stored in a class is a non-data descriptor. When you write `acct.deposit`, Python finds the function on the class and calls its `__get__`, which returns a **bound method**: the function with `acct` already filled in as `self`. That is how `self` gets its value without you ever passing it.

Two decorators you will meet in other people's code are descriptors too: `@classmethod` hands the method the class instead of the object, and `@staticmethod` hands it neither.
:::
--- task
Write two descriptors, then a class that uses them.

- `Positive`: a data descriptor for numbers that must be greater than 0. Use `__set_name__` to remember the attribute's name. In `__set__`, assigning anything that is not an `int` or `float` greater than 0 raises `ValueError`; a good value is stored in the object's `__dict__` under the attribute's own name. `__get__` reads it back from there, and reading it from the class (when `obj` is `None`) returns the descriptor itself.
- `lazy(func)`: a non-data descriptor (no `__set__`) that is used like a decorator on a method, the way `@cached_property` is. The first read calls `func(obj)`, stores the result in the object's `__dict__` under the same name, and returns it. Later reads never call `func` again.

Then write `Product(name, price, quantity)`, where `price` and `quantity` are `Positive` class attributes, with a property `total` that returns price × quantity.
--- starter
class Positive:
    pass


class lazy:
    def __init__(self, func):
        self.func = func


class Product:
    def __init__(self, name, price, quantity):
        self.name = name
        self.price = price
        self.quantity = quantity
--- solution
class Positive:
    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name

    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        return obj.__dict__[self.name]

    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if not isinstance(value, (int, float)) or isinstance(value, bool) or value <= 0:
            raise ValueError(f"{self.name} must be a number greater than 0")
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value


class lazy:
    def __init__(self, func):
        self.func = func

    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name

    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        value = self.func(obj)
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value
        return value


class Product:
    price = Positive()
    quantity = Positive()

    def __init__(self, name, price, quantity):
        self.name = name
        self.price = price
        self.quantity = quantity

    @property
    def total(self):
        return self.price * self.quantity
--- hint
`Positive` follows the `NonEmpty` example almost line for line: `__set_name__` saves `self.name = name`, and `__get__` returns `self` when `obj is None`, otherwise `obj.__dict__[self.name]`. Only the test in `__set__` is different.
--- hint
In `Positive.__set__`, raise `ValueError` if `not isinstance(value, (int, float))` or `value <= 0` (and, to be careful, if it is a `bool`). Otherwise store it with `obj.__dict__[self.name] = value`, never on `self`. In `Product`, add `price = Positive()` and `quantity = Positive()` in the class body.
--- hint
`lazy` needs `__set_name__` and `__get__`, but no `__set__`. Its `__get__` returns `self` when `obj is None`; otherwise it computes `value = self.func(obj)`, stores it in `obj.__dict__[self.name]`, and returns it. The stored value then hides the descriptor on every later read.
--- check case | A valid product
(lambda p: (p.price, p.quantity, p.total))(Product("pen", 1.5, 4))
=> (1.5, 4, 6.0)
--- check test | Non-positive values are rejected, at creation and later
raises(ValueError, lambda: Product("x", 0, 1)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: Product("x", 1, -3)) and (lambda p: raises(ValueError, lambda: setattr(p, "price", -1)) and p.price == 2)(Product("x", 2, 1))
--- check test | Non-numbers are rejected
raises(ValueError, lambda: Product("x", "5", 1))
--- check test | Each product keeps its own values
(lambda a, b: (a.price, b.price) == (1, 2))(Product("a", 1, 1), Product("b", 2, 1))
?? The descriptor is shared by every instance: store the value in obj.__dict__, not on self.
--- check test | Values live in the instance dict under their own names
vars(Product("a", 2.5, 3)) == {"name": "a", "price": 2.5, "quantity": 3}
--- check test | Reading from the class gives the descriptor
isinstance(Product.price, Positive)
--- check case | lazy computes once, then reads the stored value
(lambda log: (lambda R: (lambda r: (r.data, r.data, len(log), "data" in vars(r)))(R()))(type("R", (), {"data": lazy(lambda self: log.append(1) or 42)})))([])
=> (42, 42, 1, True)
--- check test | lazy values are per instance
(lambda R: (R().v, R().v) == (1, 1))(type("R", (), {"n": 0, "v": lazy(lambda self: self.n + 1)}))

+++ practice | A range checker
--- task
Write a data descriptor `Bounded(low, high)` for numbers that must lie between `low` and `high`, **both included**.

- Use `__set_name__` to remember the attribute's name.
- In `__set__`, anything that is not an `int` or a `float` raises `ValueError`, and so does a `bool`, and so does a number below `low` or above `high`. A good value is stored in the object's `__dict__` under the attribute's own name.
- `__get__` reads the value back from there. Reading it from the class (when `obj` is `None`) returns the descriptor itself.

Then write `Thruster(name, throttle, gimbal)`, with the class attributes `throttle = Bounded(0, 100)` and `gimbal = Bounded(-5, 5)`.
--- starter
class Bounded:
    def __init__(self, low, high):
        self.low = low
        self.high = high


class Thruster:
    def __init__(self, name, throttle, gimbal):
        self.name = name
        self.throttle = throttle
        self.gimbal = gimbal
--- solution
class Bounded:
    def __init__(self, low, high):
        self.low = low
        self.high = high

    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name

    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        return obj.__dict__[self.name]

    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if not isinstance(value, (int, float)) or isinstance(value, bool):
            raise ValueError(f"{self.name} must be a number")
        if not self.low <= value <= self.high:
            raise ValueError(f"{self.name} must be between {self.low} and {self.high}")
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value


class Thruster:
    throttle = Bounded(0, 100)
    gimbal = Bounded(-5, 5)

    def __init__(self, name, throttle, gimbal):
        self.name = name
        self.throttle = throttle
        self.gimbal = gimbal
--- hint
`Bounded` has the same three methods as `NonEmpty` in the lesson. Only the test in `__set__` changes, and the descriptor keeps `low` and `high` from its own `__init__`.
--- hint
In `__set__`, first refuse anything that is not an `int` or `float`, or is a `bool`. Then refuse it unless `self.low <= value <= self.high`. Store good values with `obj.__dict__[self.name] = value`, and put `throttle = Bounded(0, 100)` and `gimbal = Bounded(-5, 5)` in the body of `Thruster`.
--- check case | Both ends of each range are allowed
(lambda t: (t.throttle, t.gimbal))(Thruster("main", 100, -5))
=> (100, -5)
--- check test | Values outside the range are refused when the object is made
raises(ValueError, lambda: Thruster("a", 101, 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: Thruster("a", 50, 5.5)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: Thruster("a", -1, 0))
--- check test | Later assignments are checked too, and a refused one changes nothing
(lambda t: raises(ValueError, lambda: setattr(t, "gimbal", -6)) and t.gimbal == 2)(Thruster("a", 10, 2))
--- check test | Strings and bools are not numbers here
raises(ValueError, lambda: Thruster("a", "50", 0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: Thruster("a", True, 0))
--- check test | Each thruster keeps its own values in its own dict
(lambda a, b: (a.throttle, b.throttle) == (10, 90) and vars(a) == {"name": "a", "throttle": 10, "gimbal": 0})(Thruster("a", 10, 0), Thruster("b", 90, 0))
--- check test | Reading from the class gives the descriptor
isinstance(Thruster.throttle, Bounded) and Thruster.gimbal.low == -5
--- check test | Decimals inside the range are fine
Thruster("a", 99.5, -4.25).gimbal == -4.25

+++ practice | Set once, then locked
--- task
Write a data descriptor `WriteOnce`.

- The first assignment to the attribute stores the value in the object's `__dict__`, under the attribute's name (use `__set_name__` to learn it).
- Any later assignment to the same attribute of the same object raises `AttributeError` with the message `"<name> is already set"`, for example `"norad_id is already set"`, and leaves the old value in place.
- Reading the attribute before it was ever set raises `AttributeError`, not `KeyError`.
- Reading it from the class returns the descriptor itself.

Then write `Satellite(norad_id, name)`, where `norad_id` is a `WriteOnce` class attribute and `name` is an ordinary attribute that can change.
--- starter
class WriteOnce:
    pass


class Satellite:
    def __init__(self, norad_id, name):
        self.norad_id = norad_id
        self.name = name
--- solution
class WriteOnce:
    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name

    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        if self.name not in obj.__dict__:
            raise AttributeError(f"{self.name} has not been set")
        return obj.__dict__[self.name]

    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if self.name in obj.__dict__:
            raise AttributeError(f"{self.name} is already set")
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value


class Satellite:
    norad_id = WriteOnce()

    def __init__(self, norad_id, name):
        self.norad_id = norad_id
        self.name = name
--- hint
The object's `__dict__` already tells you whether the attribute has been set: it is set exactly when the name is a key in it.
--- hint
In `__set__`, raise `AttributeError` if `self.name in obj.__dict__`; otherwise store the value. In `__get__`, raise `AttributeError` if the name is not in `obj.__dict__` yet, before you look it up.
--- check case | The first assignment works
(lambda s: (s.norad_id, s.name))(Satellite(25544, "ISS"))
=> (25544, "ISS")
--- check test | A second assignment is refused and the old value stays
(lambda s: raises(AttributeError, lambda: setattr(s, "norad_id", 1)) and s.norad_id == 25544)(Satellite(25544, "ISS"))
--- check case | The message names the attribute
(lambda s: (lambda g: (exec("try:\n    s.norad_id = 2\nexcept AttributeError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g["msg"])[1])({"s": s}))(Satellite(1, "x"))
=> "norad_id is already set"
--- check test | An ordinary attribute can still change
(lambda s: (setattr(s, "name", "Zarya"), s.name)[1] == "Zarya")(Satellite(1, "x"))
--- check test | Each satellite locks its own value
(lambda a, b: (a.norad_id, b.norad_id) == (1, 2))(Satellite(1, "a"), Satellite(2, "b"))
--- check test | Reading before any assignment raises AttributeError
(lambda T: raises(AttributeError, lambda: T().code))(type("T", (), {"code": WriteOnce()}))
--- check test | Stored in the instance dict, and the class gives the descriptor
vars(Satellite(7, "x")) == {"norad_id": 7, "name": "x"} and isinstance(Satellite.norad_id, WriteOnce)

+++ practice | Typed fields with a readable repr
--- task
Write a data descriptor `Typed(kind)`, where `kind` is a class such as `str` or `float`. Assigning a value that is not an instance of `kind` raises `TypeError` with the message `"<name> must be <kind's name>"`, for example `"value must be float"`. A class's name is `kind.__name__`. Good values are stored in the object's `__dict__` under the attribute's name, and reading from the class returns the descriptor.

Then write `Reading(sensor, value)` with the class attributes `sensor = Typed(str)` and `value = Typed(float)`. An `int` is not a `float`, so `Reading("temp", 21)` raises `TypeError`. Give `Reading` two special methods:

- `__repr__`, so `repr(Reading("temp", 21.5))` is `"Reading(sensor='temp', value=21.5)"`.
- `__eq__`, true when the other object is a `Reading` with the same sensor and value. For any other kind of object, return `NotImplemented`.
--- starter
class Typed:
    def __init__(self, kind):
        self.kind = kind


class Reading:
    def __init__(self, sensor, value):
        self.sensor = sensor
        self.value = value
--- solution
class Typed:
    def __init__(self, kind):
        self.kind = kind

    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name

    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        return obj.__dict__[self.name]

    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if not isinstance(value, self.kind):
            raise TypeError(f"{self.name} must be {self.kind.__name__}")
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value


class Reading:
    sensor = Typed(str)
    value = Typed(float)

    def __init__(self, sensor, value):
        self.sensor = sensor
        self.value = value

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Reading(sensor={self.sensor!r}, value={self.value!r})"

    def __eq__(self, other):
        if not isinstance(other, Reading):
            return NotImplemented
        return (self.sensor, self.value) == (other.sensor, other.value)
--- hint
`Typed` is the lesson's `NonEmpty` with a different test: `isinstance(value, self.kind)`. The name for the message is `self.kind.__name__`.
--- hint
`__repr__` can use `!r` inside an f-string to put quotes around the sensor: `f"Reading(sensor={self.sensor!r}, value={self.value!r})"`. In `__eq__`, return `NotImplemented` unless `other` is a `Reading`, then compare the pairs `(sensor, value)`.
--- check case | repr shows both fields
repr(Reading("temp", 21.5))
=> "Reading(sensor='temp', value=21.5)"
--- check test | Wrong types raise TypeError
raises(TypeError, lambda: Reading("temp", 21)) and raises(TypeError, lambda: Reading(7, 1.0))
--- check case | The message names the field and the type
(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    Reading('t', '9')\nexcept TypeError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g["msg"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> "value must be float"
--- check test | Equal readings compare equal, and a tuple is not a Reading
Reading("a", 1.0) == Reading("a", 1.0) and Reading("a", 1.0) != Reading("a", 2.0) and Reading("a", 1.0) != ("a", 1.0)
--- check test | Later assignments are checked too
(lambda r: raises(TypeError, lambda: setattr(r, "value", "hot")) and r.value == 3.0)(Reading("t", 3.0))
--- check test | Values live in the instance dict
vars(Reading("t", 3.0)) == {"sensor": "t", "value": 3.0}
--- check test | Typed works for any class
(lambda C: (setattr(C(), "n", 3), raises(TypeError, lambda: setattr(C(), "n", 3.0)))[1])(type("C", (), {"n": Typed(int)}))

+++ practice | Whole-number counts
--- task
The starter's `Count` descriptor is meant to hold a count of things: a whole number, 0 or more. It is used by `Stock(item, count)`. Fix `Count` so these edge cases are right:

- `0` is allowed. Any negative number raises `ValueError`.
- A `float` raises `ValueError`, even one like `2.0`.
- `True` and `False` raise `ValueError`, even though Python counts them as `int`s.
- A very large whole number, like `10 ** 20`, is allowed.

Good values are still stored in the object's `__dict__` under the attribute's name, and reading from the class still returns the descriptor.
--- starter
class Count:
    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name

    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        return obj.__dict__[self.name]

    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if value <= 0:
            raise ValueError(f"{self.name} must be positive")
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value


class Stock:
    count = Count()

    def __init__(self, item, count):
        self.item = item
        self.count = count
--- solution
class Count:
    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name

    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        return obj.__dict__[self.name]

    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if not isinstance(value, int) or isinstance(value, bool) or value < 0:
            raise ValueError(f"{self.name} must be a whole number, 0 or more")
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value


class Stock:
    count = Count()

    def __init__(self, item, count):
        self.item = item
        self.count = count
--- hint
Go through the list one case at a time and ask what `value <= 0` does with it. Which ones get through that should not, and which one is refused that should get through?
--- hint
Refuse the value if it is not an `int`, or if it is a `bool` (a `bool` is a kind of `int`), or if it is below 0. `value < 0` lets 0 through; `value <= 0` does not.
--- check test | Zero is allowed
Stock("bolt", 0).count == 0
--- check test | Negative numbers are refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: Stock("bolt", -1))
--- check test | Floats are refused, even 2.0
raises(ValueError, lambda: Stock("bolt", 2.0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: Stock("bolt", 0.5))
--- check test | True and False are refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: Stock("bolt", True)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: Stock("bolt", False))
--- check test | Very large counts are fine
Stock("rivet", 10 ** 20).count == 10 ** 20
--- check test | Later assignments are checked, and the class gives the descriptor
(lambda s: (setattr(s, "count", 7), raises(ValueError, lambda: setattr(s, "count", -7)), s.count)[1:] == (True, 7))(Stock("nut", 3)) and isinstance(Stock.count, Count)

+++ practice | Debug: batteries that share a charge
--- task
`Percent` is a descriptor for values from 0 to 100, and `Battery(name, charge)` uses it for `charge`. With one battery everything looks fine. But make a battery at 80, then another at 20, and the first one says 20 as well.

Find the bug and fix it, so that every battery keeps its own charge in its own `__dict__`, under the attribute's name, and values outside 0 to 100 are still refused with `ValueError`.
--- starter
class Percent:
    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name

    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        return self.value

    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if not 0 <= value <= 100:
            raise ValueError(f"{self.name} must be from 0 to 100")
        self.value = value


class Battery:
    charge = Percent()

    def __init__(self, name, charge):
        self.name = name
        self.charge = charge
--- solution
class Percent:
    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name

    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        return obj.__dict__[self.name]

    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if not 0 <= value <= 100:
            raise ValueError(f"{self.name} must be from 0 to 100")
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value


class Battery:
    charge = Percent()

    def __init__(self, name, charge):
        self.name = name
        self.charge = charge
--- hint
How many `Percent` objects are there? One, made when the class was made, and every battery uses it. So where does `self.value` put the charge?
--- hint
Store the value in the battery, not in the descriptor: `obj.__dict__[self.name] = value` in `__set__`, and read it back with `obj.__dict__[self.name]` in `__get__`.
--- check test | Two batteries keep their own charge
(lambda a, b: (a.charge, b.charge) == (80, 20))(Battery("a", 80), Battery("b", 20))
--- check test | The charge lives in the battery's own dict
vars(Battery("x", 55)) == {"name": "x", "charge": 55}
--- check test | Out-of-range values are still refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: Battery("x", 101)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: Battery("x", -1))
--- check test | Changing one battery leaves the other alone
(lambda a, b: (setattr(b, "charge", 5), a.charge)[1] == 90)(Battery("a", 90), Battery("b", 40))

+++ practice | One base class for every check
--- task
Build a small checking toolkit on one base descriptor.

- `Validated`, a data descriptor. `__set_name__` remembers the attribute's name. `__get__` reads the value from the object's `__dict__`, and returns the descriptor itself when read from the class. `__set__` first calls `self.validate(value)`, and only if that does not raise, stores the value in the object's `__dict__`. `Validated.validate` itself raises `NotImplementedError`.
- `Positive(Validated)`: its `validate` raises `ValueError` unless the value is an `int` or `float` greater than 0. A `bool` does not count.
- `OneOf(Validated)`, made as `OneOf(*choices)`: its `validate` raises `ValueError` unless the value is one of the choices.
- `Burn(engine, seconds, throttle)`, with the class attributes `engine = OneOf("main", "rcs")`, `seconds = Positive()` and `throttle = Positive()`.
- `validated_fields(cls)` returns the names of the class attributes of `cls` that are `Validated` descriptors, in the order they are written in the class body. `validated_fields(Burn)` gives `["engine", "seconds", "throttle"]`. `vars(cls)` gives a class's own attributes as a dict, in the order they were written.
--- starter
class Validated:
    def validate(self, value):
        raise NotImplementedError


class Burn:
    def __init__(self, engine, seconds, throttle):
        self.engine = engine
        self.seconds = seconds
        self.throttle = throttle


def validated_fields(cls):
    return []
--- solution
class Validated:
    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name

    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        return obj.__dict__[self.name]

    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        self.validate(value)
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value

    def validate(self, value):
        raise NotImplementedError


class Positive(Validated):
    def validate(self, value):
        if not isinstance(value, (int, float)) or isinstance(value, bool) or value <= 0:
            raise ValueError(f"{self.name} must be a number greater than 0")


class OneOf(Validated):
    def __init__(self, *choices):
        self.choices = choices

    def validate(self, value):
        if value not in self.choices:
            raise ValueError(f"{self.name} must be one of {self.choices}")


class Burn:
    engine = OneOf("main", "rcs")
    seconds = Positive()
    throttle = Positive()

    def __init__(self, engine, seconds, throttle):
        self.engine = engine
        self.seconds = seconds
        self.throttle = throttle


def validated_fields(cls):
    return [name for name, value in vars(cls).items() if isinstance(value, Validated)]
--- hint
All the storing lives in `Validated`, written once. The subclasses only replace `validate`, which either raises or does nothing. Because `validate` runs before the value is stored, a refused value never reaches the dict.
--- hint
`OneOf` needs its own `__init__(self, *choices)` that keeps `self.choices`. `validated_fields` is one comprehension over `vars(cls).items()` that keeps the names whose value `isinstance(value, Validated)`.
--- check case | A good burn
(lambda b: (b.engine, b.seconds, b.throttle))(Burn("main", 12.5, 1))
=> ("main", 12.5, 1)
--- check test | An engine that is not one of the choices is refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: Burn("aux", 1, 1))
--- check test | Zero, bools and strings are not positive numbers
raises(ValueError, lambda: Burn("rcs", 0, 1)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: Burn("rcs", 1, True)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: Burn("rcs", "5", 1))
--- check test | A refused assignment leaves the old value
(lambda b: raises(ValueError, lambda: setattr(b, "engine", "warp")) and b.engine == "rcs")(Burn("rcs", 2, 0.5))
--- check test | Each burn keeps its own values
(lambda a, b: (a.seconds, b.seconds) == (1, 2))(Burn("main", 1, 1), Burn("main", 2, 1))
--- check case | validated_fields lists the checked fields in order
validated_fields(Burn)
=> ["engine", "seconds", "throttle"]
--- check test | validated_fields skips everything else
validated_fields(type("X", (), {"a": 1, "z": Positive(), "b": OneOf(1, 2), "run": lambda self: 0})) == ["z", "b"]
--- check test | The base validate raises NotImplementedError
(lambda C: raises(NotImplementedError, lambda: setattr(C(), "f", 1)))(type("C", (), {"f": Validated()}))
--- check case | A new check only needs its own validate
(lambda C: (lambda c: (setattr(c, "n", 4), raises(ValueError, lambda: setattr(c, "n", 3)), vars(c))[1:])(C()))(type("C", (), {"n": type("Even", (Validated,), {"validate": lambda self, v: int("odd") if v % 2 else None})()}))
=> (True, {"n": 4})

=== py4-03 | Class hooks: __init_subclass__ and the plugin pattern
--- teach
Last lesson, Python called a descriptor's `__set_name__` at the moment its class was created. That was a **hook**: a method Python calls for you at a particular moment. This lesson uses another class-creation hook to solve a real design problem: letting new pieces of a program add themselves.

**The problem.** Some programs are built from **[[plugins|plugins-real]]**: separate pieces that each add one feature, such as one command, one file format or one payment provider. Each lives in its own class, and the core program needs a list of them all. If a person keeps that list by hand, sooner or later someone writes a new class, forgets to add it to the list, and the feature silently does not exist.

Think of a club. A secretary copying names onto a list will eventually miss someone. A sign-up sheet at the door, which every new member signs on the way in, never misses anyone. The fix for plugins is the sign-up sheet: each class **registers itself** the moment it is defined. The shared list is called a **registry**.

**Step 1: a `class` statement is code that runs.** Python builds a class when it reaches the `class` line, the way it runs any other line, usually **[[at import|import-time]]**:

```python
print("before")
class A:
    print("building A")
print("after")
```

```
before
building A
after
```

So there *is* a moment when each class comes into existence, and Python can run code of yours at that moment.

**Step 2: the hook.** If a base class defines `__init_subclass__`, Python calls it every time a **subclass** of it is created. Its first argument, `cls`, is **[[the brand-new class itself|cls-parameter]]**: the subclass (not an object made from it). `cls.__name__` is that class's name as a string.

```python
class Base:
    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        print("new subclass:", cls.__name__)

class Red(Base):
    pass

class Blue(Base):
    pass
```

```
new subclass: Red
new subclass: Blue
```

The hook runs for `Red` and `Blue`, not for `Base` itself. The `super()` line is explained in Step 4.

**Step 3: a registry.** Put a dictionary on the base class as a class attribute (last lesson: one, shared by everyone). The hook adds each new subclass to it:

```python
class Exporter:
    formats = {}

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        Exporter.formats[cls.extension] = cls

class CsvExporter(Exporter):
    extension = "csv"

class JsonExporter(Exporter):
    extension = "json"

print(Exporter.formats)
# {'csv': <class '__main__.CsvExporter'>, 'json': <class '__main__.JsonExporter'>}
```

Nobody wrote `formats["csv"] = CsvExporter`. Defining the class was enough. The registry stores the *classes* themselves, so the program can later look one up by its extension and make an object from it: `Exporter.formats["csv"]()`.

**Step 4: the three things to get right.**

- **Register on the base class by name** (`Exporter.formats`), so there is one registry that every subclass shares.
- **Call `super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)`**. `super()` means "the parent class", as in the exceptions lesson, and `**kwargs` passes along any keyword arguments you did not use yourself, as in the `*args, **kwargs` lesson. This lets your hook **[[cooperate|cooperative-super]]** with any other base class that has a hook of its own.
- **Check at definition time.** The hook runs when the `class` statement runs. So a subclass that forgot its `extension`, or reused one that is already taken, can raise an error immediately, when the file is loaded, long before a user hits the problem.

For the check, give the base class a default value, such as `extension = None`. A subclass that does not set its own inherits the parent's `None`, so the hook can test for it:

```python
class Exporter:
    formats = {}
    extension = None

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        if not cls.extension:
            raise TypeError(f"{cls.__name__} needs an extension")
        Exporter.formats[cls.extension] = cls

class Broken(Exporter):
    pass
# TypeError: Broken needs an extension
```

`if not cls.extension` catches both `None` and an empty string `""`. Checking for a duplicate is one more `if`: is `cls.extension` already a key `in Exporter.formats`?

**A method subclasses must fill in.** The base class can list a method every plugin must provide, and make it fail loudly if a plugin forgets:

```python
def run(self, args):
    raise NotImplementedError
```

`NotImplementedError` is the built-in exception for "a subclass was supposed to replace this method". Each plugin defines its own `run`, which replaces the base one.

**Settings in the class line.** A subclass can also pass keyword arguments to the hook, right in its `class` line: `class Csv(Exporter, extension="csv")` calls `__init_subclass__` with `extension="csv"` as a keyword argument, which the hook can accept as a parameter.

**Other ways to do this.** Before `__init_subclass__` existed, this took a **[[metaclass|metaclass-word]]**: a class whose objects are themselves classes. Metaclasses can do more, but they are harder to read and hard to combine; reach for the hook first. A class decorator (`@register` above each class) also works, but then every plugin author has to remember to write it, which is the very thing we were trying to avoid.

**Making a class with a call.** A class can also be created without a `class` statement, by calling `type(name, bases, namespace)`: the name as a string, a tuple of parent classes, and a dictionary of its attributes. The hook still runs. These two do the same thing:

```python
class Echo(Base):
    sound = "echo"

Echo = type("Echo", (Base,), {"sound": "echo"})     # also prints: new subclass: Echo
```

The checks for this lesson use `type(...)` to add plugins of their own and see whether your registry picks them up.

**Using the registry: a dispatcher.** A **dispatcher** reads a request and hands it to the right plugin. For typed commands, `line.split()` with no argument splits on any run of spaces and drops the empty bits, so `"add 1 2".split()` is `['add', '1', '2']` and `"   ".split()` is `[]`. The first word picks the plugin from the registry with `.get(...)`, and the rest of the words are its arguments.

**Watch out:** a plugin registers itself only when its `class` statement runs. If it sits in a file that the program never imports, its class is never built, and it never appears in the registry. When a plugin "does not exist", first check that its file is actually imported.

::: context plugins-real Plugins in real programs
Plugin systems are everywhere. The testing tool `pytest` finds extra features through plugins. Web frameworks register each page's handler. Code editors load language support as plugins.

Flight software uses the same idea. A spacecraft receives commands from the ground, each with a name or number, and a dispatcher looks the command up in a table and calls the code that handles it. Adding a new command means adding one handler, not editing the dispatcher. That is exactly the shape of this lesson's task.
:::

::: context import-time What "at import" means
When Python loads a file, whether you run it directly or another file does `import` on it, it runs every top-level line from top to bottom. `def` and `class` statements are lines too: running them creates the function or class.

So "import time" is the moment the file is loaded, usually right as the program starts. An error raised by a class hook at import time stops the program before it does any real work, which is exactly when you want to hear about a broken plugin: on the developer's screen, not in the middle of a user's session.
:::

::: context cls-parameter cls instead of self
Ordinary methods receive the *object* as their first argument, and by convention it is called `self`. `__init_subclass__` receives a *class* instead, so by convention its first argument is called `cls`. (`class` itself is a keyword and cannot be a name.)

Python makes `__init_subclass__` a **class method** automatically. Other methods can be made into class methods with the `@classmethod` decorator; you will see them used as extra ways to build an object, such as a method that builds a date from the text "2026-09-27".
:::

::: context cooperative-super Passing the call up the chain
A class can have more than one parent. If two parents both define `__init_subclass__`, each one must call `super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)`, or the chain stops and the other parent's hook never runs.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 110" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <g font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">
    <rect x="10" y="35" width="90" height="36" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="55" y="57">your hook</text>
    <rect x="135" y="35" width="90" height="36" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="180" y="57">other hook</text>
    <rect x="260" y="35" width="90" height="36" rx="6" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="305" y="57">object</text>
  </g>
  <g stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2">
    <line x1="100" y1="53" x2="128" y2="53"/><line x1="225" y1="53" x2="253" y2="53"/>
  </g>
  <polygon points="134,53 124,48 124,58" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <polygon points="259,53 249,48 249,58" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="180" y="95" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">each arrow is one super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)</text>
</svg>
```

Passing `**kwargs` along matters too: a keyword meant for another hook must reach it. The chain ends at `object`, the class every class inherits from.
:::

::: context metaclass-word A class of classes
Every object has a type: `type(3)` is `int`. Classes are objects too, so they have a type as well, and for ordinary classes it is `type`: `type(int)` is `type`, and so is `type(Red)`. A class whose objects are classes is called a **metaclass**, and `type` is the one Python uses by default.

That is why calling `type(name, bases, namespace)` makes a new class: you are calling the metaclass, the class that makes classes. Writing your own metaclass lets you change how classes are built, which is powerful and rarely needed.
:::
--- task
Build a tiny command system.

- `class Command` with a class attribute `registry = {}`, and a method `run(self, args)` that raises `NotImplementedError`. Subclasses override `run`: `args` is a list of strings, and `run` returns a string.
- Every subclass registers itself in `Command.registry` under its class attribute `name`, using `__init_subclass__`. A subclass with no `name` (or an empty one) raises `TypeError`. A name that is already registered raises `ValueError`.
- `Add` (name `"add"`) returns the sum of its arguments, turned into integers, as a string. `Upper` (name `"upper"`) returns its arguments joined by single spaces, uppercased.
- `dispatch(line)` splits the line into words, finds the command class by the first word, and returns `command().run(rest)`, where `rest` is the list of the other words. An empty line returns `"error: empty command"`; an unknown name returns `"error: unknown command <name>"`.
--- starter
class Command:
    registry = {}

    def run(self, args):
        raise NotImplementedError


class Add(Command):
    def run(self, args):
        return str(sum(int(a) for a in args))


def dispatch(line):
    return "error: empty command"
--- solution
class Command:
    registry = {}
    name = None

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        if not cls.name:
            raise TypeError(f"{cls.__name__} needs a name")
        if cls.name in Command.registry:
            raise ValueError(f"command {cls.name!r} is already registered")
        Command.registry[cls.name] = cls

    def run(self, args):
        raise NotImplementedError


class Add(Command):
    name = "add"

    def run(self, args):
        return str(sum(int(a) for a in args))


class Upper(Command):
    name = "upper"

    def run(self, args):
        return " ".join(args).upper()


def dispatch(line):
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        return "error: empty command"
    command = Command.registry.get(words[0])
    if command is None:
        return f"error: unknown command {words[0]}"
    return command().run(words[1:])
--- hint
Follow the `Exporter` example from Step 4. Give `Command` a class attribute `name = None`, so a subclass that forgets its own inherits `None` and the hook can test `if not cls.name`.
--- hint
In `__init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs)`: call `super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)`, raise `TypeError` if `not cls.name`, raise `ValueError` if `cls.name in Command.registry`, then store `Command.registry[cls.name] = cls`. Remember to give `Add` its `name = "add"` and to write `Upper`.
--- hint
`dispatch`: `words = line.split()`. If `words` is empty, return the empty-command error. Otherwise `command = Command.registry.get(words[0])`; if that is `None`, return the unknown-command error; else return `command().run(words[1:])`.
--- check case | add sums its arguments
dispatch("add 1 2 39")
=> "42"
--- check case | upper shouts
dispatch("upper hello there")
=> "HELLO THERE"
--- check test | Both commands registered themselves
Command.registry.get("add") is Add and Command.registry.get("upper") is Upper and Command not in Command.registry.values()
--- check test | A new plugin works with no other change
(type("Echo", (Command,), {"name": "echo", "run": lambda self, args: "|".join(args)}), dispatch("echo a b c"))[1] == "a|b|c"
--- check test | A subclass without a name is rejected when it is defined
raises(TypeError, lambda: type("Nameless", (Command,), {}))
--- check test | A duplicate name is rejected
raises(ValueError, lambda: type("Add2", (Command,), {"name": "add"}))
--- check test | Empty and unknown commands give errors, not crashes
dispatch("   ") == "error: empty command" and dispatch("fly away") == "error: unknown command fly"

+++ practice | Units that register themselves
--- task
Build a unit converter whose units add themselves.

- `class Unit` with three class attributes: `registry = {}`, `symbol = None` and `meters = None`.
- Every subclass registers itself in `Unit.registry` under its `symbol`, using `__init_subclass__`. A subclass whose `symbol` is missing or empty raises `TypeError` when it is defined.
- Three units: `Meter` (symbol `"m"`, meters `1`), `Kilometer` (symbol `"km"`, meters `1000`) and `Foot` (symbol `"ft"`, meters `0.3048`). `meters` says how many meters one of that unit is.
- `convert(value, from_symbol, to_symbol)` looks both units up in the registry and returns `value * from_unit.meters / to_unit.meters`. An unknown symbol raises `KeyError`.

For example, `convert(2, "km", "m")` gives `2000.0`.
--- starter
class Unit:
    registry = {}


class Meter(Unit):
    symbol = "m"
    meters = 1


def convert(value, from_symbol, to_symbol):
    return value
--- solution
class Unit:
    registry = {}
    symbol = None
    meters = None

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        if not cls.symbol:
            raise TypeError(f"{cls.__name__} needs a symbol")
        Unit.registry[cls.symbol] = cls


class Meter(Unit):
    symbol = "m"
    meters = 1


class Kilometer(Unit):
    symbol = "km"
    meters = 1000


class Foot(Unit):
    symbol = "ft"
    meters = 0.3048


def convert(value, from_symbol, to_symbol):
    source = Unit.registry[from_symbol]
    target = Unit.registry[to_symbol]
    return value * source.meters / target.meters
--- hint
This is the `Exporter` registry from the lesson with `symbol` in place of `extension`. Give `Unit` the default `symbol = None`, so a subclass that forgets its own inherits `None` and the hook can refuse it.
--- hint
In `convert`, look the two classes up with `Unit.registry[from_symbol]` and `Unit.registry[to_symbol]`. Square brackets raise `KeyError` for an unknown symbol all by themselves.
--- check case | Kilometers to meters
convert(2, "km", "m")
=> 2000.0
--- check test | Feet, meters and kilometers convert both ways
abs(convert(1000, "ft", "m") - 304.8) < 1e-9 and abs(convert(1, "km", "ft") - 3280.839895013123) < 1e-6
--- check test | The three units registered themselves
Unit.registry.get("m") is Meter and Unit.registry.get("km") is Kilometer and Unit.registry.get("ft") is Foot and Unit not in Unit.registry.values()
--- check test | A new unit works with no other change
(type("Mile", (Unit,), {"symbol": "mi", "meters": 1609.344}), abs(convert(1, "mi", "km") - 1.609344) < 1e-9)[1]
--- check test | A unit without a symbol is refused when it is defined
raises(TypeError, lambda: type("Nothing", (Unit,), {"meters": 5})) and raises(TypeError, lambda: type("Blank", (Unit,), {"symbol": "", "meters": 5}))
--- check test | An unknown symbol raises KeyError
raises(KeyError, lambda: convert(1, "m", "parsec"))

+++ practice | Handlers named in the class line
--- task
One event can have several handlers. Write:

- `class Handler` with a class attribute `handlers = {}`, which maps each event name to a **list** of handler classes in the order they were defined, and a method `handle(self, payload)` that raises `NotImplementedError`.
- The event is given in the class line, like this: `class LogLaunch(Handler, event="launch"):`. Take it as a parameter, `__init_subclass__(cls, event, **kwargs)`, and add the class to the list for that event. A subclass with no `event` in its class line raises `TypeError` (Python does that for you when a required parameter is missing).
- Three handlers: `LogLaunch` (event `"launch"`) returns `f"log: {payload}"`; `CountLaunch` (event `"launch"`) returns `f"{len(payload)} characters"`; `AlertAbort` (event `"abort"`) returns `f"ALERT: {payload}"`.
- `fire(event, payload)` makes one object of each handler class for `event`, in order, calls `handle(payload)` on each, and returns the list of results. An event with no handlers gives `[]`.

For example, `fire("launch", "T-0")` gives `["log: T-0", "3 characters"]`.
--- starter
class Handler:
    handlers = {}

    def handle(self, payload):
        raise NotImplementedError


def fire(event, payload):
