    return []
--- solution
class Handler:
    handlers = {}

    def __init_subclass__(cls, event, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        Handler.handlers.setdefault(event, []).append(cls)

    def handle(self, payload):
        raise NotImplementedError


class LogLaunch(Handler, event="launch"):
    def handle(self, payload):
        return f"log: {payload}"


class CountLaunch(Handler, event="launch"):
    def handle(self, payload):
        return f"{len(payload)} characters"


class AlertAbort(Handler, event="abort"):
    def handle(self, payload):
        return f"ALERT: {payload}"


def fire(event, payload):
    return [handler().handle(payload) for handler in Handler.handlers.get(event, [])]
--- hint
The value in the dict is a list that grows. `setdefault(event, [])` from the dicts lesson gives you the list for that event, making an empty one the first time, and you can `.append(cls)` to it straight away.
--- hint
`fire` is one comprehension over `Handler.handlers.get(event, [])`: for each class, make an object with `handler()` and call its `handle(payload)`.
--- check case | Both launch handlers run, in order
fire("launch", "T-0")
=> ["log: T-0", "3 characters"]
--- check case | The abort handler
fire("abort", "engine fire")
=> ["ALERT: engine fire"]
--- check case | An event with no handlers
fire("landing", "x")
=> []
--- check test | The registry holds lists of classes
Handler.handlers["launch"] == [LogLaunch, CountLaunch] and Handler.handlers["abort"] == [AlertAbort]
--- check test | A new handler, given its event in the class line, joins in
(type("Echo", (Handler,), {"handle": lambda self, p: p * 2}, event="abort"), fire("abort", "ab"))[1] == ["ALERT: ab", "abab"]
--- check test | A handler with no event is refused
raises(TypeError, lambda: type("NoEvent", (Handler,), {}))

+++ practice | Preflight checks in order
--- task
Build a preflight checklist out of plugins.

- `class Check` with a class attribute `checks = []`, a class attribute `order = None`, and a method `run(self, state)` that raises `NotImplementedError`. Each subclass's `run` gets `state`, a dict, and returns `True` if the check passes and `False` if not.
- In `__init_subclass__`: a subclass must define its **own** `run` in its class body, or it raises `TypeError` (`"run" in vars(cls)` tells you; a `run` inherited from a parent does not count). Its `order` must be an `int`, or it raises `TypeError`. Then it is appended to `Check.checks`.
- Three checks: `ArmedCheck` (order 1) passes when `state["armed"]` is true; `FuelCheck` (order 2) passes when `state["fuel"]` is 90 or more; `WeatherCheck` (order 3) passes when `state["wind"]` is below 30.
- `preflight(state)` runs every registered check, sorted by `order`, and returns the list of the class names (`__name__`) of the checks that failed, in that order. Checks with the same `order` keep the order they were defined in. An empty list means "go".

For example, `preflight({"armed": True, "fuel": 90, "wind": 30})` gives `["WeatherCheck"]`.
--- starter
class Check:
    checks = []

    def run(self, state):
        raise NotImplementedError


def preflight(state):
    return []
--- solution
class Check:
    checks = []
    order = None

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        if "run" not in vars(cls):
            raise TypeError(f"{cls.__name__} must define run")
        if not isinstance(cls.order, int):
            raise TypeError(f"{cls.__name__} needs a whole-number order")
        Check.checks.append(cls)

    def run(self, state):
        raise NotImplementedError


class ArmedCheck(Check):
    order = 1

    def run(self, state):
        return bool(state["armed"])


class FuelCheck(Check):
    order = 2

    def run(self, state):
        return state["fuel"] >= 90


class WeatherCheck(Check):
    order = 3

    def run(self, state):
        return state["wind"] < 30


def preflight(state):
    ordered = sorted(Check.checks, key=lambda check: check.order)
    return [check.__name__ for check in ordered if not check().run(state)]
--- hint
The hook runs once per subclass, at the moment it is defined, so both checks go there, before the `append`. `vars(cls)` holds only what that class's own body wrote, which is why it can tell an inherited `run` from a new one.
--- hint
`sorted(Check.checks, key=lambda check: check.order)` puts them in order, and Python's sort is stable, so equal orders stay in the order they were defined. Then keep the `__name__` of each class whose `check().run(state)` is false.
--- check case | Everything passes
preflight({"armed": True, "fuel": 95, "wind": 10})
=> []
--- check case | Everything fails, reported in order
preflight({"armed": False, "fuel": 50, "wind": 45})
=> ["ArmedCheck", "FuelCheck", "WeatherCheck"]
--- check case | Exactly at the limits
preflight({"armed": True, "fuel": 90, "wind": 30})
=> ["WeatherCheck"]
--- check test | A check with no run of its own is refused, even if it inherits one
raises(TypeError, lambda: type("Lazy", (Check,), {"order": 5})) and raises(TypeError, lambda: type("Fuel2", (FuelCheck,), {"order": 9}))
--- check test | A check without a whole-number order is refused
raises(TypeError, lambda: type("Loose", (Check,), {"run": lambda self, s: True})) and raises(TypeError, lambda: type("Early", (Check,), {"order": "first", "run": lambda self, s: True}))
--- check test | A new check slots in by order, after an equal one defined earlier
(type("Doors", (Check,), {"order": 1, "run": lambda self, s: s.get("doors") == "shut"}), preflight({"armed": False, "fuel": 95, "wind": 5}))[1] == ["ArmedCheck", "Doors"]

+++ practice | Names that must be your own
--- task
The starter registers sensors by name, the obvious way. Rewrite the hook in `Sensor` so it gets these edge cases right:

- A subclass must set `name` in its **own** class body. A subclass of a registered sensor that does not would inherit its parent's name; that raises `TypeError`. `vars(cls).get("name")` gives the name from the class's own body, or `None`.
- The name must be a string with something other than spaces in it. `""`, `"   "` or a number raise `TypeError`.
- Names are stored stripped and in lowercase, so `"Temp"` is stored as `"temp"`. A name that is already taken after that clean-up, such as `"TEMP"` when `"temp"` exists, raises `ValueError`.

Keep `Thermometer` and `Barometer` as they are. Then fix `lookup(name)`: it cleans the name the same way and returns the sensor class, or `None` if there is none. So `lookup(" TEMP ")` gives `Thermometer`.
--- starter
class Sensor:
    registry = {}
    name = None

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        if not cls.name:
            raise TypeError(f"{cls.__name__} needs a name")
        Sensor.registry[cls.name] = cls


class Thermometer(Sensor):
    name = "Temp"


class Barometer(Sensor):
    name = "pressure"


def lookup(name):
    return Sensor.registry.get(name)
--- solution
class Sensor:
    registry = {}

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        name = vars(cls).get("name")
        if not isinstance(name, str) or not name.strip():
            raise TypeError(f"{cls.__name__} needs a name of its own")
        key = name.strip().lower()
        if key in Sensor.registry:
            raise ValueError(f"sensor name {key!r} is already taken")
        Sensor.registry[key] = cls


class Thermometer(Sensor):
    name = "Temp"


class Barometer(Sensor):
    name = "pressure"


def lookup(name):
    return Sensor.registry.get(name.strip().lower())
--- hint
`cls.name` finds a name anywhere up the family tree, so a child of `Thermometer` sees `"Temp"` and sails through. Read the name from `vars(cls)` instead, which only holds what the class's own body wrote.
--- hint
Refuse the name unless `isinstance(name, str)` and `name.strip()` is not empty. Then work out `key = name.strip().lower()`, raise `ValueError` if `key` is already in the registry, and store the class under `key`. `lookup` cleans its argument the same way before `.get`.
--- check test | Lookups ignore case and spaces
lookup("temp") is Thermometer and lookup(" TEMP ") is Thermometer and lookup("Pressure") is Barometer
--- check case | An unknown sensor gives None
lookup("humidity")
=> None
--- check test | Inheriting a name is refused
raises(TypeError, lambda: type("FancyThermometer", (Thermometer,), {}))
--- check test | A child with a name of its own is fine
(type("Pyrometer", (Thermometer,), {"name": "hot"}), lookup("HOT").__name__)[1] == "Pyrometer"
--- check test | Blank names and non-strings are refused
raises(TypeError, lambda: type("B1", (Sensor,), {"name": ""})) and raises(TypeError, lambda: type("B2", (Sensor,), {"name": "   "})) and raises(TypeError, lambda: type("B3", (Sensor,), {"name": 7}))
--- check test | Names that clash after the clean-up are refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: type("Temp2", (Sensor,), {"name": "TEMP"})) and raises(ValueError, lambda: type("P2", (Sensor,), {"name": " pressure"}))
--- check test | Names are stored clean
"temp" in Sensor.registry and "Temp" not in Sensor.registry

+++ practice | Debug: the hook that never ran
--- task
Two base classes each have an `__init_subclass__` hook. `Registered` puts every subclass that sets a `name` into `Registered.registry`. `Counted` counts every subclass made, in `Counted.made`. `Tool` inherits from both, `class Tool(Counted, Registered)`, and every tool is a subclass of `Tool`.

The counting works, but no tool ever shows up in the registry: after `Wrench` and `Drill` are defined, `Registered.registry` is still empty. Find the bug and fix it, so that both hooks run for every new class.
--- starter
class Registered:
    registry = {}

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        if "name" in vars(cls):
            Registered.registry[cls.name] = cls


class Counted:
    made = 0

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        Counted.made += 1


class Tool(Counted, Registered):
    pass


class Wrench(Tool):
    name = "wrench"


class Drill(Tool):
    name = "drill"
--- solution
class Registered:
    registry = {}

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        if "name" in vars(cls):
            Registered.registry[cls.name] = cls


class Counted:
    made = 0

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        Counted.made += 1


class Tool(Counted, Registered):
    pass


class Wrench(Tool):
    name = "wrench"


class Drill(Tool):
    name = "drill"
--- hint
When `Wrench` is defined, Python looks up `__init_subclass__` along the family tree and finds `Counted`'s first. Does `Counted`'s hook pass the call on to the next one in line?
--- hint
Every hook must call `super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)`, or the chain stops there. Add that line to `Counted`.
--- check test | The tools are in the registry
Registered.registry.get("wrench") is Wrench and Registered.registry.get("drill") is Drill
--- check test | A new tool is counted once and registered
(lambda before: (type("Saw", (Tool,), {"name": "saw"}), Counted.made - before, Registered.registry.get("saw").__name__)[1:])(Counted.made) == (1, "Saw")
--- check test | Tool itself has no name, so it is not registered
Tool not in Registered.registry.values()
--- check test | A class without a name is counted but not registered
(lambda before, n: (type("Clamp", (Tool,), {}), Counted.made - before, len(Registered.registry) - n)[1:])(Counted.made, len(Registered.registry)) == (1, 0)

+++ practice | Telemetry packet decoders
--- task
A ground station receives telemetry packets as lines of text, like `"T 21.5 22.0"`: a one-letter code, then numbers. Make every kind of packet a plugin.

- `class Packet` with a class attribute `decoders = {}`. Each subclass sets `code` and `fields`. In `__init_subclass__`, check them when the class is defined:
  - `code` must be a string of exactly one uppercase letter (`len(code) == 1 and code.isupper()`), or it raises `TypeError`;
  - `fields` must be a non-empty tuple of names with no name twice, or it raises `TypeError`;
  - a code that is already registered raises `ValueError`.
  Then the class is registered under its code.
- Two packets: `Temperature` (code `"T"`, fields `("inside", "outside")`) and `Position` (code `"P"`, fields `("x", "y", "z")`).
- `decode(line)` splits the line into words, and the first word picks the packet class. It returns a dict with `"type"` set to the class's name, then each field, in order, set to its value as a `float`. An empty line, an unknown code, or the wrong number of values raises `ValueError`.

For example, `decode("T 21.5 -3")` gives `{"type": "Temperature", "inside": 21.5, "outside": -3.0}`.
--- starter
class Packet:
    decoders = {}


def decode(line):
    return {}
--- solution
class Packet:
    decoders = {}
    code = None
    fields = ()

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        code = cls.code
        if not isinstance(code, str) or len(code) != 1 or not code.isupper():
            raise TypeError(f"{cls.__name__} needs a one-letter uppercase code")
        fields = cls.fields
        if not isinstance(fields, tuple) or not fields or len(set(fields)) != len(fields):
            raise TypeError(f"{cls.__name__} needs a tuple of different field names")
        if code in Packet.decoders:
            raise ValueError(f"packet code {code!r} is already registered")
        Packet.decoders[code] = cls


class Temperature(Packet):
    code = "T"
    fields = ("inside", "outside")


class Position(Packet):
    code = "P"
    fields = ("x", "y", "z")


def decode(line):
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        raise ValueError("empty packet")
    packet = Packet.decoders.get(words[0])
    if packet is None:
        raise ValueError(f"unknown packet code {words[0]!r}")
    values = words[1:]
    if len(values) != len(packet.fields):
        raise ValueError(f"{packet.__name__} needs {len(packet.fields)} values")
    result = {"type": packet.__name__}
    for field, value in zip(packet.fields, values):
        result[field] = float(value)
    return result
--- hint
Give `Packet` defaults, `code = None` and `fields = ()`, so a subclass that forgets one fails your checks instead of crashing on a missing attribute. A set of the field names is shorter than the tuple exactly when a name appears twice.
--- hint
`decode` follows the lesson's `dispatch`: split, refuse an empty list, look the first word up with `.get`, and refuse `None`. Then compare the number of values with `len(packet.fields)`, and fill the dict by walking `zip(packet.fields, values)`.
--- check case | A temperature packet
decode("T 21.5 -3")
=> {"type": "Temperature", "inside": 21.5, "outside": -3.0}
--- check case | A position packet, with extra spaces
decode("  P 1 2.5 -4  ")
=> {"type": "Position", "x": 1.0, "y": 2.5, "z": -4.0}
--- check test | The fields come in order, after the type
list(decode("P 1 2 3")) == ["type", "x", "y", "z"]
--- check test | Empty lines, unknown codes and wrong counts raise ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda l=l: decode(l)) for l in ["", "   ", "Q 1", "T 1", "T 1 2 3"])
--- check test | Bad codes are refused when the class is defined
all(raises(TypeError, lambda c=c: type("Bad", (Packet,), {"code": c, "fields": ("a",)})) for c in ["t", "TT", "", "1", None])
--- check test | Bad field lists are refused when the class is defined
all(raises(TypeError, lambda f=f: type("Bad", (Packet,), {"code": "B", "fields": f})) for f in [(), ["a"], ("a", "a"), None])
--- check test | A code that is taken is refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: type("T2", (Packet,), {"code": "T", "fields": ("a",)}))
--- check test | A new packet type works with no other change
(type("Battery", (Packet,), {"code": "B", "fields": ("volts",)}), decode("B 28.1"))[1] == {"type": "Battery", "volts": 28.1}

=== py4-04 | Advanced generators: send and yield from
--- teach
The last two lessons were about how classes are built. Now back to generators, from the advanced course. There, `yield` handed a value *out* and paused the function, and `next()` woke it up again. So far that conversation only goes one way: the generator talks, and the caller listens. This lesson adds two things. A generator can also **receive** values, and it can **delegate**: hand its work over to another generator.

**Step 1: `yield` gives back a value.** `yield` is an **[[expression|expression-word]]**, so it can sit on the right of an `=`. The line

```python fragment
x = yield value
```

does two things, with a pause in between. First it hands `value` out and pauses. Later, when the caller wakes it up with `g.send(something)`, the whole `yield value` part turns into `something`, and that is stored in `x`.

```python
def echo_upper():
    reply = None
    while True:
        text = yield reply
        reply = text.upper()

g = echo_upper()
print(next(g))            # None
print(g.send("hi"))       # HI
print(g.send("there"))    # THERE
```

Follow it step by step:

1. `next(g)` runs the body up to the first `yield`, hands out `reply` (still `None`), and pauses there.
2. `g.send("hi")` wakes it. The paused `yield reply` becomes `"hi"`, so `text = "hi"`. Then `reply = "HI"`, the loop goes round, and the next `yield reply` hands out `"HI"`. That is what `send` returns.
3. `g.send("there")` does the same again.

`send` both *puts in* one value and *takes out* the next one.

**Step 2: prime it first.** A brand-new generator has not run any of its body yet, so it is not paused at a `yield`, and there is nowhere for a sent value to go. You must call `next(g)` once (or `g.send(None)`, which is the same) to run it up to the first `yield`. This is called **[[priming|priming]]** the generator. Forget, and Python stops you:

```python
g = echo_upper()
g.send("hi")
# TypeError: can't send non-None value to a just-started generator
```

**Step 3: it remembers between messages.** A generator used with `send` keeps all its local variables between messages. That makes it a small **[[state machine|state-machine]]**, with no class needed. This one remembers the longest word it has been sent:

```python
def longest():
    best = ""
    while True:
        word = yield best
        if len(word) > len(best):
            best = word

g = longest()
next(g)                   # prime it: hands out ''
print(g.send("moon"))     # moon
print(g.send("sun"))      # moon
print(g.send("jupiter"))  # jupiter
```

A plain `x = yield`, with nothing after `yield`, hands out `None` and waits for a value. Use it when the generator only listens.

**Step 4: `yield from` hands over.** Inside a generator, `yield from other` **[[delegates|delegation]]** to another generator: it passes out every value `other` yields, one by one, until `other` finishes. Then the outer generator carries on.

```python
def inner():
    yield 1
    yield 2

def outer():
    yield 0
    yield from inner()
    yield 3

print(list(outer()))      # [0, 1, 2, 3]
```

For plain values, `yield from inner()` does the same as `for v in inner(): yield v`. But it also passes every `send` straight through to `inner`, which a `for` loop cannot do.

**Step 5: recursion with `yield from`.** `yield from` keeps a recursive generator short. This one walks a list that has lists inside it, to any depth:

```python
def walk(tree):
    for node in tree:
        if isinstance(node, list):
            yield from walk(node)    # hand over to the recursive call
        else:
            yield node

print(list(walk([1, [2, [3]], 4])))   # [1, 2, 3, 4]
```

To treat both lists and tuples as containers, pass a tuple of types: `isinstance(node, (list, tuple))` is true for either one.

**Step 6: a generator can `return` a result.** When a generator reaches `return total`, it ends. If someone was driving it with `send`, that `send` raises `StopIteration`, and the returned value **[[rides along on it|stopiteration-value]]** as its `.value`:

```python
def add_up():
    total = 0
    while True:
        x = yield
        if x is None:
            return total      # ends this generator with a result
        total += x

g = add_up()
next(g)
g.send(3)
g.send(4)
try:
    g.send(None)
except StopIteration as stop:
    print(stop.value)         # 7
```

Catching that by hand is clumsy. `yield from` does it for you: when the inner generator returns, the value becomes the result of the `yield from` expression itself.

```python
def outer(results):
    while True:
        results.append((yield from add_up()))

results = []
g = outer(results)
next(g)
g.send(3)
g.send(4)
g.send(None)     # add_up returns 7; outer appends it and starts a fresh add_up
g.send(10)
g.send(None)     # the second add_up returns 10
print(results)   # [7, 10]
```

Every `send` goes through `outer` straight into the current `add_up`. When `add_up` returns, `yield from add_up()` becomes 7, `outer` appends it, loops, and starts a new `add_up`, which runs to its first `yield` and waits. Note the extra brackets in `results.append((yield from add_up()))`: Python requires brackets around a `yield` expression used inside a call like this.

This send-and-delegate machinery is the ancestor of `async` and `await`, which are **[[the next lesson|async-bridge]]**.

**Watch out:** when flattening, do not treat strings as containers. A string can be looped over, and each of its characters is itself a string, so a flatten that recurses into anything loopable **[[never stops|string-forever]]** and crashes with `RecursionError`. Test for `list` and `tuple` by name, so a string like `"ab"` is yielded whole.

::: context expression-word Expressions and statements
An **expression** is a piece of code that produces a value: `2 + 3`, `len(word)`, `x > 0`. A **statement** is an instruction that does something but has no value of its own, like `if`, `for` or `return`.

In early Python, `yield` was only a statement: it could hand a value out, and that was all. Since Python 2.5 it has been an expression, so it can also produce a value: whatever the caller sends in. That small change is what makes everything in this lesson possible.
:::

::: context priming Why it is called priming
Old hand pumps would not work dry. You first poured a little water in, called *priming* the pump, and only then did pumping bring water up. A new generator is the same: one `next()` gets it running to its first `yield`, and only then can it take a value.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 120" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="20" y1="60" x2="340" y2="60" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <g font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">
    <circle cx="40" cy="60" r="6" fill="#6c7a93"/><text x="40" y="85">created</text><text x="40" y="100">not started</text>
    <circle cx="150" cy="60" r="6" fill="#1d6fd1"/><text x="150" y="85">paused at</text><text x="150" y="100">first yield</text>
    <circle cx="290" cy="60" r="6" fill="#1d6fd1"/><text x="290" y="85">paused at</text><text x="290" y="100">next yield</text>
  </g>
  <text x="95" y="45" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">next(g)</text>
  <text x="220" y="45" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">g.send(x)</text>
</svg>
```

Sending before priming is like pumping a dry pump: nothing happens, except that Python raises a `TypeError`.
:::

::: context state-machine State machines on real vehicles
A **state machine** is something that is always in one of a few states and moves between them when messages arrive. A traffic light is one: green, then amber, then red.

Flight software is full of them. A rocket's flight computer might move from "on the pad" to "ascent" to "coast" to "landing", each move triggered by a sensor reading or a command. A generator that receives readings with `send` and keeps its current state in a local variable is a compact way to write a small one, and it is easy to test by sending in a list of made-up readings.
:::

::: context delegation Delegating is like transferring a phone call
When a help-desk operator transfers your call to a specialist, you talk straight to the specialist until they are done; then the call comes back. `yield from` works like that. Values the inner generator yields go straight out to the caller, values the caller sends go straight in to the inner generator, and when the inner one finishes, the outer one picks the call back up, with the inner one's return value in hand.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 120" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <g font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">
    <rect x="10" y="40" width="80" height="40" rx="6" fill="#fff" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="50" y="64">caller</text>
    <rect x="140" y="40" width="80" height="40" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="180" y="64">outer</text>
    <rect x="270" y="40" width="80" height="40" rx="6" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/><text x="310" y="64">add_up</text>
  </g>
  <line x1="90" y1="30" x2="262" y2="30" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="270,30 260,25 260,35" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="180" y="20" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">send(3), send(4) go straight in</text>
  <line x1="270" y1="95" x2="228" y2="95" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="220,95 230,90 230,100" fill="#b4232c"/>
  <text x="250" y="113" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#b4232c">return 7</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context stopiteration-value The value rides on StopIteration
You met `StopIteration` in the iterators lesson: it is how an iterator says "no more values". A `for` loop catches it quietly and ends. When a generator ends with `return something`, Python raises `StopIteration` with that value attached as its `.value`. A `for` loop throws the value away; `yield from` catches the `StopIteration` and uses `.value` as its result. So you almost never handle it yourself.
:::

::: context async-bridge Where this is going next
Before Python had `async` and `await`, programs that did many things at once (waiting on many network connections, for example) were written with generators: each task was a generator that paused with `yield` while it waited, and `yield from` let one task wait on another. That style became so common that Python 3.5 gave it its own keywords. `await` works much like `yield from`, in a clearer form, and you will use it in the next lesson.
:::

::: context string-forever Why strings recurse forever
Loop over `"ab"` and you get `"a"` and `"b"`. Loop over `"a"` and you get `"a"` again: a one-letter string contains exactly itself. So a flatten that says "if it can be looped over, go inside" goes into `"a"`, finds `"a"`, goes inside again, and never reaches the bottom. Python stops it after about a thousand nested calls with `RecursionError`. Lists always get smaller as you go inside them; strings do not.
:::
--- task
Write three generators, and a fourth that uses the third.

- `averager()`: prime it with `next()`. After that, each `send(x)` returns the average of all the numbers sent so far.
- `flatten(items)`: yields every value that is not a list or tuple from lists and tuples nested to any depth, in order, using `yield from`. Strings are values, not containers.
- `tally()`: receives numbers by `send` until it receives `None`, then **returns** the tuple `(count, total)`: how many numbers it received and their sum.
- `collect(results)`: loops forever, and each time a `yield from tally()` finishes, appends its result to the list `results`.
--- starter
def averager():
    yield 0


def flatten(items):
    for item in items:
        yield item


def tally():
    return (0, 0)
    yield


def collect(results):
    yield
--- solution
def averager():
    total = 0
    count = 0
    average = None
    while True:
        x = yield average
        total += x
        count += 1
        average = total / count


def flatten(items):
    for item in items:
        if isinstance(item, (list, tuple)):
            yield from flatten(item)
        else:
            yield item


def tally():
    count = 0
    total = 0
    while True:
        x = yield
        if x is None:
            return (count, total)
        count += 1
        total += x


def collect(results):
    while True:
        results.append((yield from tally()))
--- hint
`averager` has the same shape as `longest` in Step 3: keep a running `total`, a `count` and the latest `average` in local variables. The line `x = yield average` both hands out the latest average and receives the next number.
--- hint
`flatten` follows `walk` from Step 5: if `isinstance(item, (list, tuple))`, recurse with `yield from flatten(item)`; otherwise `yield item`.
--- hint
`tally` has the shape of `add_up` from Step 6, but keeps a count as well and ends with `return (count, total)` when it receives `None`. `collect` is `outer` with `tally` in place of `add_up`: `results.append((yield from tally()))` inside `while True:` (note the extra brackets).
--- check case | averager keeps a running average
(lambda g: (next(g), g.send(10), g.send(20), g.send(0))[1:])(averager())
=> (10.0, 15.0, 10.0)
--- check case | flatten handles any nesting, and keeps strings whole
list(flatten([1, [2, (3, [4, []])], "ab", [[["deep"]]]]))
=> [1, 2, 3, 4, "ab", "deep"]
--- check case | flatten of nothing
list(flatten([]))
=> []
--- check test | tally returns (count, total) when sent None
(lambda g: (next(g), g.send(4), g.send(6), raises(StopIteration, lambda: g.send(None))))(tally())[3]
--- check case | tally's StopIteration carries (count, total)
(lambda g: (next(g), g.send(4), g.send(6), stop_value(lambda: g.send(None))))(tally())[3]
=> (2, 10)
--- check case | tally with nothing sent returns (0, 0)
(lambda g: (next(g), stop_value(lambda: g.send(None))))(tally())[1]
=> (0, 0)
--- check case | collect gathers one result per finished tally
(lambda res: (lambda g: (next(g), g.send(1), g.send(2), g.send(None), g.send(5), g.send(None), res)[-1])(collect(res)))([])
=> [(2, 3), (1, 5)]
--- check source | Uses yield from
\byield\s+from\b

+++ practice | Lowest and highest so far
--- task
Write a generator `min_max()`. Prime it with `next()`, which hands out `None`. After that, each `send(x)` returns a tuple `(lowest, highest)` of all the numbers sent so far.

For example, with `g = min_max()`: `next(g)` gives `None`, `g.send(5)` gives `(5, 5)`, `g.send(2)` gives `(2, 5)` and `g.send(9)` gives `(2, 9)`. Two `min_max()` generators never share their numbers.
--- starter
def min_max():
    while True:
        x = yield
--- solution
def min_max():
    x = yield
    lowest = x
    highest = x
    while True:
        x = yield (lowest, highest)
        lowest = min(lowest, x)
        highest = max(highest, x)
--- hint
A generator keeps its local variables between sends, so it can remember the lowest and highest numbers itself. The very first number is both.
--- hint
Let a plain `x = yield` receive the first number (priming hands out `None` there). Set `lowest` and `highest` to it, then loop: `x = yield (lowest, highest)`, and update both with `min` and `max`.
--- check case | Priming, then three numbers
(lambda g: (next(g), g.send(5), g.send(2), g.send(9)))(min_max())
=> (None, (5, 5), (2, 5), (2, 9))
--- check case | Negative numbers and repeats
(lambda g: (next(g), g.send(-1), g.send(-1), g.send(-7))[1:])(min_max())
=> ((-1, -1), (-1, -1), (-7, -1))
--- check test | Two generators keep their own numbers
(lambda a, b: (next(a), next(b), a.send(1), b.send(100), a.send(3))[4] == (1, 3))(min_max(), min_max())
--- check test | min_max is a generator function
__import__("inspect").isgeneratorfunction(min_max)

+++ practice | Walk a nested config
--- task
Settings are stored as dicts inside dicts, like this:

```
{"engine": {"thrust": 250, "fuel": {"type": "RP-1"}}, "crew": 3}
```

Write a generator `walk(config, prefix="")`. It yields a pair `(path, value)` for every value that is not itself a dict. The path is the keys on the way down joined with dots, so the dict above gives `("engine.thrust", 250)`, then `("engine.fuel.type", "RP-1")`, then `("crew", 3)`. The keys are all strings.

- Go through the keys in the dict's own order, and finish each inner dict before moving on.
- Use `yield from` for the dicts inside.
- An empty dict inside yields nothing. A list is a value, not something to walk into.
- If a `prefix` is given, every path starts with it and a dot: `walk({"x": 1}, "root")` yields `("root.x", 1)`.
--- starter
def walk(config, prefix=""):
    for key, value in config.items():
        yield key, value
--- solution
def walk(config, prefix=""):
    for key, value in config.items():
        path = f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key
        if isinstance(value, dict):
            yield from walk(value, path)
        else:
            yield path, value
--- hint
This is the lesson's `walk` over nested lists, done for dicts. The recursive call gets the path so far as its prefix.
--- hint
Work out the path first: `f"{prefix}.{key}" if prefix else key`. If the value is a dict, `yield from walk(value, path)`; otherwise `yield path, value`.
--- check case | Paths join the keys with dots
list(walk({"engine": {"thrust": 250, "fuel": {"type": "RP-1"}}, "crew": 3}))
=> [("engine.thrust", 250), ("engine.fuel.type", "RP-1"), ("crew", 3)]
--- check case | An empty config
list(walk({}))
=> []
--- check case | Empty dicts inside yield nothing
list(walk({"a": {}, "b": {"c": {}}, "d": None}))
=> [("d", None)]
--- check case | A prefix starts every path
list(walk({"x": 1, "y": {"z": 2}}, "root"))
=> [("root.x", 1), ("root.y.z", 2)]
--- check test | Lists are values
dict(walk({"s": {"list": [1, {"no": 2}]}})) == {"s.list": [1, {"no": 2}]}
--- check source | Uses yield from
\byield\s+from\b

+++ practice | A launch sequencer
--- task
Write a generator `sequencer()` that follows a rocket through its flight phases. Prime it with `next()`, which hands out `"pad"`. After that, each `send(event)` returns the phase after that event:

| phase | event | next phase |
|---|---|---|
| `"pad"` | `"ignite"` | `"ascent"` |
| `"ascent"` | `"meco"` | `"coast"` |
| `"coast"` | `"deploy"` | `"orbit"` |
| any other phase | `"abort"` | `"aborted"` |

Any other event leaves the phase as it is. Once the phase is `"orbit"` or `"aborted"`, it never changes again, whatever is sent. Keep the three ordinary moves in a dict whose keys are `(phase, event)` tuples.

Then write `run_sequence(events)`, which primes a fresh sequencer, sends it each event in turn, and returns the list of phases it answered. For example, `run_sequence(["ignite", "meco", "deploy"])` gives `["ascent", "coast", "orbit"]`.
--- starter
def sequencer():
    phase = "pad"
    while True:
        event = yield phase


def run_sequence(events):
    return []
--- solution
TRANSITIONS = {
    ("pad", "ignite"): "ascent",
    ("ascent", "meco"): "coast",
    ("coast", "deploy"): "orbit",
}


def sequencer():
    phase = "pad"
    while True:
        event = yield phase
        if phase in ("orbit", "aborted"):
            continue
        if event == "abort":
            phase = "aborted"
        else:
            phase = TRANSITIONS.get((phase, event), phase)


def run_sequence(events):
    machine = sequencer()
    next(machine)
    return [machine.send(event) for event in events]
--- hint
The phase is the generator's memory. After `event = yield phase` receives an event, work out the new phase; the loop then hands it out at the next `yield`.
--- hint
Check the two final phases first and `continue` if the rocket is in one. Then `"abort"`, then the dict: `TRANSITIONS.get((phase, event), phase)` gives the new phase, or the old one when the pair is not in the dict. `run_sequence` primes with `next()` and sends each event in a comprehension.
--- check case | A good flight
run_sequence(["ignite", "meco", "deploy"])
=> ["ascent", "coast", "orbit"]
--- check case | Events out of order are ignored, and an abort is final
run_sequence(["meco", "ignite", "ignite", "abort", "ignite"])
=> ["pad", "ascent", "ascent", "aborted", "aborted"]
--- check case | Nothing changes once in orbit, not even an abort
run_sequence(["ignite", "meco", "deploy", "abort"])
=> ["ascent", "coast", "orbit", "orbit"]
--- check case | No events
run_sequence([])
=> []
--- check test | Priming hands out "pad", and an abort on the pad works
next(sequencer()) == "pad" and run_sequence(["abort", "ignite"]) == ["aborted", "aborted"]

+++ practice | A moving average
--- task
Write a generator `moving_average(window)`. Prime it with `next()`, which hands out `None`. After that, each `send(x)` returns the average of the last `window` numbers sent, or of all of them while fewer than `window` have arrived. With `window` 3, sending 3, 6, 9 and 12 gives `3.0`, `4.5`, `6.0` and `9.0`.

The starter averages everything ever sent. Fix it, and get these edge cases right:

- `window` 1 gives back each number itself, as a `float`.
- Negative numbers and repeats work.
- A `window` below 1 raises `ValueError` when the generator is primed (the first `next`). A generator runs none of its body before that.
- Keep only the last `window` numbers, so a long stream does not use more and more memory. A `deque` from `collections`, with `append` and `popleft`, does this well.
--- starter
def moving_average(window):
    numbers = []
    average = None
    while True:
        x = yield average
        numbers.append(x)
        average = sum(numbers) / len(numbers)
--- solution
from collections import deque


def moving_average(window):
    if window < 1:
        raise ValueError("window must be at least 1")
    recent = deque()
    average = None
    while True:
        x = yield average
        recent.append(x)
        if len(recent) > window:
            recent.popleft()
        average = sum(recent) / len(recent)
--- hint
Put the check for `window` at the top of the body: it runs when the generator is primed, which is exactly when the task wants the error.
--- hint
After appending each number, drop the oldest with `popleft()` whenever there are more than `window`. The average is then `sum(recent) / len(recent)`, which also works while there are fewer than `window`.
--- check case | Window of 3
(lambda g: (next(g), [g.send(x) for x in [3, 6, 9, 12]])[1])(moving_average(3))
=> [3.0, 4.5, 6.0, 9.0]
--- check case | Window of 1 gives each number back
(lambda g: (next(g), [g.send(x) for x in [5, -2, 7]])[1])(moving_average(1))
=> [5.0, -2.0, 7.0]
--- check case | Negative numbers and repeats
(lambda g: (next(g), [g.send(x) for x in [-4, -4, 2, 2]])[1])(moving_average(2))
=> [-4.0, -4.0, -1.0, 2.0]
--- check test | A window below 1 raises ValueError when primed
(lambda g: raises(ValueError, lambda: next(g)))(moving_average(0)) and raises(ValueError, lambda: next(moving_average(-3)))
--- check test | Fewer numbers than the window
(lambda g: (next(g), g.send(1), g.send(2))[2])(moving_average(10)) == 1.5
--- check case | After 1,000 numbers, only the last 5 count
(lambda g: (next(g), [g.send(i) for i in range(1000)])[1][-1])(moving_average(5))
=> 997.0
--- check test | Keeps only the last window numbers
(lambda g: (next(g), [g.send(i) for i in range(1000)], max((len(v) for v in g.gi_frame.f_locals.values() if isinstance(v, (list, __import__("collections").deque))), default=0))[2] <= 5)(moving_average(5))
?? Drop the oldest number once you have more than window of them.

+++ practice | Debug: every other send
--- task
`score_keeper()` should be primed with `next()`, which hands out the starting score, `0`. After that, every `send(event)` should return the new score: `"hit"` adds 10, `"miss"` takes away 5, and any other event changes nothing.

Instead, only every other `send` works. `send("hit")` gives `10`, the next `send("hit")` gives `None`, and the one after that gives `20`. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def score_keeper():
    score = 0
    while True:
        event = yield
        if event == "hit":
            score += 10
        elif event == "miss":
            score -= 5
        yield score
--- solution
def score_keeper():
    score = 0
    while True:
        event = yield score
        if event == "hit":
            score += 10
        elif event == "miss":
            score -= 5
--- hint
Count the `yield`s in one trip round the loop. Each `send` runs the generator only as far as the *next* `yield`, whichever one that is.
--- hint
One `yield` per trip is enough, because `yield` both hands a value out and takes the next one in: `event = yield score`.
--- check case | Priming, then hit, hit, miss
(lambda g: (next(g), g.send("hit"), g.send("hit"), g.send("miss")))(score_keeper())
=> (0, 10, 20, 15)
--- check case | Other events change nothing
(lambda g: (next(g), g.send("jump"), g.send("hit"), g.send("?"))[1:])(score_keeper())
=> (0, 10, 10)
--- check test | Four misses in a row
(lambda g: (next(g), [g.send("miss") for _ in range(4)])[1])(score_keeper()) == [-5, -10, -15, -20]

+++ practice | Reassembling radio lines
--- task
Radio data arrives in chunks of text that ignore line breaks: first `"temp 21\nfu"`, then `"el 80\n"`. Write two things.

- A generator `assembler()`. Prime it with `next()`, which hands out `[]`. Each `send(chunk)` returns the list of lines that chunk completed, without their `"\n"`, and keeps any unfinished part for later. Sending `None` ends the generator, and it **returns** the unfinished part: a string, perhaps `""`.
- `read_all(chunks)`, which primes a fresh assembler, sends it every chunk and collects every line it returns, in order. At the end it sends `None`, catches the `StopIteration`, and if the unfinished part it carries is not empty, adds it as one last line.

So sending `"temp 21\nfu"` returns `["temp 21"]`, and then `"el 80\n"` returns `["fuel 80"]`. `read_all(["a\nb", "c\n", "d"])` gives `["a", "bc", "d"]`. Empty lines count: `read_all(["\n\nx"])` gives `["", "", "x"]`.
--- starter
def assembler():
    lines = []
    while True:
        chunk = yield lines
        lines = chunk.split("\n")


def read_all(chunks):
    return []
--- solution
def assembler():
    buffer = ""
    lines = []
    while True:
        chunk = yield lines
        if chunk is None:
            return buffer
        buffer += chunk
        *lines, buffer = buffer.split("\n")


def read_all(chunks):
    machine = assembler()
    next(machine)
    result = []
    for chunk in chunks:
        result.extend(machine.send(chunk))
    try:
        machine.send(None)
    except StopIteration as stop:
        if stop.value:
            result.append(stop.value)
    return result
--- hint
Keep a string `buffer` of text that has not ended in a newline yet. Add each chunk to it, then split it on `"\n"`: every piece but the last is a finished line, and the last piece is the new buffer, maybe `""`.
--- hint
Star unpacking does that split in one line: `*lines, buffer = buffer.split("\n")`. When the chunk is `None`, `return buffer`. In `read_all`, the returned value arrives as `.value` on the `StopIteration` you catch with `except StopIteration as stop:`.
--- check case | Lines finished across two chunks
(lambda g: (next(g), g.send("temp 21\nfu"), g.send("el 80\n")))(assembler())
=> ([], ["temp 21"], ["fuel 80"])
--- check case | Several lines at once, and an empty chunk
(lambda g: (next(g), g.send("a\nb\nc"), g.send(""), g.send("\n"))[1:])(assembler())
=> (["a", "b"], [], ["c"])
--- check case | Sending None returns the unfinished part
(lambda g: (next(g), g.send("x\npartial"), (lambda h: (exec("try:\n    g.send(None)\nexcept StopIteration as s:\n    v = s.value", h), h["v"])[1])({"g": g}))[2])(assembler())
=> "partial"
--- check case | read_all keeps the last unfinished line
read_all(["a\nb", "c\n", "d"])
=> ["a", "bc", "d"]
--- check case | No unfinished part, no extra line
read_all(["one\n", "two\n"])
=> ["one", "two"]
--- check case | No chunks at all
read_all([])
=> []
--- check case | Empty lines count
read_all(["\n\nx"])
=> ["", "", "x"]
--- check test | Thousands of one-letter chunks
read_all(list("ab\ncd\n" * 1000)) == ["ab", "cd"] * 1000

=== py4-05 | asyncio: concurrency with async and await
--- teach
Last lesson a generator learned to pause at `yield`, take a value from `send`, and carry on later. This lesson uses that same pause-and-resume trick for something very practical: doing lots of **waiting** at once.

Picture one cook making dinner. She puts the pasta on to boil. She does not stand and stare at the pot for ten minutes. She chops the salad while the water heats, and goes back to the pot when it is ready. One cook, several dishes on the go, and nobody standing idle.

Programs spend a surprising amount of time [[waiting|why-programs-wait]]: for a reply from another computer, for a database, for a timer. Python's `asyncio` module lets one [[thread|what-a-thread-is]] (one cook) start many waits and deal with whichever finishes first, instead of finishing each wait before starting the next.

That is called **concurrency**: juggling several tasks, switching between them while each one waits. It is not **parallelism**, which means doing several computations at the very same instant on several processors. The cook is concurrent, not parallel: she only ever does one thing at a time, but she never wastes the waiting.

## Step 1: `async def` makes a coroutine

Put `async` in front of `def` and you get a **coroutine function**: a function that is allowed to pause while it waits.

```python
import asyncio

async def fetch(name, delay):
    await asyncio.sleep(delay)      # pretend this is a network call
    return f"{name} ready"
```

`asyncio.sleep(delay)` waits `delay` seconds without blocking anything else. It is our stand-in for "wait for a reply from somewhere".

Here is the surprise. Calling `fetch("a", 1)` does **not** run the function. It hands back a **[[coroutine|where-coroutine-comes-from]] object**, a paused function that has not started yet (just like calling a generator function gives you a generator that has not run yet):

```python
c = fetch("a", 1)
type(c).__name__     # 'coroutine'   nothing has run yet
```

## Step 2: `await` runs it

To actually run a coroutine and get its result, you **`await`** it. The word means "run this, and while it is waiting, let other tasks have a turn":

```python
a = await fetch("a", 1)     # a is now 'a ready'
```

Who hands out the turns? The **[[event loop|the-event-loop]]**: the part of `asyncio` that keeps a list of paused tasks and wakes each one up when the thing it was waiting for is done.

Two rules about `await`:

- It only works inside an `async def` function.
- Or at the top level of a program, in environments that allow it. This editor does, so the code at the bottom of the task can say `await` directly.

## Step 3: one at a time, or all at once

Awaiting one coroutine after another is still one at a time. The second wait does not start until the first has finished:

```python
a = await fetch("a", 1)     # 1 second
b = await fetch("b", 1)     # another second: about 2 seconds in total
```

**`asyncio.gather`** starts all the coroutines you give it, lets them wait **side by side**, and hands back all their results once every one has finished:

```python
a, b = await asyncio.gather(fetch("a", 1), fetch("b", 1))   # about 1 second in total
```

The results come back **in the order you passed them in**, not the order they finished. So this list starts with the slow one, even though the fast one was done first:

```python
await asyncio.gather(fetch("slow", 0.3), fetch("fast", 0.1))
# ['slow ready', 'fast ready']
```

The [[saving in time|picture-of-the-saving]] is the whole point: two one-second waits take about one second, not two.

## Step 4: gather a whole list

Often the jobs arrive as a list. `gather` wants each coroutine as a separate argument, so you build them with a generator expression and **spread** them with `*`, the star you met in the `*args` lesson ("take these items and pass each one as its own argument"):

```python
jobs = [("x", 0.2), ("y", 0.1)]
await asyncio.gather(*(fetch(name, delay) for name, delay in jobs))
# ['x ready', 'y ready']
```

Read the inside first: for each `(name, delay)` pair, make the coroutine `fetch(name, delay)`. The `*` then passes them all to `gather`.

## Step 5: a queue and a fixed number of workers

`gather` starts **everything** at once. Sometimes that is too many: a thousand downloads at the same moment might overload the other computer. Then you want "at most `n` jobs running at a time".

Picture a restaurant: order tickets pile up on a counter, and three cooks each take the next ticket as soon as their hands are free. In `asyncio` the counter is an **`asyncio.Queue`**, a line of items waiting to be handled, first in, first out. The cooks are **worker tasks**.

The pieces, one at a time:

- `queue.put(item)` adds an item to the back of the line. You `await` it.
- `queue.get()` takes the item at the front. If the line is empty, it waits until something arrives. You `await` it too.
- `queue.task_done()` tells the queue "I have finished the item I took".
- `queue.join()` waits until every item that was put in has been marked `task_done()`.
- `asyncio.create_task(coro)` starts a coroutine running as a separate **task**, right away, without waiting for it. It gives you back the task so you can stop it later.
- `task.cancel()` stops a task.

Put together (here `handle` stands for whatever slow work each item needs):

```python
async def worker(queue, results):
    while True:                        # a worker never stops by itself
        item = await queue.get()       # take the next ticket (or wait for one)
        try:
            results.append(await handle(item))
        finally:
            queue.task_done()          # tell the queue this item is finished

async def run(items, n):
    queue = asyncio.Queue()
    results = []
    workers = [asyncio.create_task(worker(queue, results)) for _ in range(n)]
    for item in items:
        await queue.put(item)          # the producer fills the queue
    await queue.join()                 # wait until every item is task_done
    for w in workers:
        w.cancel()                     # the workers loop forever; stop them
    return results
```

The code that fills the queue is the **producer**; the workers are **consumers**. `task_done()` sits in a `finally` (the clean-up block from the exceptions lesson) so it runs even if `handle` raises an error. With 6 items that each take 0.1 seconds and 3 workers, the whole thing takes about 0.2 seconds: two rounds of three.

Notice that the results come back in the order the workers *finished*, which may not match the order of `items`. If order matters, sort them afterwards.

**Watch out** for three mistakes:

- Forgetting `await`. You get a coroutine object and a warning ("coroutine was never awaited"), not a result.
- Calling `time.sleep` inside a coroutine. It freezes **everything**, because while the one thread sleeps, the event loop cannot give anyone else a turn. Use `await asyncio.sleep` instead.
- Forgetting `task_done()`. Then `queue.join()` waits forever for items that already finished.

This machinery is how real programs talk to many things at once; see [[where engineers use it|asyncio-in-real-life]]. And it did not appear from nowhere: it [[grew out of generators|history-of-async]] like the ones from last lesson.

::: context why-programs-wait Why a program waits so much
A computer adds numbers in billionths of a second. A message to a server on another continent and back takes tens of milliseconds, and a slow database query can take much longer. To the processor, that reply takes forever: millions of steps it could have done in the meantime. So a program that talks to a network spends most of its life waiting, not computing. That waiting is what `asyncio` fills with useful work.
:::

::: context what-a-thread-is One line of work
A **thread** is one line of work inside a running program: one "cook" following the instructions step by step. A program can have several threads, but `asyncio` normally uses just one. It gets its speed not from more cooks but from never letting the one cook stand idle. That also makes it simpler to reason about: two tasks never change the same variable at the very same instant, because only one of them runs at a time.
:::

::: context where-coroutine-comes-from Where the word comes from
"Coroutine" was coined by the computer scientist Melvin Conway in the late 1950s. A normal subroutine is called, runs to the end and returns. Co-routines take turns as equals: each runs a while, pauses, and lets another continue from where it stopped. That is exactly what a Python generator does at `yield`, and what a coroutine does at `await`.
:::

::: context the-event-loop The traffic controller
The event loop is a loop inside `asyncio` that repeats one job: "which paused tasks are ready to go on?" A task that reaches `await asyncio.sleep(1)` tells the loop "wake me in a second" and pauses. The loop runs another ready task in the meantime. When the second is up, it resumes the first one right after its `await`. It is the same pause-and-resume you saw with `send` last lesson, driven by the loop instead of by you.
:::

::: context picture-of-the-saving Two timelines side by side
With plain `await`, each wait starts only when the one before has ended, so the total is the sum of the waits. With `gather`, all the waits start together, so the total is only as long as the longest one.

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 160" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <text x="10" y="18" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44">one after another: about 2 s</text>
  <rect x="20" y="28" width="140" height="18" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="90" y="41" font-size="11" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">a (1 s)</text>

  <rect x="20" y="124" width="140" height="18" fill="#f2b880" stroke="#1f2a44"/>

```
:::

::: context asyncio-in-real-life Where engineers use it
Anything that talks to many other machines at once is a good fit: a web server answering thousands of visitors, a scraper downloading pages, a ground-station tool polling many sensors or radios. Popular Python web frameworks such as FastAPI are built on it. The queue-and-workers pattern shows up whenever you must limit how many requests hit a service at once. It is not a fit for heavy number crunching: that keeps the one thread busy, and there is no waiting to fill.
:::

::: context history-of-async From generators to await
`asyncio` arrived in Python 3.4, in 2014, and its first coroutines were plain generators that used `yield from`, the delegation you met last lesson. The dedicated keywords `async` and `await` came in Python 3.5, a year later, so that coroutines would look different from ordinary generators and be harder to mix up.
:::
--- task
Write four coroutines (each is an `async def` function):

- `fetch(name, delay)`: waits `delay` seconds with `asyncio.sleep` and returns `f"{name}:{delay}"`.
- `fetch_all(jobs)`: takes a list of `(name, delay)` pairs, runs every fetch **at the same time** with `asyncio.gather`, and returns the results as a list, in the same order as `jobs`.
- `square_slowly(x)`: waits `0.05` seconds and returns `x * x`.
- `process(items, workers)`: squares every item with `square_slowly`, using an `asyncio.Queue` and exactly `workers` worker tasks, and returns the results **sorted**.

The code at the bottom of the starter already runs them and times them; keep it. The starter's `fetch_all` and `process` work, but one job at a time, which is too slow.
--- starter
import asyncio
import time


async def fetch(name, delay):
    await asyncio.sleep(delay)
    return f"{name}:{delay}"


async def fetch_all(jobs):
    results = []
    for name, delay in jobs:
        results.append(await fetch(name, delay))
    return results


async def square_slowly(x):
    await asyncio.sleep(0.05)
    return x * x


async def process(items, workers):
    return sorted([await square_slowly(x) for x in items])


start = time.perf_counter()
results = await fetch_all([("a", 0.3), ("b", 0.1), ("c", 0.2)])
fetch_seconds = time.perf_counter() - start

start = time.perf_counter()
squares = await process(list(range(20)), 5)
process_seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
print(results, round(fetch_seconds, 1))
--- solution
import asyncio
import time


async def fetch(name, delay):
    await asyncio.sleep(delay)
    return f"{name}:{delay}"


async def fetch_all(jobs):
    return list(await asyncio.gather(*(fetch(name, delay) for name, delay in jobs)))


async def square_slowly(x):
    await asyncio.sleep(0.05)
    return x * x


async def process(items, workers):
    queue = asyncio.Queue()
    results = []

    async def worker():
        while True:
            item = await queue.get()
            try:
                results.append(await square_slowly(item))
            finally:
                queue.task_done()

    tasks = [asyncio.create_task(worker()) for _ in range(workers)]
    for item in items:
        await queue.put(item)
    await queue.join()
    for task in tasks:
        task.cancel()
    return sorted(results)


start = time.perf_counter()
results = await fetch_all([("a", 0.3), ("b", 0.1), ("c", 0.2)])
fetch_seconds = time.perf_counter() - start

start = time.perf_counter()
squares = await process(list(range(20)), 5)
process_seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
print(results, round(fetch_seconds, 1))
--- hint
`fetch_all` is Step 4: build one `fetch(name, delay)` coroutine per job with a generator expression, spread them into `asyncio.gather` with `*`, and `await` the result. Wrap it in `list(...)`.
--- hint
`process` is Step 5. Make a `Queue` and an empty `results` list, define a small `async def worker():` inside `process`, start `workers` of them with `asyncio.create_task(worker())`, `await queue.put(item)` for every item, then `await queue.join()`.
--- hint
Each worker is `while True:` with `item = await queue.get()`, then `results.append(await square_slowly(item))` inside `try:`, and `queue.task_done()` inside `finally:`. After `join()`, call `.cancel()` on every task and return `sorted(results)`.
--- check case | fetch_all returns results in the order of the jobs
results
=> ["a:0.3", "b:0.1", "c:0.2"]
--- check test | The fetches ran at the same time (about 0.3s, not 0.6s)
fetch_seconds < 0.55
?? Awaiting each fetch in a loop waits for them one after another. Use asyncio.gather.
--- check case | process squares everything
squares
=> [x * x for x in range(20)]
--- check test | Five workers made it about five times faster than one at a time
process_seconds < 0.8
?? 20 items at 0.05s each is 1s one by one. Five workers pulling from a Queue should take about 0.2s.
--- check test | They are all coroutine functions
all(__import__("inspect").iscoroutinefunction(f) for f in (fetch, fetch_all, square_slowly, process))
--- check source | process uses an asyncio.Queue
asyncio\.Queue\(

+++ practice | Ping every station at once
--- task
Write two coroutines (each is an `async def` function).

- `ping(station, delay)` waits `delay` seconds with `asyncio.sleep`, then returns `f"{station} ok"`. The starter has it already.
- `ping_all(stations)`: `stations` is a dict from each station's name to its delay. Ping them all **at the same time** with `asyncio.gather`, and return a dict from each name to its reply, in the same order as `stations`. An empty dict gives `{}`.

The code at the bottom of the starter already runs and times them; keep it. The starter's `ping_all` works, but it pings one station after another, which takes 0.5 seconds instead of about 0.2.
--- starter
import asyncio
import time


async def ping(station, delay):
    await asyncio.sleep(delay)
    return f"{station} ok"


async def ping_all(stations):
    replies = {}
    for station, delay in stations.items():
        replies[station] = await ping(station, delay)
    return replies


start = time.perf_counter()

--- solution
import asyncio
import time


async def ping(station, delay):
    await asyncio.sleep(delay)
    return f"{station} ok"


async def ping_all(stations):
    answers = await asyncio.gather(*(ping(station, delay) for station, delay in stations.items()))
    return dict(zip(stations, answers))


start = time.perf_counter()
replies = await ping_all({"goldstone": 0.2, "madrid": 0.1, "canberra": 0.2})
seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
nothing = await ping_all({})
--- hint
Build one `ping(station, delay)` coroutine per item of `stations.items()` with a generator expression, spread them into `asyncio.gather` with `*`, and `await` it. The replies come back in the order you passed the coroutines in.
--- hint
A dict loops over its keys in order, so `zip(stations, answers)` pairs each name with its reply, and `dict(...)` turns the pairs into the result.
--- check case | Every station answers, under its own name
replies
=> {"goldstone": "goldstone ok", "madrid": "madrid ok", "canberra": "canberra ok"}
--- check test | The names stay in the order they were given
list(replies) == ["goldstone", "madrid", "canberra"]
--- check test | The pings ran at the same time (about 0.2s, not 0.5s)
seconds < 0.4
?? Awaiting each ping in a loop waits for them one after another. Use asyncio.gather.
--- check case | No stations
nothing
=> {}
--- check test | Both are coroutine functions
all(__import__("inspect").iscoroutinefunction(f) for f in (ping, ping_all))

+++ practice | Two steps per image, all images at once
--- task
Every image from a probe must be downloaded and then decoded. The starter has `download(name)`, which waits 0.1 seconds and returns `name + ".raw"`, and `decode(data)`, which waits 0.1 seconds and returns `data.upper()`.

- Write `handle(name)`, a coroutine that downloads one image and then decodes it, one step after the other, and returns what `decode` returned.
- Rewrite `handle_all(names)` so that it handles every name **at the same time** and returns the results in the order of `names`. No names gives `[]`.

Handled one by one, five images take a whole second. Handled together, they take about 0.2 seconds, however many there are. Keep the code at the bottom that runs and times them.
--- starter
import asyncio
import time


async def download(name):
    await asyncio.sleep(0.1)
    return name + ".raw"


async def decode(data):
    await asyncio.sleep(0.1)
    return data.upper()


async def handle_all(names):
    results = []
    for name in names:
        data = await download(name)
        results.append(await decode(data))
    return results


start = time.perf_counter()

empty = await handle_all([])
--- solution
import asyncio
import time


async def download(name):
    await asyncio.sleep(0.1)
    return name + ".raw"


async def decode(data):
    await asyncio.sleep(0.1)
    return data.upper()


async def handle(name):
    data = await download(name)
    return await decode(data)


async def handle_all(names):
    return list(await asyncio.gather(*(handle(name) for name in names)))


start = time.perf_counter()
images = await handle_all(["a", "b", "c", "d", "e"])
seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
empty = await handle_all([])
--- hint
Inside one image's work the two steps must stay in order, because `decode` needs what `download` returned. Between images there is no order at all. So put the two steps in their own coroutine, `handle`, and run one `handle` per name side by side.
--- hint
`handle_all` is then one line: gather one `handle(name)` per name, spread in with `*`, await it, and wrap it in `list(...)`.
--- check case | Every image is downloaded and decoded, in order

=> ["A.RAW", "B.RAW", "C.RAW", "D.RAW", "E.RAW"]
--- check test | The images were handled at the same time (about 0.2s, not 1s)
seconds < 0.5
?? Awaiting download and decode for one name before starting the next is one at a time. Gather one handle per name.
--- check case | No images
empty
=> []
--- check test | handle and handle_all are coroutine functions
all(__import__("inspect").iscoroutinefunction(f) for f in (handle, handle_all))

+++ practice | Word counts from many pages
--- task
`PAGES` in the starter maps page names to their text, and `fetch(page)` waits 0.1 seconds and returns that page's text, or `""` for a page that is not in `PAGES`.

Rewrite `word_counts(pages)`, a coroutine, so that it fetches all the pages **at the same time** with `asyncio.gather` and returns a `Counter` of every word on all of them, in lowercase. Split each text into words with `.split()`.

The starter gets the right counts, but fetches one page after another. Keep the code at the bottom that runs and times it.
--- starter
import asyncio
import time
from collections import Counter

PAGES = {
    "news": "Launch today Launch window opens",
    "wiki": "The launch window is short",
    "blog": "",
}


async def fetch(page):
    await asyncio.sleep(0.1)
    return PAGES.get(page, "")


async def word_counts(pages):
    counts = Counter()
    for page in pages:
        text = await fetch(page)
        for word in text.lower().split():
            counts[word] += 1
    return counts


start = time.perf_counter()
counts = await word_counts(["news", "wiki", "blog", "missing"])
seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
--- solution
import asyncio
import time
from collections import Counter

PAGES = {
    "news": "Launch today Launch window opens",
    "wiki": "The launch window is short",
    "blog": "",
}


async def fetch(page):
    await asyncio.sleep(0.1)
    return PAGES.get(page, "")


async def word_counts(pages):
    texts = await asyncio.gather(*(fetch(page) for page in pages))
    return Counter(word for text in texts for word in text.lower().split())


start = time.perf_counter()
counts = await word_counts(["news", "wiki", "blog", "missing"])
seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
--- hint
First get every text at once: `texts = await asyncio.gather(*(fetch(page) for page in pages))`. Counting needs no waiting at all, so it happens afterwards, in ordinary code.
--- hint
A `Counter` can be built straight from all the words, with a generator expression that has two `for`s: one over the texts, and one over the words of each text.
--- check test | Counts every word on every page, in lowercase
counts == Counter({"launch": 3, "window": 2, "today": 1, "opens": 1, "the": 1, "is": 1, "short": 1})
--- check test | The result is a Counter
isinstance(counts, Counter)
--- check case | The most common word
counts.most_common(1)
=> [("launch", 3)]
--- check test | The pages were fetched at the same time (about 0.1s, not 0.4s)
seconds < 0.3

+++ practice | A few workers, results in order
--- task
Write `run_limited(delays, limit)`, a coroutine. Each number in `delays` is one job: `job(i, delay)` in the starter waits `delay` seconds and returns `f"job {i} done"`, where `i` is the job's position in the list. It also records in `peak` the most jobs that were ever running at the same moment.

Run the jobs with an `asyncio.Queue` and exactly `limit` worker tasks, so that at most `limit` jobs run at once. Return the results **in the order of `delays`**, not in the order they finished. Put `(i, delay)` pairs on the queue, so each worker knows where its result belongs. Get these edge cases right:

- No jobs gives `[]`.
- A `limit` bigger than the number of jobs is fine.
- A `limit` below 1 raises `ValueError`.

Keep the code at the bottom of the starter, which runs these cases.
--- starter
import asyncio
import time

running = 0
peak = 0


async def job(i, delay):
    global running, peak
    running += 1
    peak = max(peak, running)
    await asyncio.sleep(delay)
    running -= 1
    return f"job {i} done"


async def run_limited(delays, limit):
    return [await job(i, delay) for i, delay in enumerate(delays)]


start = time.perf_counter()
done = await run_limited([0.2, 0.05, 0.1, 0.05], 2)
limited_seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
peak_limited = peak
none = await run_limited([], 3)
many = await run_limited([0.05, 0.05], 10)
try:
    await run_limited([0.1], 0)
    refused = False
except ValueError:
    refused = True
--- solution
import asyncio
import time

running = 0
peak = 0


async def job(i, delay):
    global running, peak
    running += 1
    peak = max(peak, running)
    await asyncio.sleep(delay)
    running -= 1
    return f"job {i} done"


async def run_limited(delays, limit):
    if limit < 1:
        raise ValueError("limit must be at least 1")
    queue = asyncio.Queue()
    results = [None] * len(delays)

    async def worker():
        while True:
            i, delay = await queue.get()
            try:
                results[i] = await job(i, delay)
            finally:
                queue.task_done()

    workers = [asyncio.create_task(worker()) for _ in range(limit)]
    for i, delay in enumerate(delays):
        await queue.put((i, delay))
    await queue.join()
    for w in workers:
        w.cancel()
    return results


start = time.perf_counter()
done = await run_limited([0.2, 0.05, 0.1, 0.05], 2)
limited_seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
peak_limited = peak
none = await run_limited([], 3)
many = await run_limited([0.05, 0.05], 10)
try:
    await run_limited([0.1], 0)
    refused = False
except ValueError:
    refused = True
--- hint
Start from the queue and workers in the lesson's Step 5. To keep the order, make `results = [None] * len(delays)` first, and let each worker write into its own slot: `results[i] = await job(i, delay)`.
--- hint
Check `limit` before you make anything. `task_done()` goes in a `finally`, so `queue.join()` is never left waiting. With no jobs, `join()` returns at once; cancel the workers afterwards either way.
--- check case | Results come back in the order of the jobs
done
=> ["job 0 done", "job 1 done", "job 2 done", "job 3 done"]
--- check test | Two workers: two jobs at a time, never more
peak_limited == 2
?? One job at a time gives a peak of 1, and starting every job at once gives 4. Start exactly limit workers.
--- check test | Two workers finish in about 0.2s, not 0.4s
limited_seconds < 0.33
--- check case | No jobs
none
=> []
--- check case | More workers than jobs
many
=> ["job 0 done", "job 1 done"]
--- check case | A limit of 0 raises ValueError
refused
=> True
--- check source | Uses an asyncio.Queue
asyncio\.Queue\(

+++ practice | Debug: beacons that take turns
--- task
`beacon(name, delay)` should wait `delay` seconds and return `name`. `listen(names)` gathers one beacon per name, each waiting 0.1 seconds, so five names should take about 0.1 seconds in total.

The names come back right, but it takes about 0.1 seconds **per beacon**: half a second for five. Find the bug and fix it. The fix is one line.
--- starter
import asyncio
import time


async def beacon(name, delay):
    time.sleep(delay)
    return name


async def listen(names):
    return list(await asyncio.gather(*(beacon(name, 0.1) for name in names)))


start = time.perf_counter()
heard = await listen(["alpha", "bravo", "charlie", "delta", "echo"])
seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
--- solution
import asyncio
import time


async def beacon(name, delay):
    await asyncio.sleep(delay)
    return name


async def listen(names):
    return list(await asyncio.gather(*(beacon(name, 0.1) for name in names)))


start = time.perf_counter()
heard = await listen(["alpha", "bravo", "charlie", "delta", "echo"])
seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
--- hint
`gather` is right. So look at what each beacon does while it waits: does it let the other beacons have a turn?
--- hint
`time.sleep` stops the one thread, and with it the event loop. The waiting kind that lets others run is `await asyncio.sleep(delay)`.
--- check case | Every beacon is heard, in order
heard
=> ["alpha", "bravo", "charlie", "delta", "echo"]
--- check test | The beacons waited side by side (about 0.1s, not 0.5s)
seconds < 0.3
--- check source absent | No time.sleep inside a coroutine
