raises(Exception, lambda: test_parse_percent(lambda text: (lambda s: int(s[:-1]) if s.endswith("%") and s[:-1].strip().isdigit() and 0 < int(s[:-1]) <= 100 else int("bad"))(text.strip())))
?? Test exactly 0%.

+++ practice | Test a stack class
--- task
A stack has four operations: `push(item)` puts an item on top; `pop()` removes and returns the top item; `peek()` returns the top item **without** removing it; and `len(stack)` is the number of items. On an empty stack, both `pop()` and `peek()` raise `IndexError`.

Write `test_stack(make_stack)`. It receives `make_stack`, which returns a brand-new, empty stack each time it is called (it may be a class). Use only the four operations above, never the stack's insides. Your test must pass for a correct stack and raise `AssertionError` for every buggy one the checker tries: think of a stack that is really a queue, a `peek` that removes, a length that never goes down, and an empty stack that does not raise.
--- starter
def test_stack(make_stack):
    s = make_stack()
    s.push(1)
    assert s.pop() == 1
--- solution
def test_stack(make_stack):
    s = make_stack()
    assert len(s) == 0
    s.push(1)
    s.push(2)
    s.push(3)
    assert len(s) == 3
    # peek shows the top and leaves it there
    assert s.peek() == 3
    assert len(s) == 3
    # last in, first out
    assert s.pop() == 3
    assert s.pop() == 2
    assert len(s) == 1
    assert s.pop() == 1
    assert len(s) == 0
    # an empty stack refuses both
    for action in (s.pop, s.peek):
        try:
            action()
        except IndexError:
            pass
        else:
            raise AssertionError("an empty stack must raise IndexError")
--- hint
Push more than two different items. With only one item, a stack and a queue behave the same, and so do "top" and "bottom".
--- hint
Check `len` after a `peek` and after each `pop`, and pop the stack empty. Then check that `pop()` and `peek()` each raise `IndexError`, with `try` / `except IndexError` / `else: raise AssertionError(...)`.
--- check test | Passes a correct stack
(lambda g: (exec("class Stack:\n    def __init__(self):\n        self.items = []\n    def push(self, x):\n        self.items.append(x)\n    def pop(self):\n        if not self.items:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self.items.pop()\n    def peek(self):\n        if not self.items:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self.items[-1]\n    def __len__(self):\n        return len(self.items)\n", g), not raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_stack(g["Stack"])))[1])({})
--- check test | Passes another correct stack
(lambda g: (exec("class Pile:\n    def __init__(self):\n        self._data = {}\n    def push(self, x):\n        self._data[len(self._data)] = x\n    def pop(self):\n        if not self._data:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        top = self._data[len(self._data) - 1]\n        del self._data[len(self._data) - 1]\n        return top\n    def peek(self):\n        if not self._data:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self._data[len(self._data) - 1]\n    def __len__(self):\n        return len(self._data)\n", g), not raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_stack(g["Pile"])))[1])({})
--- check test | Catches a stack that is really a queue
(lambda g: (exec("class Stack:\n    def __init__(self):\n        self.items = []\n    def push(self, x):\n        self.items.append(x)\n    def pop(self):\n        if not self.items:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self.items.pop(0)\n    def peek(self):\n        if not self.items:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self.items[-1]\n    def __len__(self):\n        return len(self.items)\n", g), raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_stack(g["Stack"])))[1])({})
--- check test | Catches a pop that returns None when empty
(lambda g: (exec("class Stack:\n    def __init__(self):\n        self.items = []\n    def push(self, x):\n        self.items.append(x)\n    def pop(self):\n        return self.items.pop() if self.items else None\n    def peek(self):\n        if not self.items:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self.items[-1]\n    def __len__(self):\n        return len(self.items)\n", g), raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_stack(g["Stack"])))[1])({})
--- check test | Catches a peek that removes the item
(lambda g: (exec("class Stack:\n    def __init__(self):\n        self.items = []\n    def push(self, x):\n        self.items.append(x)\n    def pop(self):\n        if not self.items:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self.items.pop()\n    def peek(self):\n        if not self.items:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self.items.pop()\n    def __len__(self):\n        return len(self.items)\n", g), raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_stack(g["Stack"])))[1])({})
--- check test | Catches a length that never goes down
(lambda g: (exec("class Stack:\n    def __init__(self):\n        self.items = []\n        self.n = 0\n    def push(self, x):\n        self.items.append(x)\n        self.n += 1\n    def pop(self):\n        if not self.items:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self.items.pop()\n    def peek(self):\n        if not self.items:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self.items[-1]\n    def __len__(self):\n        return self.n\n", g), raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_stack(g["Stack"])))[1])({})
--- check test | Catches a peek that shows the bottom item
(lambda g: (exec("class Stack:\n    def __init__(self):\n        self.items = []\n    def push(self, x):\n        self.items.append(x)\n    def pop(self):\n        if not self.items:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self.items.pop()\n    def peek(self):\n        if not self.items:\n            raise IndexError('empty')\n        return self.items[0]\n    def __len__(self):\n        return len(self.items)\n", g), raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_stack(g["Stack"])))[1])({})

+++ practice | Test a median, edge cases included
--- task
`median(values)` returns the middle value of a list of numbers once they are sorted. For an even count it returns the average of the two middle values, so `median([4, 1, 3, 2])` is `2.5`. It must **not** change the list it is given, and for an empty list it raises `ValueError`.

Write `test_median(median)`. It must pass for a correct `median` and raise `AssertionError` for every buggy one the checker tries. The likely bugs sit in the edge cases: an unsorted list, an even count, whole-number division, a list that gets sorted in place, an empty list.
--- starter
def test_median(median):
    assert median([1, 2, 3]) == 2
--- solution
def test_median(median):
    assert median([3, 1, 2]) == 2
    assert median([4, 1, 3, 2]) == 2.5
    assert median([1, 2]) == 1.5
    assert median([7]) == 7
    assert median([-5, -1, -3]) == -3
    # the list passed in is left alone
    values = [5, 3, 9, 1]
    median(values)
    assert values == [5, 3, 9, 1], "median must not change its input"
    # an empty list is refused
    try:
        median([])
    except ValueError:
        pass
    else:
        raise AssertionError("an empty list must raise ValueError")
--- hint
An already sorted list like `[1, 2, 3]` cannot catch a median that forgets to sort. Use lists in a jumbled order, with both odd and even lengths.
--- hint
For the in-place bug, keep your own list in a variable, call `median` on it, then assert the list is still exactly what you made. Pick an even count whose middle average is not a whole number, like `[1, 2]`, to catch `//`.
--- check test | Passes a correct median
not raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_median(lambda values: (lambda s: (s[len(s) // 2] if len(s) % 2 else (s[len(s) // 2 - 1] + s[len(s) // 2]) / 2) if s else int("empty"))(sorted(values))))
--- check test | Catches a median that sorts the list it was given
(lambda good: raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_median(lambda values: (values.sort(), good(values))[1])))(lambda values: (lambda s: (s[len(s) // 2] if len(s) % 2 else (s[len(s) // 2 - 1] + s[len(s) // 2]) / 2) if s else int("empty"))(sorted(values)))
?? Keep your own list in a variable, call median on it, and check the list afterwards.
--- check test | Catches a median that forgets to sort
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_median(lambda values: (lambda s: (s[len(s) // 2] if len(s) % 2 else (s[len(s) // 2 - 1] + s[len(s) // 2]) / 2) if s else int("empty"))(list(values))))
--- check test | Catches a median that does not average an even count
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_median(lambda values: (lambda s: s[len(s) // 2] if s else int("empty"))(sorted(values))))
--- check test | Catches a median that averages with whole-number division
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_median(lambda values: (lambda s: (s[len(s) // 2] if len(s) % 2 else (s[len(s) // 2 - 1] + s[len(s) // 2]) // 2) if s else int("empty"))(sorted(values))))
--- check test | Catches a median that returns None for an empty list
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_median(lambda values: (lambda s: (s[len(s) // 2] if len(s) % 2 else (s[len(s) // 2 - 1] + s[len(s) // 2]) / 2) if s else None)(sorted(values))))

+++ practice | Debug: the test that always passes
--- task
`test_discount(discount)` is supposed to check `discount(price, percent)`, which takes `percent` percent off `price`: `discount(80, 25)` is `60`, and `discount(100, 10)` is `90`.

But the test passes for broken versions too, such as one that subtracts the percent as if it were money (`80 - 25` is 55). There are two mistakes in the test, in its first two lines. Fix them, so the test passes for a correct `discount` and raises `AssertionError` for broken ones.
--- starter
def test_discount(discount):
    assert (discount(80, 25) == 60, "25% off 80 is 60")
    assert discount(100, 10) == discount(100, 10)
    assert discount(50, 0) == 50
--- solution
def test_discount(discount):
    assert discount(80, 25) == 60, "25% off 80 is 60"
    assert discount(100, 10) == 90, "10% off 100 is 90"
    assert discount(50, 0) == 50
--- hint
Line one: what does `assert (something, "message")` assert? A tuple with two things in it is always truthy. Line two: where does its expected answer come from?
--- hint
Take the brackets off the first `assert`, so the comma separates the condition from the message. In the second line, write the expected answer by hand, `90`, instead of asking the code under test.
--- check test | Passes a correct discount
not raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_discount(lambda price, percent: price * (100 - percent) / 100))
--- check test | Catches a discount that subtracts the percent as money
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_discount(lambda price, percent: price - percent))
--- check test | Catches a discount that divides by 1,000
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_discount(lambda price, percent: price * (1 - percent / 1000)))
--- check test | Catches a discount that returns the amount taken off
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_discount(lambda price, percent: price * percent / 100))

+++ practice | Test a shipping price
--- task
Here is the specification for `shipping_cost(weight, express)`, where `weight` is in kilograms:

- A parcel of up to and including 1 kg costs 5.
- Every kilogram above 1 kg costs 2 more, and a part of a kilogram counts as a whole one: 1.2 kg costs 7, 2 kg costs 7, and 2.01 kg costs 9.
- `express=True` doubles the whole price.
- A weight of 0 or less raises `ValueError`.

Write `test_shipping_cost(shipping_cost)`. It must pass for a correct `shipping_cost` and raise `AssertionError` for every buggy one the checker tries. List the bugs you can imagine first, rule by rule, and make sure some case catches each one.
--- starter
def test_shipping_cost(shipping_cost):
    assert shipping_cost(1, False) == 5
    assert shipping_cost(1, True) == 10
--- solution
def test_shipping_cost(shipping_cost):
    # up to and including 1 kg
    assert shipping_cost(0.5, False) == 5
    assert shipping_cost(1, False) == 5
    # each started kilogram above 1 adds 2
    assert shipping_cost(1.2, False) == 7
    assert shipping_cost(2, False) == 7
    assert shipping_cost(2.01, False) == 9
    assert shipping_cost(5, False) == 13
    # express doubles the whole price, also for heavier parcels
    assert shipping_cost(1, True) == 10
    assert shipping_cost(2.5, True) == 18
    # zero and negative weights are refused
    for weight in (0, -1):
        try:
            shipping_cost(weight, False)
        except ValueError:
            pass
        else:
            raise AssertionError(f"weight {weight} should be refused")
--- hint
Walk the rules one at a time. The part-kilogram rule needs a weight just above a whole number (like 1.2 or 2.01) and a weight exactly on one (like 2). The 1 kg limit needs exactly 1.
--- hint
"Doubles" and "adds 5" give the same answer for a 1 kg parcel, so test express on a heavier one too. And "0 or less" means both 0 and a negative weight must raise.
--- check test | Passes a correct shipping_cost
not raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_shipping_cost(lambda weight, express: (5 + 2 * max(0, __import__("math").ceil(weight - 1))) * (2 if express else 1) if weight > 0 else int("bad")))
--- check test | Passes another correct shipping_cost
not raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_shipping_cost(lambda weight, express: (5 + 2 * sum(1 for k in range(1, 1000) if weight > k)) * (1 + bool(express)) if weight > 0 else int("bad")))
--- check test | Catches one that rounds part kilograms down
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_shipping_cost(lambda weight, express: (5 + 2 * max(0, int(weight - 1))) * (2 if express else 1) if weight > 0 else int("bad")))
--- check test | Catches one that is wrong exactly on whole kilograms
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_shipping_cost(lambda weight, express: (5 + 2 * (__import__("math").floor(weight) if weight >= 1 else 0)) * (2 if express else 1) if weight > 0 else int("bad")))
?? Test weights of exactly 1 and exactly 2.
--- check test | Catches one where express adds 5 instead of doubling
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_shipping_cost(lambda weight, express: (5 + 2 * max(0, __import__("math").ceil(weight - 1))) + (5 if express else 0) if weight > 0 else int("bad")))
?? Test express on a parcel heavier than 1 kg.
--- check test | Catches one that ignores express
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_shipping_cost(lambda weight, express: 5 + 2 * max(0, __import__("math").ceil(weight - 1)) if weight > 0 else int("bad")))
--- check test | Catches one that accepts a weight of 0
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_shipping_cost(lambda weight, express: (5 + 2 * max(0, __import__("math").ceil(weight - 1))) * (2 if express else 1) if weight >= 0 else int("bad")))
--- check test | Catches one that refuses 0 but accepts negative weights
raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_shipping_cost(lambda weight, express: (5 + 2 * max(0, __import__("math").ceil(weight - 1))) * (2 if express else 1) if weight != 0 else int("bad")))
?? Test a negative weight as well as 0.

=== py4-09 | Design: pure logic, thin I/O, injected dependencies
--- teach
Last lesson you wrote tests that catch bugs. This lesson is about the other half of the job: writing code that is *easy* to test in the first place.

Think of a pocket calculator. You press keys and a number appears on the screen. But the chip inside that does the arithmetic does not care about keys or screens. It takes numbers in and hands a number back. A factory can test that chip by feeding it numbers down a wire, with nobody pressing anything. Good programs are built the same way.

### The problem: logic tangled with input and output

Look at almost any hard-to-test program and you find the same problem: the **logic is tangled with the input and output**. The function that decides *what* should happen also reads the keyboard, prints, sends email or looks at the clock. **Input and output** (people say **I/O**) means everything a program takes in from the outside world or sends back out to it.

```python
def check_temperature():
    reading = float(input())      # reads the keyboard
    if reading > 90:
        print("too hot")          # writes to the screen
```

To test this, you would have to type a number and then read the screen. The decision (is it above 90?) is trapped between the reading and the printing.

### Step 1: a pure function

A **pure function** takes data in and returns data out, and does nothing else. Same input, same output, every time. Anything else a function does (printing, reading input, changing a variable outside itself, looking at the clock) is called a **[[side effect|side-effect]]**.

Pull the decision out into a pure function:

```python
def too_hot(reading):
    return reading > 90

too_hot(95)     # True
too_hot(20)     # False
```

Now a test is one line: `assert too_hot(95) is True`. No typing, no screen.

### Step 2: a pure core inside a thin shell

Do that for all the logic, and the program splits into two layers. This design is sometimes called **[[functional core, imperative shell|core-and-shell]]**:

- A **pure core**: functions that take data and return data. No `input()`, no `print()`, no global state, no clock. Same input, same output, every time. Very easy to test.
- A **thin shell**: a small layer that reads input, calls the core, and writes output. It has almost no logic, so it barely needs testing.

### Step 3: hand the function its tools

The shell still has to *do* something with the results: print them, or send an email. How can you test the part that does that without actually printing or emailing?

Picture a plumber who brings her own wrench versus one who is handed a wrench at the door. Hand her a toy wrench and you can watch what she does without flooding the kitchen. **Dependency injection** means the same thing for code: instead of reaching out for `print` or the clock itself, a function *receives* them as parameters. A **dependency** is anything a function relies on from outside itself.

This works because in Python [[a function is a value|functions-are-values]], like a number or a list. You can pass `print` into another function, and it calls whatever it was given:

```python
def run(lines, notify):
    for line in lines:
        if "ERROR" in line:
            notify(f"alert: {line}")

run(["ok", "ERROR disk full"], print)       # the real program: prints alert: ERROR disk full
sent = []
run(["ok", "ERROR disk full"], sent.append) # a test: collect what would be sent
sent                                        # ['alert: ERROR disk full']
```

In the test, `sent.append` stands in for `print`. It is a **[[fake|test-doubles]]**: a stand-in that records what happened instead of doing it for real. `run` cannot tell the difference, and it does not need to.

The same goes for time. Reading [[the clock|injected-time]] inside a function is a hidden input:

```python
from datetime import date

def is_overdue(due, today):
    return today > due

is_overdue(date(2026, 1, 10), date(2026, 1, 11))    # True
```

(`date` comes from the standard library's `datetime` module; `date(2026, 1, 10)` is the 10th of January 2026.) Written like this, `is_overdue(due, today)` is testable for any date. A version `is_overdue(due)` that calls `date.today()` inside is only testable today.

### Step 4: reading input

A program reads what the user types, or what a pipe sends in, from **[[standard input|standard-streams]]**: in this editor, that is the Input box under your code. There are two ways to read it:

- `input()` reads **one line** and returns it without the newline at the end.
- `sys.stdin.read()` reads **all of it at once** as one string. You need `import sys` at the top first; `sys` is the standard library module for talking to the system Python runs on.

Then `.splitlines()` turns that one string into a list of lines. If the Input box holds two lines, `widget 3 10` and `gizmo 25 5`:

```python
import sys

lines = sys.stdin.read().splitlines()
lines       # ['widget 3 10', 'gizmo 25 5']
```

Only the shell reads input. The pure core is handed the lines as an ordinary list, which is why a test can hand it a list it made up.

### Step 5: the entry point

The **entry point** is where a program starts doing things when you run it. In Python it goes under this line:

```python
if __name__ == "__main__":
    run(sys.stdin.read().splitlines(), print)
```

Read it as "if this file is being run as a program". Python gives every file a [[hidden variable|dunder-name]] called `__name__`. That block runs when the file is executed as a program, but not when another file imports it to reuse (or test) the functions. So a test can import `run` without the program starting to read input.

**Watch out:** "injecting" by setting a global variable that the function reads, like `NOTIFY = print` at the top of the file and `NOTIFY(...)` inside the function. That still couples everything to [[one shared value|globals-couple]]. Pass it in as an argument instead.

::: context side-effect Side effects are not bad, only hard to test
"Side effect" sounds like something a medicine does wrong, but in programming it means anything a function does besides handing back a value: printing, writing a file, changing a list it was given, moving a motor. A program with no side effects at all would be useless, because nobody could see its answer. The point is not to avoid them. It is to push them to the edges, into a few short functions, so that the part that thinks never has any.
:::

::: context core-and-shell A core you test, a shell you can see through
"Imperative" means giving commands, like "print this" or "read that". "Functional" here means built from pure functions. The name was popularized by programmer Gary Bernhardt in a 2012 screencast. The shape is the calculator from the start of the lesson: data goes in through the shell, the core does all the deciding, and the shell sends the answer out.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 170" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="70" y="10" width="220" height="150" rx="14" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="180" y="32" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">thin shell: reads, calls, writes</text>
  <rect x="110" y="50" width="140" height="80" rx="10" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="180" y="86" font-size="14" font-weight="700" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">pure core</text>
  <text x="180" y="106" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">data in, data out</text>
  <line x1="8" y1="150" x2="68" y2="150" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="68,150 60,146 60,154" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="36" y="140" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">input</text>
  <line x1="292" y1="150" x2="350" y2="150" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="352,150 344,146 344,154" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="322" y="140" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">output</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context functions-are-values Passing print, not calling it
You met this in the decorators lesson: a function can be stored in a variable and handed to another function. The difference is the brackets. `print` with no brackets is the function itself, the tool. `print("hi")` *uses* the tool, right now. So `run(lines, print)` hands `run` the tool, and `run` decides when to use it. `sent.append` works the same way: it is the `append` method of that one list, not yet called.
:::

::: context test-doubles Crash-test dummies for code
Car makers do not test seat belts with real people. They use crash-test dummies that record every bump. Engineers call the code version a **test double**, and there are a few kinds: a *fake* does the job in a simpler way (a list instead of an email server), a *stub* returns fixed answers, and a *mock* checks that it was called in the right way. People mix the words up; what matters is that the real thing and the stand-in can be swapped because the function was handed its tools.
:::

::: context injected-time Flight software runs on a clock you can control
A clock is an input that changes every time you look at it, so a test that reads the real clock may pass today and fail tomorrow. Spacecraft teams meet this problem at full size. Before launch, flight software is run for hours or days in simulation, where the sensors and the clock are simulated too. That only works if the software asks for the time through something that can be swapped, instead of reading the hardware clock directly. Then a test can jump to "ten seconds before engine cutoff" whenever it likes.
:::

::: context standard-streams Three streams every program gets
Every program starts with three streams already open: **standard input** (stdin), where it reads; **standard output** (stdout), where `print` writes; and **standard error** (stderr), for error messages. You met the other end in the Terminal course: a pipe such as `cat stock.txt | python3 stock.py` connects one program's stdout to the next one's stdin. In this editor, the Input box plays the part of stdin.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 120" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="4" y="40" width="86" height="40" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="47" y="64" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">Input box</text>
  <line x1="90" y1="60" x2="136" y2="60" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="140,60 132,56 132,64" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="115" y="50" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">stdin</text>
  <rect x="140" y="35" width="90" height="50" rx="6" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="185" y="64" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">your program</text>
  <line x1="230" y1="60" x2="276" y2="60" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="280,60 272,56 272,64" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="255" y="50" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1" text-anchor="middle">stdout</text>
  <rect x="280" y="40" width="76" height="40" rx="6" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="318" y="64" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">screen</text>
  <text x="185" y="108" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">input() or sys.stdin.read() in, print() out</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context dunder-name What Python puts in __name__
Before a file runs, Python sets a variable called `__name__` in it. When you run the file itself, the value is the string `"__main__"`, meaning "you are the main program". When another file imports it, for example with `import stock`, the value is the module's own name, `"stock"`, and the `if` block is skipped. Names with two underscores on each side are special names Python itself uses; programmers call them "dunder" names, short for "double underscore".
:::

::: context globals-couple Why a global is not really injected
A global variable is one box that every function in the file can reach into. If a function reads `NOTIFY` from there, a test has to change that box before calling it and remember to put it back after. Forget, and the next test quietly uses the wrong tool. Two tests running at once would fight over the same box. An argument belongs to one call only, so each caller hands over its own tool and nothing leaks between them.
:::
--- task
The stock checker works but is one tangled function. Refactor it (change its shape, not what it does) so that the **output stays exactly the same** for the given input, into:

- `parse_stock(lines)` → a list of `(name, stock, minimum)` tuples with ints, skipping blank lines.
- `needs_reorder(items)` → only the items whose stock is **below** their minimum, in order.
- `format_alert(item)` → `"REORDER widget: 3 left (minimum 10)"`.
- `run(lines, notify)` → calls `notify(message)` once per alert, then once with the summary `"2 of 5 items need reordering"`, and returns the number of alerts. It must not print anything itself.
- The entry point, under `if __name__ == "__main__":`, calls `run` with the lines of standard input and `print`.
--- starter
import sys


def main():
    count = 0
    total = 0
    for line in sys.stdin.read().splitlines():
        line = line.strip()
        if not line:
            continue
        total += 1
        name, stock, minimum = line.split()
        stock = int(stock)
        minimum = int(minimum)
        if stock < minimum:
            count += 1
            print(f"REORDER {name}: {stock} left (minimum {minimum})")
    print(f"{count} of {total} items need reordering")


main()
--- solution
import sys


def parse_stock(lines):
    items = []
    for line in lines:
        if not line.strip():
            continue
        name, stock, minimum = line.split()
        items.append((name, int(stock), int(minimum)))
    return items


def needs_reorder(items):
    return [item for item in items if item[1] < item[2]]


def format_alert(item):
    name, stock, minimum = item
    return f"REORDER {name}: {stock} left (minimum {minimum})"


def run(lines, notify):
    items = parse_stock(lines)
    low = needs_reorder(items)
    for item in low:
        notify(format_alert(item))
    notify(f"{len(low)} of {len(items)} items need reordering")
    return len(low)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    run(sys.stdin.read().splitlines(), print)
--- hint
Pull out the pure pieces first: `parse_stock` (the loop that builds tuples), `needs_reorder` (a filter), `format_alert` (the f-string). None of them prints; each one returns its result.
--- hint
`run(lines, notify)` wires them together and calls `notify(...)` where the old code called `print(...)`. The summary counts come from `len(...)` of the two lists, and `run` returns the number of alerts.
--- hint
The entry point is two lines: `if __name__ == "__main__":` and `run(sys.stdin.read().splitlines(), print)`. Delete the old `main()` call at the bottom, or the program will run twice.
--- check output | The program's output is unchanged
REORDER widget: 3 left (minimum 10)
REORDER sprocket: 0 left (minimum 1)
2 of 5 items need reordering
--- check case | parse_stock builds tuples and skips blank lines
parse_stock(["bolt 4 9", "  ", "nut 12 3"])
=> [("bolt", 4, 9), ("nut", 12, 3)]
--- check case | needs_reorder keeps only items below their minimum
needs_reorder([("a", 1, 2), ("b", 2, 2), ("c", 5, 1), ("d", 0, 3)])
=> [("a", 1, 2), ("d", 0, 3)]
--- check case | format_alert
format_alert(("washer", 3, 10))
=> "REORDER washer: 3 left (minimum 10)"
--- check case | run sends messages through the notifier it is given
(lambda sent: (run(["bolt 1 5", "nut 9 2"], sent.append), sent))([])
=> (1, ["REORDER bolt: 1 left (minimum 5)", "1 of 2 items need reordering"])
--- check case | run with nothing to report
(lambda sent: (run([], sent.append), sent))([])
=> (0, ["0 of 0 items need reordering"])
--- stdin
widget 3 10
gizmo 25 5

sprocket 0 1
doohickey 7 7
flange 40 12

+++ practice | A pure check and an injected notifier
--- task
The starter's `report` decides which temperature readings are a problem and prints them, all in one function. Split it in two:

- `classify(temp)`, a pure function: it returns `"cold"` for a reading below 0, `"hot"` for one above 40, and `"ok"` otherwise.
- `report(readings, notify)`: for every reading that is not `"ok"`, it calls `notify(message)` once, with a message like `"reading 2: -3 is cold"` (readings are numbered from 1). It returns how many messages it sent, and it must not print anything itself.

For example, `report([12, -3, 41, 20], sent.append)` returns `2` and leaves `["reading 2: -3 is cold", "reading 3: 41 is hot"]` in the list `sent`.
--- starter
def report(readings):
    count = 0
    for i, temp in enumerate(readings, start=1):
        if temp < 0:
            print(f"reading {i}: {temp} is cold")
            count += 1
        elif temp > 40:
            print(f"reading {i}: {temp} is hot")
            count += 1
    return count
--- solution
def classify(temp):
    if temp < 0:
        return "cold"
    if temp > 40:
        return "hot"
    return "ok"


def report(readings, notify):
    alerts = 0
    for i, temp in enumerate(readings, start=1):
        label = classify(temp)
        if label != "ok":
            notify(f"reading {i}: {temp} is {label}")
            alerts += 1
    return alerts
--- hint
The deciding part is the two comparisons; pull them out into `classify`, which returns a word instead of printing. Then `report` has only one message to build: the label goes into the f-string.
--- hint
`notify` is a function handed in as a parameter. Call it wherever the old code called `print`. In a test, `sent.append` stands in for it and collects the messages.
--- check case | classify, on and around both limits
[classify(t) for t in (-0.5, 0, 40, 40.5)]
=> ["cold", "ok", "ok", "hot"]
--- check case | report sends one message per problem reading
(lambda sent: (report([12, -3, 41, 20], sent.append), sent))([])
=> (2, ["reading 2: -3 is cold", "reading 3: 41 is hot"])
--- check case | No readings, no messages
(lambda sent: (report([], sent.append), sent))([])
=> (0, [])
--- check test | report prints nothing itself
(lambda buf: (lambda cm: (cm.__enter__(), report([-1, 99], lambda m: None), cm.__exit__(None, None, None), buf.getvalue())[3])(__import__("contextlib").redirect_stdout(buf)))(__import__("io").StringIO()) == ""

+++ practice | A ranking from standard input
--- task
Scores arrive on standard input, one `name score` pair per line, where the score is a whole number. Blank lines may appear and are skipped. Replace the starter with three parts:

- `parse_scores(lines)` returns a list of `(name, score)` tuples with the score as an `int`, in the order given.
- `rank(scores)` returns a list of lines like `"1. ada 91"`: highest score first, equal scores in A to Z order of name, numbered from 1.
- The entry point, under `if __name__ == "__main__":`, reads all of standard input, and prints each line of the ranking.

For the input in the Input box, the program prints:

```
1. ada 91
2. sam 91
3. lin 78
4. bo 60
```
--- starter
import sys

for line in sys.stdin.read().splitlines():
    if line.strip():
        name, score = line.split()
        print(name, score)
--- solution
import sys


def parse_scores(lines):
    scores = []
    for line in lines:
        if line.strip():
            name, score = line.split()
            scores.append((name, int(score)))
    return scores


def rank(scores):
    ordered = sorted(scores, key=lambda pair: (-pair[1], pair[0]))
    return [f"{place}. {name} {score}" for place, (name, score) in enumerate(ordered, start=1)]


if __name__ == "__main__":
    for line in rank(parse_scores(sys.stdin.read().splitlines())):
        print(line)
--- hint
Only the entry point touches standard input and the screen. `parse_scores` gets a list of lines and `rank` gets a list of tuples, so both can be tested with lists you make up.
--- hint
Sort with `key=lambda pair: (-pair[1], pair[0])`: the minus puts high scores first, and the name breaks ties. `enumerate(ordered, start=1)` numbers the places.
--- check output | The program prints the ranking
1. ada 91
2. sam 91
3. lin 78
4. bo 60
--- check case | parse_scores builds tuples and skips blank lines
parse_scores(["zed 5", "", "  ", "amy 12"])
=> [("zed", 5), ("amy", 12)]
--- check case | Equal scores go A to Z
rank([("cy", 3), ("al", 7), ("bo", 3)])
=> ["1. al 7", "2. bo 3", "3. cy 3"]
--- check case | No scores, no lines
rank([])
=> []
--- stdin
ada 91
lin 78

sam 91
bo 60

+++ practice | An alarm with a clock you control
--- task
The starter's `Alarm` reads the real clock and prints its alerts, so it can only be tested by waiting and watching the screen. Rewrite it so its tools are handed in: `Alarm(limit, cooldown, clock, notify)`.

- `clock()` returns the current time in seconds, and `notify(message)` sends an alert. `check` must call `clock()` exactly once per call, and must never print.
- `check(reading)` returns `True` and calls `notify(f"{now}: high {reading}")` when the reading is above `limit` and no alert was sent in the last `cooldown` seconds, where `now` is the time `clock()` gave. Otherwise it returns `False` and sends nothing.
- The first high reading always alerts. A reading exactly `limit` is not high. Exactly `cooldown` seconds after the last alert, a high reading alerts again.

For example, with a limit of 100 and a cooldown of 10, the readings 105, 110 and 120 at times 0, 3 and 10 give `True`, `False`, `True`, and the alerts `"0: high 105"` and `"10: high 120"`.
--- starter
import time


class Alarm:
    def __init__(self, limit, cooldown):
        self.limit = limit
        self.cooldown = cooldown
        self.last_alert = None

    def check(self, reading):
        now = time.time()
        if reading > self.limit and (self.last_alert is None or now - self.last_alert >= self.cooldown):
            self.last_alert = now
            print(f"{now}: high {reading}")
            return True
        return False
--- solution
class Alarm:
    def __init__(self, limit, cooldown, clock, notify):
        self.limit = limit
        self.cooldown = cooldown
        self.clock = clock
        self.notify = notify
        self.last_alert = None

    def check(self, reading):
        now = self.clock()
        if reading <= self.limit:
            return False
        if self.last_alert is not None and now - self.last_alert < self.cooldown:
            return False
        self.last_alert = now
        self.notify(f"{now}: high {reading}")
        return True
--- hint
Store `clock` and `notify` on the object in `__init__`, like any other value. In `check`, `self.clock()` replaces `time.time()` and `self.notify(...)` replaces `print(...)`.
--- hint
Call `self.clock()` once, at the top of `check`, before any `return`. Remember the time of the last alert; a new alert is allowed when there was none yet, or when `now - self.last_alert` is at least `cooldown`.
--- check case | Alerts, with a quiet spell after each one
(lambda sent, t: (lambda a: ([a.check(r) for r in [105, 110, 90, 120, 130]], sent))(Alarm(100, 10, lambda: next(t), sent.append)))([], iter([0, 3, 5, 10, 12]))
=> ([True, False, False, True, False], ["0: high 105", "10: high 120"])
--- check test | check calls the clock exactly once per call
(lambda calls: (lambda a: ([a.check(r) for r in [1, 200, 2]], len(calls))[1])(Alarm(100, 5, lambda: calls.append(1) or len(calls), lambda m: None)))([]) == 3
--- check test | A reading equal to the limit is not high
Alarm(100, 5, lambda: 0, lambda m: None).check(100) is False
--- check case | With no cooldown, every high reading alerts
(lambda sent: ((lambda a: [a.check(200) for _ in range(3)])(Alarm(100, 0, lambda: 7, sent.append)), len(sent)))([])
=> ([True, True, True], 3)
--- check test | Two alarms keep their own quiet spells
(lambda a, b: (a.check(150), b.check(150), a.check(150)) == (True, True, False))(Alarm(100, 60, lambda: 0, lambda m: None), Alarm(100, 60, lambda: 0, lambda m: None))
--- check test | check prints nothing
(lambda buf: (lambda cm: (cm.__enter__(), Alarm(1, 1, lambda: 0, lambda m: None).check(5), cm.__exit__(None, None, None), buf.getvalue())[3])(__import__("contextlib").redirect_stdout(buf)))(__import__("io").StringIO()) == ""

+++ practice | A settings parser with sharp edges
--- task
Write a pure function `parse_config(lines)` that turns lines of settings into a dict of strings. The starter is the obvious version; it breaks on most of these rules:

- Each line is `key = value`. Strip the spaces around the key and around the value.
- Skip blank lines, and lines whose first character that is not a space is `#`.
- Cut at the **first** `=` only, because a value may contain `=`: `url = a=b` gives the value `"a=b"`.
- An empty value is allowed: `debug =` gives `""`.
- A key that appears again replaces the earlier value.
- A line with no `=`, or with nothing before it (like `= 5`), raises `ValueError` with the message `"line N: expected key = value"`, where `N` is the line's number, counting from 1 and counting blank and comment lines too.
--- starter
def parse_config(lines):
    config = {}
    for line in lines:
        key, value = line.split("=")
        config[key.strip()] = value.strip()
    return config
--- solution
def parse_config(lines):
    config = {}
    for number, line in enumerate(lines, start=1):
        text = line.strip()
        if not text or text.startswith("#"):
            continue
        key, sep, value = text.partition("=")
        key = key.strip()
        if not sep or not key:
            raise ValueError(f"line {number}: expected key = value")
        config[key] = value.strip()
    return config
--- hint
`partition("=")` from the strings lesson cuts at the first `=` only and always gives three pieces. The middle piece is `""` when there is no `=` at all, which is how you spot a bad line.
--- hint
Number the lines with `enumerate(lines, start=1)`, and strip each line before testing for blank or `#`. Raise the error when the separator or the stripped key is empty.
--- check case | Keys and values are stripped
parse_config(["name = Orbiter", "  mass=1200 "])
=> {"name": "Orbiter", "mass": "1200"}
--- check case | Blank lines and comments are skipped
parse_config(["", "# a comment", "   # an indented comment", "mode = auto"])
=> {"mode": "auto"}
--- check case | Only the first = cuts
parse_config(["url = a=b=c"])
=> {"url": "a=b=c"}
--- check case | A repeated key keeps the last value, and an empty value is fine
parse_config(["x = 1", "debug =", "x = 2"])
=> {"x": "2", "debug": ""}
--- check case | The error names the line, counting every line from 1
(lambda g: (exec("try:\n    parse_config(['a = 1', '', 'oops'])\nexcept ValueError as e:\n    msg = str(e)", g), g["msg"])[1])(dict(globals()))
=> "line 3: expected key = value"
--- check test | A line with no key is refused
raises(ValueError, lambda: parse_config(["= 5"]))
--- check case | No lines
parse_config([])
=> {}

+++ practice | Debug: every line at the same time
--- task
`log_line(message, now=time.time())` was meant to stamp each message with the time it was logged, like `"1759140000.5 engine start"`. But every line gets the same time: the moment the program started.

Find out why, and fix it by handing the clock in instead of a time: rewrite it as `log_line(message, clock=time.time)`, which calls `clock()` every time it runs and returns `f"{clock()} {message}"`. A test can then pass a fake clock, such as `lambda: 100.0`.
--- starter
import time


def log_line(message, now=time.time()):
    return f"{now} {message}"
--- solution
import time


def log_line(message, clock=time.time):
    return f"{clock()} {message}"
--- hint
When is a default value worked out? Think back to the mutable default argument, and to the default-argument fix for late binding in the first lesson of this course.
--- hint
`time.time()` with brackets is a number, worked out once when the `def` runs. `time.time` without brackets is the function itself. Make that the default, and call `clock()` inside the function.
--- check case | A fake clock sets the time
log_line("engine start", clock=lambda: 100.0)
=> "100.0 engine start"
--- check test | The clock is asked again on every call
(lambda t: [log_line("x", clock=lambda: next(t)) for _ in range(3)])(iter([1, 2, 3])) == ["1 x", "2 x", "3 x"]
--- check test | The default is the real clock, read when the line is logged
(lambda before: before - 1 <= float(log_line("hi").split()[0]) <= __import__("time").time() + 1)(__import__("time").time()) and __import__("inspect").signature(log_line).parameters["clock"].default is __import__("time").time

+++ practice | A fuel log: pure core, thin shell
--- task
A small program looks after a fuel tank, which starts empty. Commands arrive on standard input, one per line:

- `fill N` adds `N` litres, and `burn N` uses `N` litres, where `N` is a whole number written with digits only. A `burn` of more than is in the tank is refused with the message `error: only X left`, and nothing changes.
- `report` gives the message `fuel: X`.
- Blank lines are skipped. Any other line gives the message `error: unknown command <the line, stripped>`, and nothing changes.

Write three parts:

- `step(fuel, line)`, a pure function. It returns a tuple `(new_fuel, message)`, where `message` is `None` when there is nothing to say (a fill, a burn that worked, or a blank line).
- `run(lines, out)` starts at 0 litres, goes through the lines with `step`, calls `out(message)` for every message that is not `None`, and returns the fuel left at the end.
- The entry point, under `if __name__ == "__main__":`, which calls `run` with the lines of standard input and `print`.

For the input in the Input box, the program prints:

```
fuel: 30
error: only 30 left
error: unknown command fly
fuel: 35
```
--- starter
import sys

fuel = 0
for line in sys.stdin.read().splitlines():
    words = line.split()
    if words and words[0] == "fill":
        fuel += int(words[1])
    elif words and words[0] == "burn":
        fuel -= int(words[1])
    elif words and words[0] == "report":
        print(f"fuel: {fuel}")
--- solution
import sys


def step(fuel, line):
    words = line.split()
    if not words:
        return fuel, None
    if words == ["report"]:
        return fuel, f"fuel: {fuel}"
    if len(words) == 2 and words[0] in ("fill", "burn") and words[1].isdigit():
        amount = int(words[1])
        if words[0] == "fill":
            return fuel + amount, None
        if amount > fuel:
            return fuel, f"error: only {fuel} left"
        return fuel - amount, None
    return fuel, f"error: unknown command {line.strip()}"


def run(lines, out):
    fuel = 0
    for line in lines:
        fuel, message = step(fuel, line)
        if message is not None:
            out(message)
    return fuel


if __name__ == "__main__":
    run(sys.stdin.read().splitlines(), print)
--- hint
`step` never changes anything outside itself: it gets the fuel in, and hands back the new fuel together with what to say. Returning the old fuel is how "nothing changes" is written.
--- hint
Split the line into words. Handle the blank line first, then `["report"]`, then two words where the first is `fill` or `burn` and the second `.isdigit()`. Everything that falls through is an unknown command. `run` unpacks `fuel, message = step(fuel, line)` each time round the loop.
--- check output | The program's messages
fuel: 30
error: only 30 left
error: unknown command fly
fuel: 35
--- check case | A fill says nothing
step(10, "fill 5")
=> (15, None)
--- check case | Burning more than there is is refused
step(10, "burn 11")
=> (10, "error: only 10 left")
--- check case | Burning exactly what there is works
step(10, "burn 10")
=> (0, None)
--- check case | A burn without a number is an unknown command
step(3, "burn x")
=> (3, "error: unknown command burn x")
--- check case | A blank line
step(3, "   ")
=> (3, None)
--- check case | run sends its messages through out and returns the fuel
(lambda sent: (run(["fill 7", "report", "report now"], sent.append), sent))([])
=> (7, ["fuel: 7", "error: unknown command report now"])
--- stdin

report

=== py4-10 | Debugging: floating-point surprises
--- teach
Last lesson you pulled logic out into pure functions so it could be tested. Sometimes a test of a pure function fails and the numbers themselves look impossible. This lesson is about that kind of bug.

Try these, and it looks like Python is broken:

```python
0.1 + 0.2            # 0.30000000000000004
0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3     # False
round(2.675, 2)      # 2.67, not 2.68
```

It is not a bug. It is how floats work in every language.

### Why it happens

Write 1/3 as a decimal and you get 0.3333… forever. You have to stop somewhere, so what you write down is a tiny bit wrong. Computers have the same problem, in a different place.

A `float` stores numbers in **[[binary|binary-fractions]]**: base two, using only the digits 0 and 1. Most decimal fractions, including 0.1, have no exact binary form, the same way 1/3 has no exact decimal form. So each float is **the [[nearest representable value|nearest-float]]**: the closest number the computer *can* store. Tiny errors appear, and they grow when you add many of them up. `2.675` is actually stored as `2.67499999...`, which is why it rounds down.

### Diagnose it

When a comparison of computed numbers fails "impossibly", look at more digits. Print with `repr()` (the exact form Python would type back to you) or with many digits, `f"{x:.20f}"`, where `.20f` means "20 digits after the point":

```python
print(f"{2.675:.20f}")     # 2.67499999999999982236
print(repr(0.1 + 0.2))     # 0.30000000000000004
```

If you see `...0000004` or `...9999998` at the end, it is floating point. This matters for real: a [[tiny error in a tenth of a second|patriot-clock]] has cost lives.

### Fix 1: measurements, compare with a tolerance

For **measurements and science** (lengths, sensor readings, physics), floats are the right tool. But never compare them with `==`. Compare with a **[[tolerance|tolerance]]**: an amount of difference you agree to ignore.

`math.isclose(a, b)` does that. By default it uses a **relative tolerance** of `1e-9` (read "one times ten to the minus nine", 0.000000001): the two numbers may differ by up to a billionth of their size.

```python
import math
math.isclose(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3)                       # True (relative tolerance 1e-9)
math.isclose(1e-12, 0.0)                           # False
math.isclose(1e-12, 0.0, abs_tol=1e-9)             # True: near zero you need abs_tol
```

A billionth of zero is zero. So a relative tolerance alone never considers anything "close" to exactly 0. When zero is a possible answer, also give an **absolute tolerance**, `abs_tol=`: a fixed amount of difference that is always allowed. You can set the relative one yourself too, with `rel_tol=`. The numbers count as close if they pass either one.

### Fix 2: money, use Decimal

For **money**, never use floats. A bank balance that is off by a millionth of a cent will, sooner or later, round the wrong way. Use `decimal.Decimal` instead, a number type that works in [[base ten, like people do|decimal-module]]. Create it **from strings**:

```python
from decimal import Decimal
Decimal("0.10") + Decimal("0.20") == Decimal("0.30")    # True, exactly
Decimal(0.1)      # Decimal('0.1000000000000000055511151231257827...')  the float's error, preserved
```

The second line is the trap. `0.1` without quotes is already a float, with its error baked in, and `Decimal` copies it faithfully. The string `"0.1"` never went through a float.

One more thing you will need. `sum()` starts adding from the int `0`. That is fine for a list of Decimals, but an **empty** list gives back the int `0`, not a Decimal. `sum` takes a second argument, the starting value, to fix that:

```python
sum([Decimal("1.5"), Decimal("2")])      # Decimal('3.5')
sum([], Decimal("0"))                    # Decimal('0'), not the int 0
```

The other way to handle money is to work in **[[whole cents|integer-cents]]**, as an `int`: 10 dollars is `1000`. Ints are always exact.

### Fix 3: exact fractions

For **exact fractions** (ratios, probabilities), `fractions.Fraction` keeps the top and bottom as whole numbers, so nothing is ever rounded. `Fraction(1, 3) * 3 == 1` is exactly `True`.

### Splitting money

**Splitting money** has its own trap. 10.00 split three ways is not three times 3.33, because 3.33 × 3 = 9.99 and a cent disappears. The fix, step by step:

1. Work in cents: 10.00 is `1000` cents.
2. Give everyone the rounded-down share, and find the leftover cents.
3. Hand out the leftover cents one each, to the first people.

For step 2, `divmod(a, b)` does whole-number division and the remainder in one go. It gives back both as a pair, the same as `(a // b, a % b)`:

```python
divmod(1000, 3)      # (333, 1): 333 cents each, 1 cent left over
```

So the shares are 334, 333 and 333 cents: the first person gets the spare cent. To turn cents back into money, divide a Decimal by 100: `Decimal(334) / 100` is `Decimal('3.34')`. The shares add up to exactly 10.00.

**Watch out:** `float(total)` or `Decimal(0.1)` anywhere in money code lets the float error back in. Every amount should start life as a string (`Decimal("0.10")`) or as whole cents.

::: context binary-fractions Why 0.1 never ends in binary
In decimal, the places after the point are tenths, hundredths, thousandths. In binary they are halves, quarters, eighths, sixteenths, and so on. A number has an exact binary form only if it can be built from a *finite* sum of those. 0.5 is one half, 0.75 is a half plus a quarter. But 0.1 written in binary is 0.000110011001100… with the "0011" repeating forever, just as 1/3 is 0.333… in decimal. The computer stops after a fixed number of binary digits, so the stored value is a hair off.
:::

::: context nearest-float Floats are dots on a line, with gaps between
A Python float uses 64 bits, and 53 of them hold the digits, which gives about 15 to 17 significant decimal digits. So the numbers a float can hold are like dots on a number line with small gaps between them. Any real number that falls in a gap is stored as the nearest dot. The gaps grow with the size of the number: near 10^20 they are 16,384 wide, which is why `1e20 + 1 == 1e20` is `True`. (The picture is not to scale.)

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 110" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <line x1="12" y1="60" x2="348" y2="60" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <g fill="#1d6fd1">
    <circle cx="40" cy="60" r="5"/><circle cx="110" cy="60" r="5"/><circle cx="180" cy="60" r="5"/>
    <circle cx="250" cy="60" r="5"/><circle cx="320" cy="60" r="5"/>
  </g>
  <line x1="196" y1="30" x2="196" y2="60" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="200" y="24" font-size="12" fill="#b4232c" text-anchor="start">0.1 exactly (not a dot)</text>
  <path d="M 194 72 Q 188 84 182 72" fill="none" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="1.5"/>
  <polygon points="182,70 179,78 186,76" fill="#1f2a44"/>
  <text x="180" y="100" font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">stored as the nearest dot</text>
  <text x="40" y="44" font-size="11" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">floats</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context patriot-clock A tenth of a second that mattered
In 1991, during the Gulf War, a Patriot missile battery at Dhahran, Saudi Arabia, failed to stop an incoming Scud missile, and 28 American soldiers were killed. Its computer counted time in tenths of a second and multiplied by 0.1 to get seconds, but 0.1 was stored in 24 binary digits, so every tick was off by about 0.000000095 seconds. After about 100 hours of running, the clock was off by about 0.34 seconds. A Scud travels more than half a kilometer in that time, so the system looked in the wrong place.
:::

::: context tolerance Engineers never ask for exactly
No machined part is exactly 50 mm long. A drawing says "50 mm ± 0.05 mm", and any part inside that range passes. That range is the tolerance. Comparing floats works the same way: you decide how close counts as the same. A relative tolerance scales with the numbers, like "within 1 percent", which suits values from millimeters to kilometers. An absolute tolerance is a fixed amount, like "within 0.05 mm", which is what you need near zero.
:::

::: context decimal-module A calculator that counts in tens
The `decimal` module does arithmetic the way you learned at school, digit by digit in base ten. So `Decimal("0.10")` really is ten hundredths, and adding money gives exactly what a person with a pencil would get. It still has a limit, 28 significant digits by default, so `Decimal(1) / 3` is rounded too. But for money, where every amount is a whole number of cents, it never needs to round. It is slower than float, which is why science code sticks with floats.
:::

::: context integer-cents Many payment systems count in cents
Counting in the smallest unit is how a lot of real money software works. Stripe's payment API, for example, takes amounts as whole numbers in the currency's smallest unit, so 10.00 US dollars is sent as `1000`. An `int` in Python is always exact, however big, so adding and subtracting cents can never drift. You only turn cents into dollars and cents at the very end, when you show them to a person.
:::
--- task
**Bug report** from the finance team:

1. `is_settled(["0.10", "0.20", "-0.30"])` says `False`, but those transactions cancel out. `balance` must return the exact total as a `Decimal`.
2. `split("10.00", 3)` returns three equal shares that add up to 9.99. It must return a list of `Decimal` amounts in cents that add up **exactly** to the total, with any leftover cents going one each to the first people: `[Decimal("3.34"), Decimal("3.33"), Decimal("3.33")]`.
3. `same_reading(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3)` says two sensor readings differ. Readings count as the same if they are within a relative tolerance of `1e-9` or an absolute tolerance of `1e-9`.
--- starter
def balance(amounts):
    return sum(float(a) for a in amounts)


def is_settled(amounts):
    return balance(amounts) == 0


def split(total, people):
    share = round(float(total) / people, 2)
    return [share] * people


def same_reading(a, b):
    return a == b
--- solution
import math
from decimal import Decimal


def balance(amounts):
    return sum((Decimal(a) for a in amounts), Decimal("0"))


def is_settled(amounts):
    return balance(amounts) == 0


def split(total, people):
    cents = int(Decimal(total) * 100)
    base, extra = divmod(cents, people)
    return [Decimal(base + (1 if i < extra else 0)) / 100 for i in range(people)]


def same_reading(a, b):
    return math.isclose(a, b, rel_tol=1e-9, abs_tol=1e-9)
--- hint
Print `repr(balance(["0.10", "0.20", "-0.30"]))`. Then build each amount with `Decimal(a)` from the string, never from a float.
--- hint
`sum` starts from the int `0`; give it a `Decimal("0")` start so the result is always a `Decimal`, even for no amounts. For `same_reading`, use `math.isclose` with both `rel_tol=` and `abs_tol=`.
--- hint
For `split`, turn the total into whole cents with `int(Decimal(total) * 100)`. `divmod(cents, people)` gives each person's base share and the leftover cents; give one extra cent to each of the first `extra` people, then divide by 100 as a `Decimal`.
--- check case | balance is exact
balance(["0.10", "0.20"])
=> __import__("decimal").Decimal("0.30")
--- check test | balance returns a Decimal, even for no amounts
type(balance(["1.5"])).__name__ == "Decimal" and balance([]) == 0 and type(balance([])).__name__ == "Decimal"
--- check test | Transactions that cancel out are settled
is_settled(["0.10", "0.20", "-0.30"]) and is_settled([]) and not is_settled(["0.01"])
--- check case | split gives leftover cents to the first people
split("10.00", 3)
=> [__import__("decimal").Decimal("3.34"), __import__("decimal").Decimal("3.33"), __import__("decimal").Decimal("3.33")]
--- check test | split always adds up exactly
all(sum(split(t, n)) == __import__("decimal").Decimal(t) for t in ("100.00", "0.05", "19.99", "7.00") for n in (1, 2, 3, 7))
--- check test | same_reading uses a tolerance
same_reading(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3) and not same_reading(1.0, 1.001) and same_reading(1e-12, 0.0) and same_reading(1e20, 1e20 + 1)

+++ practice | An exact shopping total
--- task
Prices arrive as strings, like `"2.10"`. Write two functions:

- `cart_total(prices)` returns the exact total as a `Decimal`, and `Decimal("0")` for an empty list. Build each `Decimal` from its string.
- `can_afford(prices, budget)` returns `True` if the total is at most `budget`, which is a string too, and `False` otherwise.

With floats, `can_afford(["0.10", "0.20"], "0.30")` says `False`, because the float total is `0.30000000000000004`. Yours must say `True`.
--- starter
def cart_total(prices):
    return sum(float(p) for p in prices)


def can_afford(prices, budget):
    return cart_total(prices) <= float(budget)
--- solution
from decimal import Decimal


def cart_total(prices):
    return sum((Decimal(p) for p in prices), Decimal("0"))


def can_afford(prices, budget):
    return cart_total(prices) <= Decimal(budget)
--- hint
Every `float(...)` lets the float error in. Make every amount, the budget included, a `Decimal` built straight from its string.
--- hint
`sum` starts from the int `0`, so an empty list would give an int. Give it a second argument, `Decimal("0")`, to start from. Put the generator expression in its own brackets when it is not the only argument.
--- check case | The total is exact
