--- check test | Exactly on budget is affordable
can_afford(["0.10", "0.20"], "0.30") is True
--- check test | A cent over is not, and an empty cart always is
can_afford(["19.99", "0.02"], "20.00") is False and can_afford([], "0") is True

+++ practice | Exact chances with fractions
--- task
Each part of a mission works with some chance, given as a pair `(top, bottom)` that means top divided by bottom: `(9, 10)` is a 9 in 10 chance. Write two functions that give exact answers as `Fraction`s:

- `chance_all(parts)`: the chance that **every** part works, which is all the chances multiplied together. With no parts it is `Fraction(1)`, the whole number 1 as a fraction.
- `chance_any(parts)`: the chance that **at least one** part works, which is 1 minus the chance that none of them does. The chance that one part fails is 1 minus its chance of working. With no parts it is `Fraction(0)`.

Build each chance with `Fraction(top, bottom)`. For example, `chance_all([(1, 2), (2, 3)])` is `Fraction(1, 3)`, and `chance_any([(1, 2), (1, 2)])` is `Fraction(3, 4)`.
--- starter
def chance_all(parts):
    result = 1.0
    for top, bottom in parts:
        result *= top / bottom
    return result


def chance_any(parts):
    return 1.0
--- solution
from fractions import Fraction


def chance_all(parts):
    result = Fraction(1)
    for top, bottom in parts:
        result *= Fraction(top, bottom)
    return result


def chance_any(parts):
    none_work = Fraction(1)
    for top, bottom in parts:
        none_work *= 1 - Fraction(top, bottom)
    return 1 - none_work
--- hint
Start from `Fraction(1)` instead of `1.0`, and multiply by `Fraction(top, bottom)` each time. Fractions keep the top and bottom as whole numbers, so nothing is ever rounded.
--- hint
For `chance_any`, multiply together the chances that each part fails, `1 - Fraction(top, bottom)`, starting from `Fraction(1)`. The answer is 1 minus that product. With no parts the product stays 1, and the answer is 0.
--- check case | Every part works
chance_all([(1, 2), (2, 3)])
=> __import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 3)
--- check case | At least one part works
chance_any([(1, 2), (1, 2)])
=> __import__("fractions").Fraction(3, 4)
--- check test | No parts
chance_all([]) == 1 and chance_any([]) == 0 and type(chance_all([])).__name__ == "Fraction" and type(chance_any([])).__name__ == "Fraction"
--- check test | Tenths stay exact
chance_all([(1, 10)] * 3) == __import__("fractions").Fraction(1, 1000) and chance_any([(1, 10)] * 10) == __import__("fractions").Fraction(6513215599, 10000000000)

+++ practice | Totals by category
--- task
Bank transactions are `(category, amount)` pairs, with the amount as a string like `"-12.50"` (negative means money spent). Write two functions:

- `totals_by_category(transactions)` returns a dict from each category to its exact total as a `Decimal`, with the categories in A to Z order.
- `biggest_expense(transactions)` returns the category with the lowest (most negative) total. If two tie, return the one that comes first from A to Z. If no category's total is below zero, return `None`.

For example, `[("food", "-4.10"), ("pay", "100.00"), ("food", "-0.20"), ("fuel", "-30.30")]` gives the totals `{"food": Decimal("-4.30"), "fuel": Decimal("-30.30"), "pay": Decimal("100.00")}`, and the biggest expense is `"fuel"`.
--- starter
def totals_by_category(transactions):
    totals = {}
    for category, amount in transactions:
        totals[category] = totals.get(category, 0) + float(amount)
    return totals


def biggest_expense(transactions):
    totals = totals_by_category(transactions)
    return min(totals, key=totals.get)
--- solution
from collections import defaultdict
from decimal import Decimal


def totals_by_category(transactions):
    totals = defaultdict(lambda: Decimal("0"))
    for category, amount in transactions:
        totals[category] += Decimal(amount)
    return {category: totals[category] for category in sorted(totals)}


def biggest_expense(transactions):
    totals = totals_by_category(transactions)
    spent = [(total, category) for category, total in totals.items() if total < 0]
    if not spent:
        return None
    return min(spent)[1]
--- hint
Group with a `defaultdict` whose default is `Decimal("0")` (its factory can be `lambda: Decimal("0")`), and add `Decimal(amount)` for each transaction. A dict comprehension over `sorted(totals)` puts the keys in A to Z order.
--- hint
For `biggest_expense`, make `(total, category)` pairs for the negative totals only. `min` compares the totals first and the names second, which breaks ties from A to Z. With no pairs at all, return `None`.
--- check case | Exact totals per category
totals_by_category([("food", "-4.10"), ("pay", "100.00"), ("food", "-0.20"), ("fuel", "-30.30")])
=> {"food": __import__("decimal").Decimal("-4.30"), "fuel": __import__("decimal").Decimal("-30.30"), "pay": __import__("decimal").Decimal("100.00")}
--- check test | Categories come in A to Z order, and tenths add up exactly
list(totals_by_category([("z", "1"), ("a", "1"), ("m", "1")])) == ["a", "m", "z"] and totals_by_category([("a", "0.1")] * 3)["a"] == __import__("decimal").Decimal("0.3")
--- check case | The biggest expense
biggest_expense([("food", "-4.10"), ("pay", "100.00"), ("food", "-0.20"), ("fuel", "-30.30")])
=> "fuel"
--- check case | No spending at all
biggest_expense([("pay", "5.00")])
=> None
--- check case | A tie goes to the first name from A to Z
biggest_expense([("b", "-1.00"), ("a", "-0.50"), ("a", "-0.50")])
=> "a"
--- check case | No transactions
totals_by_category([])
=> {}

+++ practice | Cents to text and back
--- task
Money is stored as a whole number of cents. Write two functions, and get every edge case right:

- `to_cents(text)` turns text like `"12.34"`, `"-0.05"`, `"7"` or `"3.5"` into an `int` of cents: `1234`, `-5`, `700` and `350`. It must be exact: `"19.99"` is `1999` (with floats, `19.99 * 100` is `1998.9999999999998`). Text with more than two decimal places, like `"1.234"`, raises `ValueError`.
- `format_cents(cents)` turns cents back into text with exactly two digits after the point: `1234` gives `"12.34"`, `5` gives `"0.05"`, `0` gives `"0.00"`, `-5` gives `"-0.05"`, `-150` gives `"-1.50"`, and `100000` gives `"1000.00"`.

Watch the negative numbers: `divmod(-5, 100)` is `(-1, 95)`, not `(0, -5)`.
--- starter
def to_cents(text):
    return int(float(text) * 100)


def format_cents(cents):
    whole, part = divmod(cents, 100)
    return f"{whole}.{part}"
--- solution
from decimal import Decimal


def to_cents(text):
    cents = Decimal(text) * 100
    if cents != int(cents):
        raise ValueError(f"{text!r} has more than two decimal places")
    return int(cents)


def format_cents(cents):
    sign = "-" if cents < 0 else ""
    whole, part = divmod(abs(cents), 100)
    return f"{sign}{whole}.{part:02d}"
--- hint
In `to_cents`, `Decimal(text) * 100` is exact. If it is not a whole number, there were too many decimal places; `int(...)` of it would quietly drop them, so compare the two first.
--- hint
In `format_cents`, deal with the sign on its own: remember whether `cents` is negative, then use `divmod(abs(cents), 100)`. The format spec `:02d` pads the cents to two digits.
--- check case | Text to cents
[to_cents(t) for t in ["12.34", "-0.05", "7", "3.5", "19.99"]]
=> [1234, -5, 700, 350, 1999]
--- check test | The result is an int, and three decimal places are refused
type(to_cents("1.10")) is int and raises(ValueError, lambda: to_cents("1.234"))
--- check case | Cents to text, negatives included
[format_cents(c) for c in [1234, 5, 0, -5, -150, 100000]]
=> ["12.34", "0.05", "0.00", "-0.05", "-1.50", "1000.00"]
--- check test | There and back again
all(to_cents(format_cents(c)) == c for c in range(-1000, 1000, 7))

+++ practice | Debug: never quite at rest
--- task
`at_rest(velocity)` should say whether a probe has stopped. Its velocity sensor is noisy, so a reading counts as zero when it is within `1e-6` of 0, on either side, `1e-6` itself included.

The code uses `math.isclose(velocity, 0.0)`, and it only ever says `True` for a reading of exactly `0.0`: `at_rest(1e-9)` gives `False`. Find out why, and fix it.
--- starter
import math


def at_rest(velocity):
    return math.isclose(velocity, 0.0)
--- solution
import math


def at_rest(velocity):
    return math.isclose(velocity, 0.0, abs_tol=1e-6)
--- hint
With only a relative tolerance, how close to 0.0 does a number have to be? A billionth of zero is zero.
--- hint
Near zero you need an absolute tolerance: pass `abs_tol=1e-6` to `math.isclose`.
--- check test | Tiny readings count as zero, on either side
at_rest(1e-9) is True and at_rest(-5e-7) is True and at_rest(0.0) is True
--- check test | Exactly 1e-6 still counts
at_rest(1e-6) is True and at_rest(-1e-6) is True
--- check test | Real movement does not
at_rest(2e-6) is False and at_rest(-0.01) is False and at_rest(3.0) is False

+++ practice | Settling up a crew's expenses
--- task
A crew shares its costs. Each expense is `(payer, amount)`, with the amount as a string like `"10.00"`, and every expense is shared equally by everybody in the list `people`, the payer included.

Write `settle(expenses, people)`. It returns a dict from each person, in the order of `people`, to their balance as a `Decimal`: what they paid, minus their share of every expense. Work in whole cents: split each expense with `divmod`, and give the leftover cents one each to the first people in `people`. That way the balances always add up to exactly zero.

For example, `settle([("ana", "10.00")], ["ana", "ben", "cy"])` gives `{"ana": Decimal("6.66"), "ben": Decimal("-3.33"), "cy": Decimal("-3.33")}`: the shares are 3.34, 3.33 and 3.33, and Ana paid the 10.00.
--- starter
def settle(expenses, people):
    balance = {person: 0.0 for person in people}
    for payer, amount in expenses:
        share = round(float(amount) / len(people), 2)
        balance[payer] += float(amount)
        for person in people:
            balance[person] -= share
    return balance
--- solution
from decimal import Decimal


def settle(expenses, people):
    balance = {person: 0 for person in people}
    for payer, amount in expenses:
        cents = int(Decimal(amount) * 100)
        balance[payer] += cents
        share, extra = divmod(cents, len(people))
        for i, person in enumerate(people):
            balance[person] -= share + (1 if i < extra else 0)
    return {person: Decimal(cents) / 100 for person, cents in balance.items()}
--- hint
Keep every balance in whole cents, as an `int`, until the very end. For each expense: turn the amount into cents with `Decimal`, add it to the payer, and take each person's share away from them.
--- hint
`share, extra = divmod(cents, len(people))`: the person at position `i` pays `share + 1` if `i < extra`, and `share` otherwise. At the end, turn each balance into money with `Decimal(cents) / 100`.
--- check case | One expense shared by three
settle([("ana", "10.00")], ["ana", "ben", "cy"])
=> {"ana": __import__("decimal").Decimal("6.66"), "ben": __import__("decimal").Decimal("-3.33"), "cy": __import__("decimal").Decimal("-3.33")}
--- check case | Two payers
settle([("ben", "0.10"), ("ana", "0.20")], ["ana", "ben"])
=> {"ana": __import__("decimal").Decimal("0.05"), "ben": __import__("decimal").Decimal("-0.05")}
--- check test | The balances always add up to exactly zero
sum(settle([("ana", "10.00"), ("ben", "0.05"), ("cy", "7.77")], ["ana", "ben", "cy", "dee"]).values()) == 0 and all(type(v).__name__ == "Decimal" for v in settle([("ana", "1.00")], ["ana", "ben", "cy"]).values())
--- check test | No expenses, in the order of people
settle([], ["zed", "amy"]) == {"zed": 0, "amy": 0} and list(settle([], ["zed", "amy"])) == ["zed", "amy"]
--- check test | Someone alone owes nothing
settle([("solo", "3.30")], ["solo"]) == {"solo": 0}

=== py4-11 | Problem solving: edit distance
--- teach
Last lesson was about numbers that are almost equal. This one asks the same question about words: how different are two strings?

Spell checkers, [[DNA comparison|diff-and-dna]] and `diff` all need a precise answer. When you type "teh", a spell checker suggests "the" because it is only a small change away. To make "small change" exact, count the edits.

### The edit distance

The **edit distance** (also called the **[[Levenshtein distance|levenshtein]]**) is the fewest single-character edits that turn one string into the other. Three kinds of edit are allowed:

- an **insertion**: add one character;
- a **deletion**: remove one character;
- a **substitution**: swap one character for another.

`kitten` → `sitting` takes 3:

```
kitten
sitten     substitute k -> s
sittin     substitute e -> i
sitting    insert g at the end
```

No way does it in 2, so the distance is 3.

### Restate it as smaller problems

Like the coin problems in the dynamic programming lesson, the trick is to answer the big question using answers to smaller ones.

Let `d(i, j)` be the distance between the first `i` characters of `a` and the first `j` characters of `b`. Those beginnings are called **[[prefixes|prefix]]**: `a[:i]` and `b[:j]`. The answer to the whole problem is `d(len(a), len(b))`.

Now look at the last character of each prefix, `a[i-1]` and `b[j-1]` (the `- 1` is there because indexes start at 0):

- If they are **equal**, they cost nothing. Leave them alone and solve the rest: `d(i, j) = d(i-1, j-1)`.
- Otherwise, the last step was one of the three edits. Try all three and take the cheapest:
  - delete `a[i-1]`: `d(i-1, j) + 1`
  - insert `b[j-1]`: `d(i, j-1) + 1`
  - substitute: `d(i-1, j-1) + 1`

**Base cases.** Turning a prefix of length `i` into the empty string takes `i` deletions, so `d(i, 0) = i`. Turning the empty string into `b[:j]` takes `j` insertions, so `d(0, j) = j`.

### Why not plain recursion?

You could write `d` as a recursive function exactly as above, and it would be correct. But each call branches three ways, and the same `(i, j)` pairs come up again and again: the work grows [[exponentially|exponential-blowup]]. Two 10-letter strings with no letters in common already take over 3.7 million calls.

Memoisation (`@lru_cache`) fixes the repeats. But the recursion gets as deep as `len(a) + len(b)` calls, past [[Python's limit|recursion-limit]] for long strings.

### Fill a table, bottom-up

So fill a table **bottom-up**, row by row, with loops. Row `i` is about `a[:i]`, column `j` is about `b[:j]`, and each cell holds `d(i, j)`. Here is the whole table for `kitten` and `sitting`:

```
        ""  s  i  t  t  i  n  g
    ""   0  1  2  3  4  5  6  7
    k    1  1  2  3  4  5  6  7
    i    2  2  1  2  3  4  5  6
    t    3  3  2  1  2  3  4  5
    t    4  4  3  2  1  2  3  4
    e    5  5  4  3  2  2  3  4
    n    6  6  5  4  3  3  2  3
```

The top row and left column are the base cases: 0, 1, 2, 3… The answer is the bottom-right cell, 3.

Take one cell to see the rule work. Row `i`, column `s` (is `"ki"` → `"s"`): `i` and `s` differ, so it is 1 + the smallest of above (1), left (2) and diagonal (1), which is 2. Next cell, row `i`, column `i` (is `"ki"` → `"si"`): the letters match, so copy the diagonal, 1.

### Keep only one row

Each cell needs only [[three neighbors|three-neighbours]]: the cell to its left, the one above, and the one diagonally above-left. So you do not need the whole table. Keep only **the previous row**, and build the current row from it. That cuts memory from `len(a) × len(b)` to `len(b)`.

The time is still one step per cell: `len(a) × len(b)` cells, which is O(n·m) time, where n and m are the two lengths. That is fine for strings of a thousand or so characters each, about a million cells.

**Watch out:** an off-by-one between string indices and table indices. The table has one more row and column than the strings have letters, for the empty prefix. Row `i` of the table is about `a[:i]`, so the character it adds is `a[i - 1]`, not `a[i]`.

::: context diff-and-dna Where the same table shows up
The `git diff` you met in the Git courses finds the fewest line insertions and deletions that turn one version of a file into another. Its default method, Myers' algorithm from 1986, solves a close cousin of this problem with lines in place of letters. Biologists line up DNA the same way: the Needleman–Wunsch method from 1970 fills this kind of table, with scores for matches, mismatches and gaps, to compare genes letter by letter.
:::

::: context levenshtein Named after a Soviet mathematician
Vladimir Levenshtein described this distance in 1965, while working on codes that correct errors in messages sent as strings of 0s and 1s. If noise can drop, add or flip a symbol, you want to know how many of those slips separate two messages. The same count turned out to be useful for words, DNA and files, so the name stuck everywhere. You will also see plain "edit distance", which is a family name: some versions allow only insertions and deletions, or also count swapping two neighboring letters as one edit.
:::

::: context prefix Prefixes, and why the empty one counts
A prefix is a beginning of a string. The prefixes of `"cat"` are `""`, `"c"`, `"ca"` and `"cat"`: four of them for three letters, because the empty string counts too. In Python, `a[:i]` is the prefix of length `i`. The empty prefix is what makes the base cases work. It is the starting point every edit sequence can be measured from, the same way `best[0] = 0` started the coin table.
:::

::: context exponential-blowup Three branches per call adds up fast
Each call that does not match makes three more calls, and each of those makes three more. So the number of calls can grow like 3 × 3 × 3 … once per level, which is exponential growth: each extra letter multiplies the work instead of adding to it. Counting them for two 10-letter strings that share no letters gives 3,716,073 calls, most of them repeats of the same `(i, j)` pair. The table has only 11 × 11 = 121 cells, so the repeats are almost all of the work.
:::

::: context recursion-limit Python stops deep recursion on purpose
Every function call that has not returned yet takes a slot on Python's **call stack**. To stop a runaway recursion from eating all the memory, Python raises `RecursionError` once the stack gets about 1,000 calls deep; `sys.getrecursionlimit()` shows the exact limit, which is 1000 by default. With 1,000-character strings, the memoised version would need up to 2,000 calls stacked at once. A loop has no such limit, which is one reason bottom-up tables are the usual choice.
:::

::: context three-neighbours Where each cell gets its answer
Each arrow is one kind of last edit. Coming from above means a character of `a` was deleted. Coming from the left means a character of `b` was inserted. Coming along the diagonal means the last two characters were matched (free) or substituted (cost 1). All three neighbors are in the current row or the one before it.

```svg
<svg xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" viewBox="0 0 360 190" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <rect x="100" y="40" width="80" height="44" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="210" y="40" width="80" height="44" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="100" y="114" width="80" height="44" rx="4" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="210" y="114" width="80" height="44" rx="4" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <g font-size="12" fill="#1f2a44" text-anchor="middle">
    <text x="140" y="66">d(i-1, j-1)</text>
    <text x="250" y="66">d(i-1, j)</text>
    <text x="140" y="140">d(i, j-1)</text>
    <text x="250" y="140" font-weight="700">d(i, j)</text>
  </g>
  <g font-size="12" text-anchor="middle">
    <text x="140" y="30" fill="#b4232c">match or substitute</text>
    <text x="250" y="30" fill="#b4232c">delete</text>
    <text x="60" y="140" fill="#b4232c">insert</text>
  </g>
  <line x1="180" y1="84" x2="204" y2="108" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="208,112 199.5,109.2 205.2,103.5" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <line x1="250" y1="84" x2="250" y2="106" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="250,112 246,104 254,104" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <line x1="180" y1="136" x2="202" y2="136" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <polygon points="208,136 200,132 200,140" fill="#1d6fd1"/>
  <text x="195" y="180" font-size="12" fill="#6c7a93" text-anchor="middle">previous row on top, current row below</text>
</svg>
```
:::
--- task
Write `edit_distance(a, b)` returning the fewest insertions, deletions and substitutions that turn `a` into `b`. It must handle two strings of about 1,000 characters each in a few seconds, so fill the table bottom-up and keep only the previous row.
--- starter
def edit_distance(a, b):
    if a == b:
        return 0
    return max(len(a), len(b))
--- solution
def edit_distance(a, b):
    previous = list(range(len(b) + 1))
    for i in range(1, len(a) + 1):
        current = [i] + [0] * len(b)
        for j in range(1, len(b) + 1):
            if a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]:
                current[j] = previous[j - 1]
            else:
                current[j] = 1 + min(previous[j], current[j - 1], previous[j - 1])
        previous = current
    return previous[len(b)]
--- hint
Start with the row for the empty prefix of `a`: `list(range(len(b) + 1))`, since turning `""` into `b[:j]` takes `j` insertions.
--- hint
Each new row starts with `i`. For each `j`, if `a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]` copy the diagonal (`previous[j - 1]`); otherwise take 1 + the smallest of above (`previous[j]`), left (`current[j - 1]`) and diagonal.
--- hint
At the end of each row, the current row becomes `previous`. After the last row, the answer is its final entry, `previous[len(b)]`.
--- check case | kitten to sitting
edit_distance("kitten", "sitting")
=> 3
--- check test | Empty strings
edit_distance("", "abc") == 3 and edit_distance("abc", "") == 3 and edit_distance("", "") == 0
--- check case | Identical strings
edit_distance("same", "same")
=> 0
--- check test | Distance is the same in both directions
edit_distance("sunday", "saturday") == 3 and edit_distance("saturday", "sunday") == 3
--- check case | Completely different
edit_distance("abc", "xyz")
=> 3
--- check case | 1,200 characters, only 2 edits apart
edit_distance("ab" * 600, "ba" * 600)
=> 2
?? Plain recursion explodes here. Fill a table bottom-up, keeping only the previous row.
--- check case | 1,000 characters, all different
edit_distance("a" * 1000, "b" * 1000)
=> 1000

+++ practice | Distance between sentences
--- task
Write `word_distance(a, b)`: the fewest **word** insertions, deletions and substitutions that turn the sentence `a` into the sentence `b`. Split each sentence into words with `.split()`, and compare words exactly (so `"Go"` and `"go"` are different words).

For example, `word_distance("the rocket is ready", "the big rocket is not ready")` is `2`: insert `"big"` and insert `"not"`. It must handle sentences of 1,000 words each quickly, so fill the table bottom-up and keep only the previous row.
--- starter
def word_distance(a, b):
    return abs(len(a.split()) - len(b.split()))
--- solution
def word_distance(a, b):
    x = a.split()
    y = b.split()
    previous = list(range(len(y) + 1))
    for i in range(1, len(x) + 1):
        current = [i] + [0] * len(y)
        for j in range(1, len(y) + 1):
            if x[i - 1] == y[j - 1]:
                current[j] = previous[j - 1]
            else:
                current[j] = 1 + min(previous[j], current[j - 1], previous[j - 1])
        previous = current
    return previous[len(y)]
--- hint
Nothing in the edit-distance table cares whether its items are letters or words. Split both sentences into lists of words, and run the same table over the two lists.
--- hint
The first row is `list(range(len(y) + 1))`, and each new row starts with `i`. A matching pair of words copies the diagonal; otherwise take 1 plus the smallest of above, left and diagonal.
--- check case | Two words inserted
word_distance("the rocket is ready", "the big rocket is not ready")
=> 2
--- check test | Empty sentences
word_distance("", "go now") == 2 and word_distance("launch", "") == 1 and word_distance("", "") == 0
--- check case | The same words in a different order
word_distance("a b c", "c b a")
=> 2
--- check test | The same in both directions, and case matters
word_distance("go for launch", "go launch now") == word_distance("go launch now", "go for launch") == 2 and word_distance("Go", "go") == 1
--- check case | 1,000 words each, 2 apart
word_distance(" ".join(["w"] * 1000), " ".join(["w"] * 998 + ["x", "y"]))
=> 2

+++ practice | Insertions and deletions only
--- task
Some tools, like a simple `diff`, can only insert and delete; they cannot substitute. Write `indel_distance(a, b)`: the fewest single-character insertions and deletions that turn `a` into `b`.

Changing a letter now costs 2 (delete it, then insert the new one), so `indel_distance("kitten", "sitting")` is `5`. Build the same kind of table as the lesson, with one change to the rule for letters that differ. Keep only the previous row, so two strings of 1,000 characters are quick.
--- starter
def indel_distance(a, b):
    return abs(len(a) - len(b))
--- solution
def indel_distance(a, b):
    previous = list(range(len(b) + 1))
    for i in range(1, len(a) + 1):
        current = [i] + [0] * len(b)
        for j in range(1, len(b) + 1):
            if a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]:
                current[j] = previous[j - 1]
            else:
                current[j] = 1 + min(previous[j], current[j - 1])
        previous = current
    return previous[len(b)]
--- hint
The base cases are the same as before: turning a prefix of length `i` into `""` takes `i` deletions, and `""` into a prefix of length `j` takes `j` insertions.
--- hint
When the letters differ, the last step was either a deletion (from above) or an insertion (from the left). The diagonal, which meant a substitution, is no longer allowed.
--- check case | kitten to sitting
indel_distance("kitten", "sitting")
=> 5
--- check test | Empty and identical strings
indel_distance("", "abc") == 3 and indel_distance("abc", "") == 3 and indel_distance("abc", "abc") == 0
--- check case | Nothing in common: delete everything, insert everything
indel_distance("abc", "xyz")
=> 6
--- check case | sunday to saturday
indel_distance("sunday", "saturday")
=> 4
--- check case | 1,000 characters each
indel_distance("ab" * 500, "ba" * 500)
=> 2

+++ practice | Spelling suggestions
--- task
Write `suggest(word, dictionary, max_distance)`. It returns the words from `dictionary` whose edit distance from `word` is at most `max_distance`, comparing in lowercase.

- The closest words come first. Words at the same distance come in A to Z order of their lowercase form.
- Return the dictionary words as they are written, capitals and all.
- If nothing is close enough, return `[]`.

Write your own `edit_distance(a, b)` for it, the bottom-up way from the lesson. For example, `suggest("teh", ["the", "tea", "ten", "then", "rocket"], 1)` gives `["tea", "ten"]`, and with a `max_distance` of 2 it gives `["tea", "ten", "the", "then"]`.
--- starter
def suggest(word, dictionary, max_distance):
    return [w for w in dictionary if w.startswith(word[0])]
--- solution
def edit_distance(a, b):
    previous = list(range(len(b) + 1))
    for i in range(1, len(a) + 1):
        current = [i] + [0] * len(b)
        for j in range(1, len(b) + 1):
            if a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]:
                current[j] = previous[j - 1]
            else:
                current[j] = 1 + min(previous[j], current[j - 1], previous[j - 1])
        previous = current
    return previous[len(b)]


def suggest(word, dictionary, max_distance):
    scored = []
    for candidate in dictionary:
        distance = edit_distance(word.lower(), candidate.lower())
        if distance <= max_distance:
            scored.append((distance, candidate.lower(), candidate))
    return [candidate for distance, lowered, candidate in sorted(scored)]
--- hint
Work out the distance to every dictionary word once, and keep the ones that are close enough together with what you need to sort them by.
--- hint
Tuples like `(distance, candidate.lower(), candidate)` sort by distance first, then by the lowercase word, which is exactly the order asked for. Keep only the last item of each tuple at the end.
--- check case | Distance 1
suggest("teh", ["the", "tea", "ten", "then", "rocket"], 1)
=> ["tea", "ten"]
--- check case | Distance 2, ties from A to Z
suggest("teh", ["the", "tea", "ten", "then", "rocket"], 2)
=> ["tea", "ten", "the", "then"]
--- check case | Compared in lowercase, returned as written
suggest("Mras", ["mars", "Mass", "moon"], 2)
=> ["mars", "Mass"]
--- check case | An exact match comes first
suggest("orbit", ["orbits", "orbit", "rabbit", "orbital"], 2)
=> ["orbit", "orbits", "orbital"]
--- check case | Nothing close enough
suggest("xyz", ["abc", "moon"], 1)
=> []

+++ practice | How similar are two names
--- task
Write `similarity(a, b)`, a score from 0.0 to 1.0 for how alike two names are: `1 - distance / longest`, where `distance` is the edit distance between them and `longest` is the length of the longer name. Get the edge cases right:

- Capitals do not matter: `similarity("Mars", "mars")` is `1.0`.
- Two empty names are identical: `similarity("", "")` is `1.0`, not a `ZeroDivisionError`.
- An empty name and a non-empty one have nothing alike: `0.0`.
- The answer is always a `float`.

Write your own `edit_distance(a, b)` for it. For example, `similarity("kitten", "sitting")` is `1 - 3 / 7`, about `0.571`.
--- starter
def similarity(a, b):
    same = sum(1 for x, y in zip(a, b) if x == y)
    return same / max(len(a), len(b))
--- solution
def edit_distance(a, b):
    previous = list(range(len(b) + 1))
    for i in range(1, len(a) + 1):
        current = [i] + [0] * len(b)
        for j in range(1, len(b) + 1):
            if a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]:
                current[j] = previous[j - 1]
            else:
                current[j] = 1 + min(previous[j], current[j - 1], previous[j - 1])
        previous = current
    return previous[len(b)]


def similarity(a, b):
    a = a.lower()
    b = b.lower()
    longest = max(len(a), len(b))
    if longest == 0:
        return 1.0
    return 1 - edit_distance(a, b) / longest
--- hint
Lowercase both names first. Then the only danger is the division: `longest` is 0 exactly when both names are empty.
--- hint
Return `1.0` straight away when `longest == 0`. Otherwise `1 - edit_distance(a, b) / longest` is already a `float`, because `/` always gives one.
--- check test | kitten and sitting
abs(similarity("kitten", "sitting") - (1 - 3 / 7)) < 1e-9
--- check case | Two empty names
similarity("", "")
=> 1.0
--- check case | One empty name
similarity("", "abc")
=> 0.0
--- check case | Capitals do not matter
similarity("Mars", "mars")
=> 1.0
--- check test | Nothing in common, and the same both ways
similarity("abc", "xyz") == 0.0 and similarity("Ceres", "Cereal") == similarity("Cereal", "Ceres")
--- check test | Always a float
type(similarity("ab", "ab")) is float and type(similarity("", "")) is float

+++ practice | Debug: a table with one row
--- task
This `edit_distance` fills the whole table, one row for each prefix of `a`, instead of keeping only the previous row. The logic looks right, but the answers are wild: `edit_distance("same", "same")` gives `7` instead of `0`, and `edit_distance("flaw", "lawn")` gives `7` instead of `2`. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
def edit_distance(a, b):
    table = [[0] * (len(b) + 1)] * (len(a) + 1)
    for j in range(len(b) + 1):
        table[0][j] = j
    for i in range(len(a) + 1):
        table[i][0] = i
    for i in range(1, len(a) + 1):
        for j in range(1, len(b) + 1):
            if a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]:
                table[i][j] = table[i - 1][j - 1]
            else:
                table[i][j] = 1 + min(table[i - 1][j], table[i][j - 1], table[i - 1][j - 1])
    return table[len(a)][len(b)]
--- solution
def edit_distance(a, b):
    table = [[0] * (len(b) + 1) for _ in range(len(a) + 1)]
    for j in range(len(b) + 1):
        table[0][j] = j
    for i in range(len(a) + 1):
        table[i][0] = i
    for i in range(1, len(a) + 1):
        for j in range(1, len(b) + 1):
            if a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]:
                table[i][j] = table[i - 1][j - 1]
            else:
                table[i][j] = 1 + min(table[i - 1][j], table[i][j - 1], table[i - 1][j - 1])
    return table[len(a)][len(b)]
--- hint
Check whether the rows are really different lists: after the first line, what does `table[0] is table[1]` give? The aliasing lesson in the advanced course met this exact line.
--- hint
Multiplying a list of lists copies the reference, not the inner list, so every row is the same list. Build one fresh row per prefix with a comprehension: `[[0] * (len(b) + 1) for _ in range(len(a) + 1)]`.
--- check case | Identical strings are 0 apart
edit_distance("same", "same")
=> 0
--- check case | flaw to lawn
edit_distance("flaw", "lawn")
=> 2
--- check test | The lesson's examples
edit_distance("kitten", "sitting") == 3 and edit_distance("sunday", "saturday") == 3 and edit_distance("abc", "xyz") == 3
--- check test | Empty strings
edit_distance("", "abc") == 3 and edit_distance("abc", "") == 3 and edit_distance("", "") == 0

+++ practice | Edit distance with prices
--- task
In some jobs the three edits do not cost the same. Comparing DNA, a substitution may be rarer than an insertion; fixing a typed word, a deletion may be cheap. Write `weighted_distance(a, b, insert_cost, delete_cost, substitute_cost)`: the lowest total cost of edits that turns `a` into `b`. A letter kept as it is costs nothing.

- The base cases change: turning a prefix of length `i` into `""` costs `i * delete_cost`, and turning `""` into a prefix of length `j` costs `j * insert_cost`.
- Each cell takes the cheapest of three: a deletion from above, an insertion from the left, or the diagonal, which costs `substitute_cost` when the letters differ and nothing when they are the same.
- A substitution can cost more than a deletion and an insertion together; then the cheapest way never uses it.

For example, `weighted_distance("abc", "abd", 1, 1, 5)` is `2`: deleting `c` and inserting `d` is cheaper than one substitution at 5. It must handle two strings of 1,000 characters each quickly.
--- starter
def weighted_distance(a, b, insert_cost, delete_cost, substitute_cost):
    return max(len(a), len(b)) * substitute_cost
--- solution
def weighted_distance(a, b, insert_cost, delete_cost, substitute_cost):
    previous = [j * insert_cost for j in range(len(b) + 1)]
    for i in range(1, len(a) + 1):
        current = [i * delete_cost] + [0] * len(b)
        for j in range(1, len(b) + 1):
            same = a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]
            current[j] = min(
                previous[j] + delete_cost,
                current[j - 1] + insert_cost,
                previous[j - 1] + (0 if same else substitute_cost),
            )
        previous = current
    return previous[len(b)]
--- hint
Start from the one-row table in the lesson. The first row is now `[j * insert_cost for j in range(len(b) + 1)]`, and each new row starts with `i * delete_cost`.
--- hint
Each cell is `min(previous[j] + delete_cost, current[j - 1] + insert_cost, previous[j - 1] + (0 if same else substitute_cost))`, where `same` says whether `a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]`.
--- check case | Every edit costs 1: the ordinary edit distance
weighted_distance("kitten", "sitting", 1, 1, 1)
=> 3
--- check case | A dear substitution is never used
weighted_distance("abc", "abd", 1, 1, 5)
=> 2
--- check test | The base cases use the right prices
weighted_distance("", "abc", 2, 7, 1) == 6 and weighted_distance("abc", "", 2, 7, 1) == 21
--- check case | Cheap insertions, dear deletions
weighted_distance("ab", "ba", 1, 3, 10)
=> 4
--- check case | A DNA example
weighted_distance("GATTACA", "GCATGCU", 1, 1, 1)
=> 4
--- check case | 1,000 characters each
weighted_distance("ab" * 500, "ba" * 500, 1, 1, 1)
=> 2

=== py4-12 | Problem solving: Dijkstra's shortest path
--- teach
Last lesson you filled a table to measure how far apart two strings are. This lesson goes back to a question from the advanced course: what is the best route from here to there? There you used **BFS**, which finds the route with the fewest moves. BFS works because every move costs the same: one step. Real maps are not like that. One road is 10 km long, another is 1 km. This lesson finds the *cheapest* route when steps have different costs. The same problem sits inside [[every maps app and every internet router|routing-real]].

## Fewest steps is not always cheapest

Picture four towns. From A there is a long road straight to B: 10 km. There is also a short road from A to C (1 km), one from C to D (1 km), and one from D to B (1 km).

BFS would say "A to B is one step, done". But that one step costs 10 km. The three-step route A, C, D, B costs 1 + 1 + 1 = 3 km. More steps, much cheaper.

In Python you can store this map as a dictionary. Each town maps to a list of `(neighbour, cost)` pairs, one pair for each road out of it:

```python
graph = {
    "A": [("B", 10), ("C", 1)],
    "C": [("D", 1)],
    "D": [("B", 1)],
}
```

A map like this is a **[[weighted graph|graph-words]]**. The places are **nodes**, the roads between them are **edges**, and the cost written on each edge is its **weight**.

Two things to notice:

- The roads here are one way. A lists a road to B, but B lists nothing, so you cannot go from B back to A.
- B is not a key at all. A node with no roads out of it may be left out. So always read the roads with `graph.get(node, [])`, which means "the list for this node, or an empty list if it has none".

## The idea: settle the cheapest place next

**[[Dijkstra's algorithm|dijkstra-history]]** (say it "DIKE-stra") finds the cheapest route from a start to every place it can reach. It needs one promise: no cost is [[negative|negative-costs]].

Think of water poured in at the start and spreading along pipes of different lengths. It reaches near places first and far places later. Dijkstra copies that. For every place reached so far, it keeps the **best cost found so far**, a number that may still get lower. Then, over and over:

1. Pick the place with the lowest cost so far that is not yet settled.
2. **Settle** it: its cost is now final.
3. For each road out of it, check whether going through this place makes the neighbor cheaper. If it does, write down the new, lower cost.

Step 3 has a name: **relaxing** an edge, which means checking whether that road gives a cheaper way to reach its end.

Why is a settled cost final? Any other route to that place would have to leave the settled places through some place that is not settled yet, and every one of those already costs at least as much. Adding more roads can only add cost, because no cost is negative. So no other route can beat it.

## The heap: "cheapest next", fast

Step 1 asks "which place is cheapest right now?" again and again. In the heapq lesson you met the tool for exactly that question. A heap of `(cost, node)` tuples always keeps the smallest cost at the front, because tuples compare by their first item first. A heap used this way is called a **[[priority queue|priority-queue]]**.

Here is Dijkstra finding the cheapest cost from the start to every place it can reach:

```python
import heapq

def costs_from(graph, start):
    best = {start: 0}
    heap = [(0, start)]
    while heap:
        cost, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if cost > best[node]:
            continue                      # an old, worse entry: skip it
        for neighbour, weight in graph.get(node, []):
            new = cost + weight
            if new < best.get(neighbour, float("inf")):
                best[neighbour] = new
                heapq.heappush(heap, (new, neighbour))
    return best
```

What each part does:

- `best` holds the cheapest cost found so far for each place. The start costs 0.
- `heapq.heappop(heap)` takes out the cheapest entry. That is steps 1 and 2.
- The `for` loop is step 3. `new` is the cost of reaching the neighbor through this node.
- `best.get(neighbour, float("inf"))` is the neighbor's cost so far, or [[infinity|infinity]] if it has not been reached yet, so any real cost beats it.
- If `new` is lower, it goes into `best`, and the neighbor goes onto the heap.

On the four towns:

```python
costs_from(graph, "A")      # {'A': 0, 'B': 3, 'C': 1, 'D': 2}
```

B costs 3, by the long way round, as it should.

## Old entries on the heap

Here is the order the entries come off the heap for the four towns:

```
(0, 'A')   (1, 'C')   (2, 'D')   (3, 'B')   (10, 'B')
```

B came off twice. When A was settled, the road A to B gave B a cost of 10, so `(10, "B")` went onto the heap. Later, D found a way to B costing 3, so `(3, "B")` went on too.

A heap cannot easily find and change an entry in its middle. So instead of updating the old entry, you push a new one and leave the old one where it is. The old entry is **stale**: out of date. When `(10, "B")` finally comes off, `cost > best[node]` asks "is 10 more than 3?", which is true, and `continue` skips it.

## Getting the route, not only the cost

To know the route, also remember where you came from. Each time you lower a neighbor's cost, write `previous[neighbour] = node`, which means "the cheapest way into this neighbor, so far, comes from this node".

For the four towns, `previous` ends up as `{'B': 'D', 'C': 'A', 'D': 'C'}`. (B was first reached from A, then its entry was overwritten when D found a cheaper way.)

Now walk backwards from the goal. B came from D, D came from C, C came from A. A has no entry, because it is the start. That gives B, D, C, A. Turn it around and you have the route:

```python
path = ["B"]
node = "B"
while node in previous:
    node = previous[node]
    path.append(node)
path[::-1]       # ['A', 'C', 'D', 'B']
```

`path[::-1]` is the list read backwards: a slice with a step of minus one.

## Stopping early

If you only care about one goal, you can stop the moment the goal comes off the heap. It is settled then, so its cost is final, and the rest of the map does not matter.

## Why the heap matters

Each push or pop on a heap is O(log n). With a heap, Dijkstra is **[[O(E log V)|big-o-ev]]**, for E edges and V nodes. Without one, you would scan every place to find the cheapest each time, which is O(V²): for 20,000 places that is about 400 million steps, far too slow.

**Watch out:** in BFS you marked a square as seen when you *added* it to the queue. Do not do that here. With weights, a route found later can still be cheaper: B was first found at a cost of 10, and later at 3. A place is only settled when it is *popped* off the heap.

::: context routing-real Where Dijkstra runs every day
Many internet routers run Dijkstra's algorithm. In a common routing system called OSPF ("Open Shortest Path First"), each router keeps a map of the network, with a cost on every link, and runs Dijkstra on it to decide where to send each packet next. Maps apps face the same question on road networks with millions of junctions. They use faster relatives of Dijkstra, such as A* (which also aims towards the goal) and tricks that pre-compute shortcuts, but the heart of all of them is today's idea: settle the cheapest place next.
:::

::: context graph-words Nodes, edges and weights
In computing, a **graph** is not a chart with axes. It is a set of things (the **nodes**) and connections between them (the **edges**). Towns and roads, web pages and links, people and friendships are all graphs. Give each edge a number, such as a length, a time or a price, and it becomes a **weighted graph**. Here is the four-town map from the lesson, with the cheap route in blue:

```svg
<svg viewBox="0 0 360 160" xmlns="http://www.w3.org/2000/svg" font-family="Inter, Arial, sans-serif">
  <defs>
    <marker id="arrow" viewBox="0 0 10 10" refX="9" refY="5" markerWidth="7" markerHeight="7" orient="auto-start-reverse">
      <path d="M0,0 L10,5 L0,10 z" fill="#1f2a44"/>
    </marker>
  </defs>
  <line x1="58" y1="100" x2="300" y2="100" stroke="#6c7a93" stroke-width="2" marker-end="url(#arrow)"/>
  <line x1="53" y1="91" x2="115" y2="50" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="3" marker-end="url(#arrow)"/>
  <line x1="148" y1="40" x2="210" y2="40" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="3" marker-end="url(#arrow)"/>
  <line x1="243" y1="49" x2="305" y2="90" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="3" marker-end="url(#arrow)"/>
  <circle cx="40" cy="100" r="16" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <circle cx="130" cy="40" r="16" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <circle cx="230" cy="40" r="16" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <circle cx="320" cy="100" r="16" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1f2a44" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="40" y="105" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">A</text>
  <text x="130" y="45" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">C</text>
  <text x="230" y="45" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">D</text>
  <text x="320" y="105" font-size="14" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">B</text>
  <text x="74" y="62" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">1</text>
  <text x="180" y="32" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">1</text>
  <text x="286" y="62" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1d6fd1">1</text>
  <text x="180" y="118" font-size="13" text-anchor="middle" fill="#6c7a93">10</text>
  <text x="180" y="148" font-size="12" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">A to B: one step costs 10, three steps cost 3</text>
</svg>
```
:::

::: context dijkstra-history Twenty minutes at a café
Edsger Dijkstra (1930 to 2002) was a Dutch computer scientist. In 1956 he wanted a problem to show off a new computer, the ARMAC, that people who were not mathematicians could understand: the shortest route between two Dutch cities. He later said he worked the algorithm out in about twenty minutes, sitting on a café terrace in Amsterdam, without pencil and paper. He published it in 1959 in a paper about three pages long. It is still one of the most used algorithms in the world.
:::

::: context negative-costs Why a negative cost breaks it
Suppose A to B costs 2, A to C costs 5, and C to B costs minus 4. Dijkstra pops B first, at cost 2, and settles it. Only later does it pop C and find A, C, B, which costs 5 + (−4) = 1: cheaper, but B was already declared final. The promise "more roads can only add cost" was false. Graphs with negative weights need a different algorithm, such as Bellman–Ford, which is slower (about V × E steps) but copes with them. Real costs like distance and time are never negative, so Dijkstra fits most maps.
:::

::: context priority-queue A queue where the most urgent goes first
An ordinary queue is first come, first served, like the line at a shop. A **priority queue** serves the most urgent item first, like a hospital emergency room that sees the worst injury first, whoever arrived earlier. In Python the heap is the usual way to build one. One detail: if two entries have the same cost, the tuples compare their second items, the nodes, to break the tie. `(3, "B")` comes before `(3, "C")`. So all the nodes must be things Python can compare with each other: all strings, or all tuples, not a mixture.
:::

::: context infinity A number bigger than every number
`float("inf")` is a special floating-point value meaning infinity. Every ordinary number is smaller than it: `float("inf") > 10**100` is `True`, and `min(3, float("inf"))` is `3`. That makes it the perfect starting value for "the best cost so far" when you have not found any route yet: the first real cost always beats it. The `math` module has the same value under a shorter name, `math.inf`.
:::

::: context big-o-ev Reading O(E log V)
The big-O lessons measured work in terms of one size, n. A graph has two sizes: V, the number of nodes, and E, the number of edges. Dijkstra looks at every edge once, and each look may push onto the heap, which costs about log V steps. So the total is about E times log V. For a grid of 20,000 places with about 80,000 edges, log₂ of 20,000 is about 14, so that is about 80,000 × 14, roughly a million small steps. Compare that with 400 million for the scanning version.
:::
--- task
Write `cheapest(graph, start, goal)`.

- `graph` maps each node to a list of `(neighbour, cost)` pairs, like the four towns in the lesson. Costs are 0 or more.
- A node may appear only as a neighbor and not as a key, so read the roads with `graph.get(node, [])`.
- Return `(total_cost, path)`, where `path` is the list of nodes from `start` to `goal`, with both ends included.
- If the goal cannot be reached, return `None`.
- `cheapest(g, x, x)` is `(0, [x])`.

It must handle a graph of about 20,000 nodes quickly.
--- starter
def cheapest(graph, start, goal):
    return None
--- solution
import heapq


def cheapest(graph, start, goal):
    best = {start: 0}
    previous = {}
    heap = [(0, start)]
    while heap:
        cost, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if node == goal:
            path = [node]
            while node in previous:
                node = previous[node]
                path.append(node)
            return cost, path[::-1]
        if cost > best[node]:
            continue
        for neighbour, weight in graph.get(node, []):
            new = cost + weight
            if new < best.get(neighbour, float("inf")):
                best[neighbour] = new
                previous[neighbour] = node
                heapq.heappush(heap, (new, neighbour))
    return None
--- hint
Keep three things: `best` (the cheapest known cost for each node), `previous` (where you came from) and a heap of `(cost, node)` entries, starting with `(0, start)`.
--- hint
Pop the cheapest entry. Skip it if its cost is more than `best[node]`. Otherwise, for each neighbor in `graph.get(node, [])`, work out the new cost; if it beats the neighbor's best so far, store it in `best`, set `previous[neighbour] = node` and push `(new, neighbour)`.
--- hint
When the goal is popped, follow `previous` back to the start, reverse the list with `[::-1]`, and return it together with the cost. If the heap runs empty first, return `None`.
--- check case | The cheaper route has more steps
cheapest({"A": [("B", 10), ("C", 1)], "C": [("D", 1)], "D": [("B", 1)]}, "A", "B")
=> (3, ["A", "C", "D", "B"])
--- check case | Start is the goal
cheapest({"A": [("B", 1)]}, "A", "A")
=> (0, ["A"])
--- check case | Unreachable
cheapest({"A": [("B", 1)], "C": [("A", 1)]}, "A", "C")
=> None
--- check case | Edges are one way
cheapest({"A": [("B", 5)], "B": [("A", 1), ("C", 1)]}, "C", "A")
=> None
--- check case | Zero-cost edges are fine
cheapest({"s": [("a", 0), ("b", 2)], "a": [("b", 0)], "b": [("t", 1)]}, "s", "t")
=> (1, ["s", "a", "b", "t"])
--- check case | A 150 by 150 weighted grid (22,500 nodes)
cheapest({(r, c): [((r + dr, c + dc), ((r + dr) * 7 + (c + dc) * 13) % 10 + 1) for dr, dc in ((0, 1), (1, 0), (0, -1), (-1, 0)) if 0 <= r + dr < 150 and 0 <= c + dc < 150] for r in range(150) for c in range(150)}, (0, 0), (149, 149))[0]
=> 745
?? Finding the cheapest node by scanning every node is far too slow here. Use a heap.

+++ practice | Fastest times on two-way roads
--- task
A rover's map is a list of roads. Each road is `(a, b, minutes)`, and every road can be driven **both ways** in the same time. Write `fastest_times(roads, start)`. It returns a dict from every place the rover can reach to the fewest minutes it takes to get there from `start`, with `start` itself at `0`.

- First build a graph like the lesson's: a dict from each place to a list of `(neighbour, minutes)` pairs, adding each road in both directions.
- Then run Dijkstra's algorithm with a heap.
- Places that cannot be reached are left out. With no roads at all, the answer is `{start: 0}`.
- It must handle 20,000 places quickly.

For example, with the roads `[("base", "ridge", 7), ("base", "lake", 2), ("lake", "ridge", 3), ("ridge", "peak", 4)]`, the times from `"base"` are `{"base": 0, "lake": 2, "ridge": 5, "peak": 9}`.
--- starter
import heapq


def fastest_times(roads, start):
    return {start: 0}
--- solution
import heapq
from collections import defaultdict


def fastest_times(roads, start):
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for a, b, minutes in roads:
        graph[a].append((b, minutes))
        graph[b].append((a, minutes))
    best = {start: 0}
    heap = [(0, start)]
    while heap:
        time, place = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if time > best[place]:
            continue
        for neighbour, minutes in graph[place]:
            new = time + minutes
            if new < best.get(neighbour, float("inf")):
                best[neighbour] = new
                heapq.heappush(heap, (new, neighbour))
    return best
--- hint
A `defaultdict(list)` builds the graph without checking whether a place is already a key: append `(b, minutes)` to `graph[a]`, and `(a, minutes)` to `graph[b]`.
--- hint
The search is the lesson's: `best` starts as `{start: 0}` and the heap as `[(0, start)]`. Pop the cheapest entry, skip it if it is stale, and relax each road out of that place.
--- check case | The long way round is quicker
fastest_times([("base", "ridge", 7), ("base", "lake", 2), ("lake", "ridge", 3), ("ridge", "peak", 4)], "base")
=> {"base": 0, "ridge": 5, "lake": 2, "peak": 9}
--- check case | No roads at all
fastest_times([], "x")
=> {"x": 0}
--- check case | Places that cannot be reached are left out
fastest_times([("a", "b", 1), ("c", "d", 1)], "a")
=> {"a": 0, "b": 1}
--- check case | Roads work both ways
fastest_times([("a", "b", 4)], "b")
=> {"b": 0, "a": 4}
--- check case | Two roads between the same places: the quicker one counts
fastest_times([("a", "b", 9), ("b", "a", 2)], "a")
=> {"a": 0, "b": 2}
--- check case | 20,000 places in a row
fastest_times([(i, i + 1, 1) for i in range(20000)], 0)[20000]
=> 20000

+++ practice | The cheapest walk across a grid
--- task
A grid is a list of strings of digits, all the same length. Each digit is the cost of stepping **into** that square. You start in the top-left square (its own cost is not counted) and want to reach the bottom-right one, moving up, down, left or right, one square at a time.

Write `cheapest_walk(grid)`, which returns the lowest total cost. A grid of one square costs `0`. Treat each square `(row, column)` as a node, and its up to four neighbors as its roads, with the neighbor's digit as the cost. It must handle a 100 by 100 grid quickly.

For example, for `["131", "191", "111"]` the answer is `4`: down, down, right, right.
--- starter
def cheapest_walk(grid):
    right = sum(int(d) for d in grid[0][1:])
    down = sum(int(row[-1]) for row in grid[1:])
    return right + down
--- solution
import heapq


def cheapest_walk(grid):
    rows = len(grid)
    cols = len(grid[0])
    goal = (rows - 1, cols - 1)
    best = {(0, 0): 0}
    heap = [(0, (0, 0))]
    while heap:
        cost, (r, c) = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if (r, c) == goal:
            return cost
        if cost > best[(r, c)]:
            continue
        for dr, dc in ((1, 0), (-1, 0), (0, 1), (0, -1)):
            nr, nc = r + dr, c + dc
            if 0 <= nr < rows and 0 <= nc < cols:
                new = cost + int(grid[nr][nc])
                if new < best.get((nr, nc), float("inf")):
                    best[(nr, nc)] = new
                    heapq.heappush(heap, (new, (nr, nc)))
--- hint
The neighbors come from the grid itself, as in the BFS lesson: the four `(dr, dc)` steps, kept only when the new square is inside the grid. The cost of the road into a square is `int(grid[nr][nc])`.
--- hint
Run Dijkstra on `(row, column)` tuples, starting from `(0, 0)` at cost 0, and return the cost when the bottom-right square is popped. Every square is reachable, so the loop always finds it.
--- check case | Down first is cheaper
cheapest_walk(["131", "191", "111"])
=> 4
--- check case | One square
cheapest_walk(["5"])
=> 0
--- check case | Two by two
cheapest_walk(["19", "11"])
=> 2
--- check case | The cheap path winds back to the left
cheapest_walk(["1111", "9991", "1111", "1999", "1111"])
=> 13
--- check case | A 100 by 100 grid
cheapest_walk(["".join(str((r * 7 + c * 3) % 9 + 1) for c in range(100)) for r in range(100)])
=> 627

+++ practice | A route report from text
--- task
Roads arrive as lines of text like `"A B 5"`: a one-way road from `A` to `B` that costs `5` (a whole number, 0 or more). Blank lines are skipped. Write `route_report(lines, start, goal)`, which returns one line of text:

- the cheapest route as the places joined by `" -> "`, then a space and the total cost in brackets, like `"A -> C -> D -> B (3)"`;
- `"no route from A to Z"` (with the real names) if `goal` cannot be reached;
- just `"A (0)"` when `start` is the goal.

Build the graph from the lines, then use Dijkstra's algorithm, remembering where each place was reached from, as in the lesson.
--- starter
def route_report(lines, start, goal):
    return f"no route from {start} to {goal}"
--- solution
import heapq


def parse_roads(lines):
    graph = {}
    for line in lines:
        if not line.strip():
            continue
        a, b, cost = line.split()
        graph.setdefault(a, []).append((b, int(cost)))
    return graph


def route_report(lines, start, goal):
    graph = parse_roads(lines)
    best = {start: 0}
    previous = {}
    heap = [(0, start)]
    while heap:
        cost, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if node == goal:
            path = [node]
            while node in previous:
                node = previous[node]
                path.append(node)
            return f"{' -> '.join(path[::-1])} ({cost})"
        if cost > best[node]:
            continue
        for neighbour, weight in graph.get(node, []):
            new = cost + weight
            if new < best.get(neighbour, float("inf")):
                best[neighbour] = new
                previous[neighbour] = node
                heapq.heappush(heap, (new, neighbour))
    return f"no route from {start} to {goal}"
--- hint
Split the job: a small `parse_roads(lines)` that builds the dict of `(neighbour, cost)` lists (with `setdefault`), and the search itself, which never sees text.
--- hint
When the goal comes off the heap, follow `previous` back to the start, turn the list round with `[::-1]`, and join it with `" -> "`. If the heap runs empty first, return the "no route" line.
--- check case | The cheapest route, with its cost
route_report(["A B 10", "A C 1", "C D 1", "D B 1"], "A", "B")
=> "A -> C -> D -> B (3)"
--- check case | Blank lines are skipped
route_report(["", "X Y 4", "   ", "Y Z 5", ""], "X", "Z")
=> "X -> Y -> Z (9)"
--- check case | No route
route_report(["A B 1"], "B", "A")
=> "no route from B to A"
--- check case | Start is the goal
route_report(["A B 1"], "A", "A")
=> "A (0)"
--- check case | No roads at all
route_report([], "home", "moon")
=> "no route from home to moon"

+++ practice | Dijkstra's promise, and other edges
--- task
Write `cheapest_cost(graph, start, goal)`, which returns the cheapest total cost from `start` to `goal`, or `None` if the goal cannot be reached. `graph` is a dict from nodes to lists of `(neighbour, cost)` pairs, as in the lesson. The starter is the obvious version; make it handle every one of these edge cases:

- Dijkstra's algorithm is only right when no cost is negative. So before searching, look at **every** road in the graph, reachable or not, and raise `ValueError` if any cost is below 0. That comes first, even when `start` is the goal.
- `start` may have no entry in the graph at all, and so may other nodes. `cheapest_cost({}, "q", "q")` is `0`.
- Two roads between the same pair of nodes, roads from a node to itself, and loops of roads that cost 0 must all work.
--- starter
import heapq


def cheapest_cost(graph, start, goal):
    best = {start: 0}
    heap = [(0, start)]
    while heap:
        cost, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if node == goal:
            return cost
        if cost > best[node]:
            continue
        for neighbour, weight in graph[node]:
            new = cost + weight
            if new < best.get(neighbour, float("inf")):
                best[neighbour] = new
                heapq.heappush(heap, (new, neighbour))
    return None
--- solution
import heapq


def cheapest_cost(graph, start, goal):
    for roads in graph.values():
        for _, cost in roads:
            if cost < 0:
                raise ValueError("Dijkstra's algorithm needs costs of 0 or more")
    best = {start: 0}
    heap = [(0, start)]
    while heap:
        cost, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if node == goal:
            return cost
        if cost > best[node]:
            continue
        for neighbour, weight in graph.get(node, []):
            new = cost + weight
            if new < best.get(neighbour, float("inf")):
                best[neighbour] = new
                heapq.heappush(heap, (new, neighbour))
    return None
--- hint
Two separate problems: a node with no key in the graph, and negative costs. For the first, remember how the lesson read the roads out of a node.
--- hint
Read roads with `graph.get(node, [])`. Before the search starts, loop over `graph.values()`, and over each list of `(neighbour, cost)` pairs, and raise `ValueError` if a cost is below 0.
--- check case | Two roads between the same pair: the cheaper one counts
cheapest_cost({"a": [("b", 5), ("b", 2)]}, "a", "b")
=> 2
--- check case | A road to itself and a loop that costs nothing
cheapest_cost({"a": [("a", 0), ("b", 0)], "b": [("a", 0), ("c", 3)]}, "a", "c")
=> 3
--- check case | A start with no entry in the graph
cheapest_cost({"x": [("y", 1)]}, "q", "y")
=> None
--- check case | A start with no entry that is also the goal
cheapest_cost({}, "q", "q")
=> 0
--- check test | Any negative cost is refused, even one that cannot be reached
raises(ValueError, lambda: cheapest_cost({"a": [("b", 1)], "z": [("a", -1)]}, "a", "b")) and raises(ValueError, lambda: cheapest_cost({"a": [("a", -2)]}, "a", "a"))
--- check case | A goal reached only through nodes with no entry
cheapest_cost({"s": [("m", 2)], "m": [("t", 3), ("dead", 1)]}, "s", "t")
=> 5
--- check case | 30,000 nodes, two roads between each pair
cheapest_cost({i: [(i + 1, 2), (i + 1, 1)] for i in range(30000)}, 0, 30000)
=> 30000

+++ practice | Debug: settled too soon
--- task
`cheapest(graph, start, goal)` should return the cheapest total cost from `start` to `goal`, or `None` if it cannot be reached. It works on many maps, but on the lesson's four towns, `{"A": [("B", 10), ("C", 1)], "C": [("D", 1)], "D": [("B", 1)]}`, it says A to B costs `10` instead of `3`. Find the bug and fix it.
--- starter
import heapq


def cheapest(graph, start, goal):
    heap = [(0, start)]
    seen = {start}
    while heap:
        cost, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if node == goal:
            return cost
        for neighbour, weight in graph.get(node, []):
            if neighbour not in seen:
                seen.add(neighbour)
                heapq.heappush(heap, (cost + weight, neighbour))
    return None
--- solution
import heapq


def cheapest(graph, start, goal):
    heap = [(0, start)]
    settled = set()
    while heap:
        cost, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if node in settled:
            continue
        settled.add(node)
        if node == goal:
            return cost
        for neighbour, weight in graph.get(node, []):
            if neighbour not in settled:
                heapq.heappush(heap, (cost + weight, neighbour))
    return None
--- hint
When is B first put into `seen`? At that moment the only way known to B costs 10. What happens when D later finds the way that costs 3?
--- hint
That is BFS's rule, and it is wrong with costs. A node is only settled when it comes **off** the heap. Mark it then, skip any entry for a node that is already settled, and let a node go on the heap more than once.
--- check case | The cheaper route has more steps
cheapest({"A": [("B", 10), ("C", 1)], "C": [("D", 1)], "D": [("B", 1)]}, "A", "B")
=> 3
--- check case | A cheaper way found after a dearer one
cheapest({"s": [("x", 1), ("y", 5)], "x": [("y", 1)], "y": [("t", 1)]}, "s", "t")
=> 3
--- check test | Unreachable, and start is the goal
cheapest({"a": [("b", 1)]}, "b", "a") is None and cheapest({"a": [("b", 1)]}, "a", "a") == 0
--- check case | A 60 by 60 weighted grid
cheapest({(r, c): [((r + dr, c + dc), (r * 5 + c * 11 + dr + 2 * dc) % 7 + 1) for dr, dc in ((0, 1), (1, 0), (0, -1), (-1, 0)) if 0 <= r + dr < 60 and 0 <= c + dc < 60] for r in range(60) for c in range(60)}, (0, 0), (59, 59))
=> 311

+++ practice | Cheapest flights with few stops
--- task
Flights are `(from_city, to_city, price)` tuples, one way. Write `cheapest_flight(flights, start, goal, max_stops)`: the lowest total price from `start` to `goal` using a route with **at most `max_stops` stops** in between, which means at most `max_stops + 1` flights. Return `None` if there is no such route, and `0` when `start` is the goal. City names are strings.

The cheapest route overall may have too many stops, so plain Dijkstra is not enough. Search over **states** instead: a state is `(city, flights_taken)`, and the heap holds `(price, city, flights_taken)`. Keep the best price for each state, never take another flight from a state that has already used `max_stops + 1` flights, and return the price the first time `goal` comes off the heap.

For example, with `[("A", "B", 100), ("B", "C", 100), ("A", "C", 500), ("C", "D", 100), ("B", "D", 600)]`, the cheapest route from A to D is A, B, C, D for 300, with 2 stops. With at most 1 stop it is A, C, D for 600, and with no stops there is no route.
--- starter
import heapq


def cheapest_flight(flights, start, goal, max_stops):
    best = None
    for a, b, price in flights:
        if a == start and b == goal and (best is None or price < best):
            best = price
    return best
--- solution
import heapq
from collections import defaultdict


def cheapest_flight(flights, start, goal, max_stops):
    graph = defaultdict(list)
    for a, b, price in flights:
        graph[a].append((b, price))
    best = {(start, 0): 0}
    heap = [(0, start, 0)]
    while heap:
        cost, city, taken = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if city == goal:
            return cost
        if cost > best[(city, taken)] or taken == max_stops + 1:
            continue
        for nxt, price in graph[city]:
            new = cost + price
            state = (nxt, taken + 1)
            if new < best.get(state, float("inf")):
                best[state] = new
                heapq.heappush(heap, (new, nxt, taken + 1))
    return None
--- hint
The same city reached with fewer flights is a different situation: it has more flights left to use. That is why the state includes `flights_taken`, and why `best` is keyed by `(city, flights_taken)`.
--- hint
Pop `(cost, city, taken)`. Return `cost` if `city` is the goal. Skip the entry if it is stale or if `taken == max_stops + 1`. Otherwise relax each flight out of `city` into the state `(next_city, taken + 1)`.
--- check case | Enough stops for the cheapest route
cheapest_flight([("A", "B", 100), ("B", "C", 100), ("A", "C", 500), ("C", "D", 100), ("B", "D", 600)], "A", "D", 2)
=> 300
--- check case | At most one stop
cheapest_flight([("A", "B", 100), ("B", "C", 100), ("A", "C", 500), ("C", "D", 100), ("B", "D", 600)], "A", "D", 1)
=> 600
--- check case | No stops, and no direct flight
cheapest_flight([("A", "B", 100), ("B", "C", 100), ("A", "C", 500), ("C", "D", 100), ("B", "D", 600)], "A", "D", 0)
=> None
--- check test | Start is the goal, and a direct flight with no stops
cheapest_flight([("X", "Y", 7)], "X", "X", 0) == 0 and cheapest_flight([("X", "Y", 7), ("X", "Z", 1), ("Z", "Y", 1)], "X", "Y", 0) == 7
--- check case | Loops in the flights do not trap the search
cheapest_flight([("A", "B", 1), ("B", "A", 1), ("B", "C", 50), ("A", "C", 100)], "A", "C", 3)
=> 51
--- check case | 200 cities in a row: the cheap way has too many stops
cheapest_flight([(f"c{i}", f"c{i + 1}", 1) for i in range(200)] + [(f"c{i}", f"c{i + 10}", 12) for i in range(0, 200, 10)], "c0", "c200", 30)
=> 238

=== py4-13 | Problem solving: a tokenizer
--- teach
Edit distance and Dijkstra were algorithms someone else invented, which you learned to write. The last lessons of this course build a small program the way real language tools are built: a calculator that understands text like `2 * (3 + 4) - -1`.

Think about how you read a sentence. First you see words and punctuation marks, not single letters. Then you work out how the words fit together. A calculator, and Python itself, and [[every interpreter and compiler|interpreters-too]], works in the same two stages:

1. **Tokenizing** (this lesson): cut the text into meaningful pieces, called **[[tokens|token-word]]**. For `2 * (3 + 4) - -1` the tokens are `2`, `*`, `(`, `3`, `+`, `4`, `)`, `-`, `-`, `1`.
2. **Parsing** (the next lessons): work out how the tokens fit together, and compute the answer.

Splitting the job keeps each half simple. The parser never has to think about spaces, or about how many characters a number has. It gets a tidy list like `[12, "+", 3]`.

## Walking through the text with an index

Keep a number `i` that points at the current character, like a finger resting on the text. Look at the character under your finger and decide what kind of token starts there. Then move the finger past it.

Here is a tokenizer for whole numbers, `+` and `-`:

```python
def tokenize(text):
    tokens = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(text):
        ch = text[i]
        if ch.isspace():
            i += 1                      # a space: skip it, no token
        elif ch.isdigit():
            start = i
            while i < len(text) and text[i].isdigit():
                i += 1                  # move past the whole number
            tokens.append(int(text[start:i]))
        elif ch in "+-":
            tokens.append(ch)
            i += 1
        else:
            raise ValueError("unexpected character")
    return tokens

tokenize("12 + 3")        # [12, '+', 3]
```

Step by step:

- `ch.isspace()` is true for a space (or a tab or newline). Spaces make no token; the finger moves on by one.
- `ch.isdigit()` is true for `0` to `9`. A number can be several digits long, so remember where it began (`start = i`), move `i` forward while you still see digits, and then cut out the whole run with the slice `text[start:i]`. `int` turns the text `"12"` into the number `12`.
- `ch in "+-"` is true when `ch` is one of the characters in that string. An operator is one character, so it becomes a token by itself and `i` moves on by one.
- Anything else is a character the calculator does not understand.

## Numbers with a decimal point

Numbers like `4.5` have a dot. So a number may start with a digit *or* a dot (`.5` is a number too), and the number loop should accept dots as well as digits. Then look at the run you cut out before converting it:

- `"4.5".count(".")` is `1`, so it is a decimal number: `float("4.5")` gives `4.5`.
- `"12"` has no dot, so `int("12")` gives `12`.
- `"1.2.3"` has two dots, and `"."` alone has no digits at all. Neither is a number, so they are errors.

`float` also accepts a dot at either end: `float(".5")` is `0.5` and `float("5.")` is `5.0`.

## Always take the longest token

Python writes powers as `**`: `2 ** 3` is 8. So when you see `*`, you cannot decide yet. It might be a multiplication, or the first half of a power. You have to peek at the next character too.

The rule tokenizers follow is called **[[maximal munch|maximal-munch]]**: always take the *longest* token that fits. `123` is one number, not three. `**` is one power operator, not two multiplications.

A neat way to peek is `text.startswith("**", i)`. It asks: "does the text, starting at position `i`, begin with `**`?"

```python
"2**3".startswith("**", 1)     # True: position 1 starts "**"
"2**3".startswith("**", 0)     # False: position 0 is "2"
```

Check for `**` *before* you check for a single `*`. Otherwise the single `*` wins first and the power is split in two. When you find `**`, move `i` on by 2, not 1.

## Errors that say what and where

When a character does not start any token, raise an error that says *what* the character was and *where* it is:

```python
ch = "$"
i = 4
raise ValueError(f"unexpected character {ch!r} at position {i}")
# ValueError: unexpected character '$' at position 4
```

The [[`!r` inside the braces|repr-quotes]] puts quotes around the character, so you can see exactly what it was. The position is the index `i`, which [[counts from 0|position-zero]]. A message like this is the difference between a tool people can use and one they give up on.

**Watch out:** do not move along by chopping the text, as in `text = text[1:]`. It gives the right tokens, but every chop copies the whole rest of the string. For long input that makes the tokenizer [[quadratic|quadratic-copies]]: twice the text, four times the work. Move the index `i` instead; the text itself never changes.

::: context interpreters-too Python tokenizes your code too
An **interpreter** reads a program and carries it out; a **compiler** translates a program into another form, often machine code, to be run later. Python is usually run by an interpreter, and the very first thing it does with your file is tokenize it. The standard library even lets you watch: the `tokenize` module turns the line `2**3*4` into the pieces `'2'`, `'**'`, `'3'`, `'*'`, `'4'`, plus markers for the end of the line and the end of the file. Your calculator follows the same plan, only smaller.
:::

::: context token-word Tokens, and the other names for this stage
A **token** is one small meaningful piece, the way a word or a comma is in a sentence. The word comes from an old English word for a sign or symbol, and it is still used for things that stand for something else, like a bus token. Programmers also call this stage **lexing** or **scanning**, and a tokenizer a **lexer**, from "lexical", which means "to do with words". All three names mean the same job: turning a stream of characters into a list of tokens.
:::

::: context maximal-munch Taking the biggest bite
"Munch" is the picture: at each position the tokenizer takes the biggest bite that is still a real token. Without the rule, `2**3` could be read two ways, and only one is right:

```svg

  <text x="10" y="38" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44">right</text>
  <rect x="70" y="18" width="40" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="118" y="18" width="70" height="30" fill="#8fb8f0" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="196" y="18" width="40" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#1d6fd1" stroke-width="2"/>

  <text x="153" y="39" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">**</text>
  <text x="216" y="39" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">3</text>
  <text x="250" y="38" font-size="12" fill="#1d6fd1">power: 8</text>
  <text x="10" y="98" font-size="13" fill="#1f2a44">wrong</text>
  <rect x="70" y="78" width="40" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="118" y="78" width="31" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="157" y="78" width="31" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <rect x="196" y="78" width="40" height="30" fill="#ffffff" stroke="#b4232c" stroke-width="2"/>
  <text x="90" y="99" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">2</text>
  <text x="133" y="99" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">*</text>
  <text x="172" y="99" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">*</text>
  <text x="216" y="99" font-size="15" text-anchor="middle" fill="#1f2a44">3</text>
```
