+++ practice | not, and, or, and refusing nonsense
--- task
Write `evaluate_logic(tokens)` for a list of word tokens like `["not", "false", "and", "true"]`, returning `True` or `False`. Use this grammar, one method per rule:

```
expr   := term   ("or" term)*
term   := unary  ("and" unary)*
unary  := "not" unary  |  atom
atom   := "true"  |  "false"  |  "(" expr ")"
```

So `not` binds tightest, then `and`, then `or`, the same order Python uses. `not` can repeat: `not not true` is `True`.

Refuse nonsense the way the lesson does, with `ValueError`: an `atom` that gets anything but `"true"`, `"false"` or `"("` (including running out of tokens), a bracket that is not closed, and tokens left over at the end. So `[]`, `["true", "and"]`, `["(", "true"]`, `["true", ")"]` and `["true", "false"]` all raise `ValueError`. Do not use `eval`.
--- starter
def evaluate_logic(tokens):
    return tokens[-1] == "true"
--- solution
class LogicParser:
    def __init__(self, tokens):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def expr(self):
        value = self.term()
        while self.peek() == "or":
            self.take()
            right = self.term()
            value = value or right
        return value

    def term(self):
        value = self.unary()
        while self.peek() == "and":
            self.take()
            right = self.unary()
            value = value and right
        return value

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == "not":
            self.take()
            return not self.unary()
        return self.atom()

    def atom(self):
        token = self.take()
        if token == "(":
            value = self.expr()
            if self.take() != ")":
                raise ValueError("expected )")
            return value
        if token in ("true", "false"):
            return token == "true"
        raise ValueError(f"expected true, false or (, got {token!r}")


def evaluate_logic(tokens):
    parser = LogicParser(tokens)
    value = parser.expr()
    if parser.peek() is not None:
        raise ValueError(f"unexpected {parser.peek()!r}")
    return value
--- hint
`unary` is the lesson's `unary` with `"not"` in place of `"-"`: if the next token is `"not"`, take it and return `not self.unary()`; otherwise return `self.atom()`. `term` combines `unary` pieces with `and`.
--- hint
The three checks: `atom` raises unless the token is `"true"`, `"false"` or `"("`; after the inner `expr`, `atom` raises unless the token it takes is `")"`; and `evaluate_logic` raises if `peek()` is not `None` once `expr()` has returned.
--- check case | not binds tighter than and
evaluate_logic(["not", "false", "and", "true"])
=> True
--- check test | Repeated not, and not in front of a bracket
evaluate_logic(["not", "not", "true"]) is True and evaluate_logic(["not", "(", "true", "or", "false", ")"]) is False
--- check test | Agrees with Python on a batch of expressions
all(evaluate_logic(t.split()) == eval(t.replace("true", "True").replace("false", "False")) for t in ["false or not false and false", "not true or not false", "not ( true and false ) and true", "true and not not false", "not false and not false or false"])
--- check test | Nonsense raises ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: evaluate_logic(t)) for t in [[], ["true", "and"], ["(", "true"], ["true", ")"], ["not"], ["true", "false"], ["and", "true"], ["(", ")"]])
--- check source absent | Does not use eval
\beval\s*\(

+++ practice | Build the whole tree
--- task
Write `to_tree(tokens)`, which parses a list of tokens with the lesson's five-rule grammar and returns a tree instead of a value. The tokens are numbers and the strings `"+"`, `"-"`, `"*"`, `"/"`, `"**"`, `"("` and `")"`; you do not need to check for mistakes.

- A number stays a number.
- A binary operation becomes `(operator, left, right)`, like `("+", 2, 3)`.
- A unary minus becomes `("neg", operand)`, like `("neg", 3)`.
- Brackets do not appear in the tree.

The tree shows exactly how the grammar groups things. For example, `to_tree([2, "**", 3, "**", 2])` gives `("**", 2, ("**", 3, 2))`, and `to_tree(["-", 2, "**", 2])` gives `("neg", ("**", 2, 2))`.
--- starter
def to_tree(tokens):
    return tokens[0]
--- solution
class TreeParser:
    def __init__(self, tokens):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def expr(self):
        tree = self.term()
        while self.peek() in ("+", "-"):
            op = self.take()
            tree = (op, tree, self.term())
        return tree

    def term(self):
        tree = self.unary()
        while self.peek() in ("*", "/"):
            op = self.take()
            tree = (op, tree, self.unary())
        return tree

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == "-":
            self.take()
            return ("neg", self.unary())
        return self.power()

    def power(self):
        base = self.atom()
        if self.peek() == "**":
            self.take()
            return ("**", base, self.unary())
        return base

    def atom(self):
        token = self.take()
        if token == "(":
            tree = self.expr()
            self.take()
            return tree
        return token


def to_tree(tokens):
    return TreeParser(tokens).expr()
--- hint
Take the lesson's five methods and change what each one returns: instead of computing `value + right` or `-self.unary()` or `base ** exponent`, build the tuple that describes it.
--- hint
`unary` returns `("neg", self.unary())` after a minus. `power` returns `("**", base, self.unary())` when it sees `"**"`. `expr` and `term` fold with `tree = (op, tree, right_side)` inside their `while` loops.
--- check case | Powers group from the right
to_tree([2, "**", 3, "**", 2])
=> ("**", 2, ("**", 3, 2))
--- check case | The minus goes around the power
to_tree(["-", 2, "**", 2])
=> ("neg", ("**", 2, 2))
--- check case | A minus inside the exponent
to_tree([2, "**", "-", 1])
=> ("**", 2, ("neg", 1))
--- check case | Two minuses
to_tree(["-", "-", 3])
=> ("neg", ("neg", 3))
--- check case | A minus after a times sign
to_tree([2, "*", "-", 3, "+", 1])
=> ("+", ("*", 2, ("neg", 3)), 1)
--- check case | Brackets put the minus inside
to_tree(["(", "-", 2, ")", "**", 2])
=> ("**", ("neg", 2), 2)

+++ practice | A calculator that answers every line
--- task
The starter holds the tokenizer and the finished calculator from the lesson, as `evaluate(text)`. Put a friendly layer around it:

- `respond(line)` returns `"= <value>"` when the line works, like `"= 14"` or `"= 0.5"`. When `evaluate` raises `ValueError`, it returns `"error: "` followed by the error's message. When it raises `ZeroDivisionError`, it returns `"error: division by zero"`. A blank line returns `""`.
- `run(lines, out)` calls `out(respond(line))` for every line that is not blank, and returns how many of those lines were errors. It must not print anything itself.

For example, `respond("3 $")` gives `"error: unexpected character '$' at position 2"`.
--- starter
def tokenize(text):
    tokens = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(text):
        ch = text[i]
        if ch.isspace():
            i += 1
        elif ch.isdigit() or ch == ".":
            start = i
            while i < len(text) and (text[i].isdigit() or text[i] == "."):
                i += 1
            number = text[start:i]
            if number.count(".") > 1 or number == ".":
                raise ValueError(f"bad number {number!r} at position {start}")
            tokens.append(float(number) if "." in number else int(number))
        elif text.startswith("**", i):
            tokens.append("**")
            i += 2
        elif ch in "+-*/()":
            tokens.append(ch)
            i += 1
        else:
            raise ValueError(f"unexpected character {ch!r} at position {i}")
    return tokens


class Parser:
    def __init__(self, tokens):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def expr(self):
        value = self.term()
        while self.peek() in ("+", "-"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.term()
            value = value + right if op == "+" else value - right
        return value

    def term(self):
        value = self.unary()
        while self.peek() in ("*", "/"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.unary()
            value = value * right if op == "*" else value / right
        return value

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == "-":
            self.take()
            return -self.unary()
        return self.power()

    def power(self):
        base = self.atom()
        if self.peek() == "**":
            self.take()
            return base ** self.unary()
        return base

    def atom(self):
        token = self.take()
        if token == "(":
            value = self.expr()
            if self.take() != ")":
                raise ValueError("expected )")
            return value
        if isinstance(token, (int, float)):
            return token
        raise ValueError(f"expected a number, got {token!r}")


def evaluate(text):
    parser = Parser(tokenize(text))
    value = parser.expr()
    if parser.peek() is not None:
        raise ValueError(f"unexpected {parser.peek()!r}")
    return value
--- solution
def tokenize(text):
    tokens = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(text):
        ch = text[i]
        if ch.isspace():
            i += 1
        elif ch.isdigit() or ch == ".":
            start = i
            while i < len(text) and (text[i].isdigit() or text[i] == "."):
                i += 1
            number = text[start:i]
            if number.count(".") > 1 or number == ".":
                raise ValueError(f"bad number {number!r} at position {start}")
            tokens.append(float(number) if "." in number else int(number))
        elif text.startswith("**", i):
            tokens.append("**")
            i += 2
        elif ch in "+-*/()":
            tokens.append(ch)
            i += 1
        else:
            raise ValueError(f"unexpected character {ch!r} at position {i}")
    return tokens


class Parser:
    def __init__(self, tokens):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def expr(self):
        value = self.term()
        while self.peek() in ("+", "-"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.term()
            value = value + right if op == "+" else value - right
        return value

    def term(self):
        value = self.unary()
        while self.peek() in ("*", "/"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.unary()
            value = value * right if op == "*" else value / right
        return value

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == "-":
            self.take()
            return -self.unary()
        return self.power()

    def power(self):
        base = self.atom()
        if self.peek() == "**":
            self.take()
            return base ** self.unary()
        return base

    def atom(self):
        token = self.take()
        if token == "(":
            value = self.expr()
            if self.take() != ")":
                raise ValueError("expected )")
            return value
        if isinstance(token, (int, float)):
            return token
        raise ValueError(f"expected a number, got {token!r}")


def evaluate(text):
    parser = Parser(tokenize(text))
    value = parser.expr()
    if parser.peek() is not None:
        raise ValueError(f"unexpected {parser.peek()!r}")
    return value


def respond(line):
    if not line.strip():
        return ""
    try:
        value = evaluate(line)
    except ValueError as err:
        return f"error: {err}"
    except ZeroDivisionError:
        return "error: division by zero"
    return f"= {value}"


def run(lines, out):
    errors = 0
    for line in lines:
        if not line.strip():
            continue
        answer = respond(line)
        if answer.startswith("error:"):
            errors += 1
        out(answer)
    return errors
--- hint
`respond` is a `try` around one call, `evaluate(line)`, with one `except` for each kind of error: `except ValueError as err:` lets you put `err` in an f-string to get its message.
--- hint
`run` skips blank lines, hands each answer to `out`, and counts the answers that start with `"error:"`. Because `out` is handed in, a test can collect the answers with `sent.append`.
--- check case | A good line
respond("2 + 3 * 4")
=> "= 14"
--- check case | A power with a negative exponent
respond("2 ** -1")
=> "= 0.5"
--- check case | Dividing by zero
respond("1 / 0")
=> "error: division by zero"
--- check case | A bad character, with the tokenizer's message
respond("3 $")
=> "error: unexpected character '$' at position 2"
--- check case | A blank line
respond("   ")
=> ""
--- check case | run answers every line that is not blank, and counts the errors
(lambda sent: (run(["1 + 1", "", "(2", "4 / 0", "-2 ** 2"], sent.append), sent))([])
=> (2, ["= 2", "error: expected )", "error: division by zero", "= -4"])

+++ practice | Refusing huge powers
--- task
A calculator on a shared server must not let someone type `9 ** 9 ** 9` and keep it busy for hours: that number has more than 369 million digits. Write `evaluate(text)`, the lesson's full calculator (the starter has the tokenizer), with one extra rule: a power whose exponent is more than 1000, or less than -1000, raises `ValueError`, and this is checked **before** the power is computed. Get the edge cases right:

- `2 ** 1000` is allowed, and exact.
- `2 ** 1001` and `2 ** -1001` raise `ValueError`.
- `9 ** 9 ** 9` groups from the right, so `9 ** 9` (which is 387,420,489) is worked out first. The outer power then has a huge exponent, so it must raise `ValueError` at once.
- `0 ** 0` is `1`, `(-8) ** 2` is `64`, and `2 ** 0.5` is allowed.
- Everything else works as in the lesson, including `ValueError` for malformed input.
--- starter
def tokenize(text):
    tokens = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(text):
        ch = text[i]
        if ch.isspace():
            i += 1
        elif ch.isdigit() or ch == ".":
            start = i
            while i < len(text) and (text[i].isdigit() or text[i] == "."):
                i += 1
            number = text[start:i]
            if number.count(".") > 1 or number == ".":
                raise ValueError(f"bad number {number!r} at position {start}")
            tokens.append(float(number) if "." in number else int(number))
        elif text.startswith("**", i):
            tokens.append("**")
            i += 2
        elif ch in "+-*/()":
            tokens.append(ch)
            i += 1
        else:
            raise ValueError(f"unexpected character {ch!r} at position {i}")
    return tokens


def evaluate(text):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def tokenize(text):
    tokens = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(text):
        ch = text[i]
        if ch.isspace():
            i += 1
        elif ch.isdigit() or ch == ".":
            start = i
            while i < len(text) and (text[i].isdigit() or text[i] == "."):
                i += 1
            number = text[start:i]
            if number.count(".") > 1 or number == ".":
                raise ValueError(f"bad number {number!r} at position {start}")
            tokens.append(float(number) if "." in number else int(number))
        elif text.startswith("**", i):
            tokens.append("**")
            i += 2
        elif ch in "+-*/()":
            tokens.append(ch)
            i += 1
        else:
            raise ValueError(f"unexpected character {ch!r} at position {i}")
    return tokens


MAX_EXPONENT = 1000


class Parser:
    def __init__(self, tokens):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def expr(self):
        value = self.term()
        while self.peek() in ("+", "-"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.term()
            value = value + right if op == "+" else value - right
        return value

    def term(self):
        value = self.unary()
        while self.peek() in ("*", "/"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.unary()
            value = value * right if op == "*" else value / right
        return value

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == "-":
            self.take()
            return -self.unary()
        return self.power()

    def power(self):
        base = self.atom()
        if self.peek() == "**":
            self.take()
            exponent = self.unary()
            if abs(exponent) > MAX_EXPONENT:
                raise ValueError(f"exponent {exponent} is too large")
            return base ** exponent
        return base

    def atom(self):
        token = self.take()
        if token == "(":
            value = self.expr()
            if self.take() != ")":
                raise ValueError("expected )")
            return value
        if isinstance(token, (int, float)):
            return token
        raise ValueError(f"expected a number, got {token!r}")


def evaluate(text):
    parser = Parser(tokenize(text))
    value = parser.expr()
    if parser.peek() is not None:
        raise ValueError(f"unexpected {parser.peek()!r}")
    return value
--- hint
Build the lesson's calculator first. The new rule lives in one place: `power`, between parsing the exponent and using it.
--- hint
In `power`, after `self.take()` of the `"**"`, store `exponent = self.unary()`. Raise `ValueError` if `abs(exponent) > 1000`, and only then `return base ** exponent`. The inner power of a tower is parsed (and checked) first, because the exponent's `unary` reaches it before the outer `**` is computed.
--- check test | 2 ** 1000 is allowed and exact
evaluate("2 ** 1000") == 2 ** 1000
--- check test | Exponents beyond 1000 either way are refused
all(raises(ValueError, lambda t=t: evaluate(t)) for t in ["2 ** 1001", "2 ** -1001", "10 ** (500 * 3)"])
--- check test | 9 ** 9 ** 9 is refused at once
raises(ValueError, lambda: evaluate("9 ** 9 ** 9"))
?? Check the exponent before computing the power. The inner 9 ** 9 is fine; the outer power is not.
--- check test | Small edge cases
evaluate("0 ** 0") == 1 and evaluate("(-8) ** 2") == 64 and abs(evaluate("2 ** 0.5") - 2 ** 0.5) < 1e-12
--- check test | The lesson's rules still hold
evaluate("2 ** 3 ** 2") == 512 and evaluate("-2 ** 2") == -4 and evaluate("2 * (3 + 4) - -1") == 15 and evaluate("100 / 10 / 5") == 2.0
--- check test | Malformed input raises ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda e=e: evaluate(e)) for e in ["", "2 +", "(1 + 2", "1 + 2)", "1 2", "3 ** "])

+++ practice | Debug: the minus that grabbed too much
--- task
This parser takes a list of tokens and gets most of the lesson's rules right: `2 ** 3 ** 2` is `512` and `2 ** -1` is `0.5`. But `-2 ** 2` gives `4` instead of `-4`, and `-3 ** 2` gives `9` instead of `-9`. The minus is being applied before the power, instead of to the power's result. Find the bug and fix it, so the methods follow the lesson's order: `term` uses `unary`, `unary` uses `power`, and `power` uses `atom`.
--- starter
class Parser:
    def __init__(self, tokens):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def expr(self):
        value = self.term()
        while self.peek() in ("+", "-"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.term()
            value = value + right if op == "+" else value - right
        return value

    def term(self):
        value = self.power()
        while self.peek() in ("*", "/"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.power()
            value = value * right if op == "*" else value / right
        return value

    def power(self):
        base = self.unary()
        if self.peek() == "**":
            self.take()
            return base ** self.power()
        return base

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == "-":
            self.take()
            return -self.unary()
        return self.atom()

    def atom(self):
        token = self.take()
        if token == "(":
            value = self.expr()
            self.take()
            return value
        return token


def evaluate(tokens):
    return Parser(tokens).expr()
--- solution
class Parser:
    def __init__(self, tokens):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def expr(self):
        value = self.term()
        while self.peek() in ("+", "-"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.term()
            value = value + right if op == "+" else value - right
        return value

    def term(self):
        value = self.unary()
        while self.peek() in ("*", "/"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.unary()
            value = value * right if op == "*" else value / right
        return value

    def power(self):
        base = self.atom()
        if self.peek() == "**":
            self.take()
            return base ** self.unary()
        return base

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == "-":
            self.take()
            return -self.unary()
        return self.power()

    def atom(self):
        token = self.take()
        if token == "(":
            value = self.expr()
            self.take()
            return value
        return token


def evaluate(tokens):
    return Parser(tokens).expr()
--- hint
Follow `["-", 2, "**", 2]`: `term` calls `power`, which asks `unary` for the base. What does `unary` hand back, and what is then raised to the power 2?
--- hint
The rule that is worked out last must sit higher up. The minus is worked out after the power, so `unary` must call `power`, and `power` must call `atom` for its base and `unary` for its exponent. `term` then calls `unary`.
--- check case | The minus goes around the power
evaluate(["-", 2, "**", 2])
=> -4
--- check case | And again
evaluate(["-", 3, "**", 2])
=> -9
--- check test | Brackets put the minus inside
evaluate(["(", "-", 2, ")", "**", 2]) == 4
--- check test | The other rules still hold
evaluate([2, "**", 3, "**", 2]) == 512 and evaluate([2, "**", "-", 1]) == 0.5 and evaluate(["-", "-", 3]) == 3 and evaluate([2, "*", "-", 3]) == -6
--- check case | A minus on both sides of a power
evaluate(["-", 2, "**", "-", 2])
=> -0.25

+++ practice | Factorials: an operator that comes after
--- task
In maths, `5!` ("five factorial") is `5 × 4 × 3 × 2 × 1 = 120`, and `0!` is `1`. The `!` comes **after** its number, and it binds tighter than everything else. Write `evaluate(text)`: the lesson's full calculator, plus `!`, using this grammar:

```
expr     := term     (("+" | "-") term)*
term     := unary    (("*" | "/") unary)*
unary    := "-" unary  |  power
power    := postfix  ("**" unary)?
postfix  := atom  "!"*
atom     := NUMBER  |  "(" expr ")"
```

- Add `!` to the tokenizer in the starter.
- `postfix` reads an atom, then applies `!` once for each `!` that follows: `3!!` is `(3!)!`, which is `720`.
- So `2 ** 3!` is `2 ** 6 = 64`, `-3!` is `-6`, and `(1 + 2)!` is `6`.
- The factorial of anything that is not an `int` of 0 or more raises `ValueError`: `2.5!`, `(-3)!`, and `(6 / 2)!` too, because `/` always gives a `float`.
- Everything else works as in the lesson, including `ValueError` for malformed input, like a `!` with no number before it.
--- starter
def tokenize(text):
    tokens = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(text):
        ch = text[i]
        if ch.isspace():
            i += 1
        elif ch.isdigit() or ch == ".":
            start = i
            while i < len(text) and (text[i].isdigit() or text[i] == "."):
                i += 1
            number = text[start:i]
            if number.count(".") > 1 or number == ".":
                raise ValueError(f"bad number {number!r} at position {start}")
            tokens.append(float(number) if "." in number else int(number))
        elif text.startswith("**", i):
            tokens.append("**")
            i += 2
        elif ch in "+-*/()":
            tokens.append(ch)
            i += 1
        else:
            raise ValueError(f"unexpected character {ch!r} at position {i}")
    return tokens


def evaluate(text):
    raise NotImplementedError
--- solution
def tokenize(text):
    tokens = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(text):
        ch = text[i]
        if ch.isspace():
            i += 1
        elif ch.isdigit() or ch == ".":
            start = i
            while i < len(text) and (text[i].isdigit() or text[i] == "."):
                i += 1
            number = text[start:i]
            if number.count(".") > 1 or number == ".":
                raise ValueError(f"bad number {number!r} at position {start}")
            tokens.append(float(number) if "." in number else int(number))
        elif text.startswith("**", i):
            tokens.append("**")
            i += 2
        elif ch in "+-*/()!":
            tokens.append(ch)
            i += 1
        else:
            raise ValueError(f"unexpected character {ch!r} at position {i}")
    return tokens


def factorial(n):
    if not isinstance(n, int) or n < 0:
        raise ValueError(f"factorial needs a whole number, 0 or more, not {n!r}")
    result = 1
    for k in range(2, n + 1):
        result *= k
    return result


class Parser:
    def __init__(self, tokens):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def expr(self):
        value = self.term()
        while self.peek() in ("+", "-"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.term()
            value = value + right if op == "+" else value - right
        return value

    def term(self):
        value = self.unary()
        while self.peek() in ("*", "/"):
            op = self.take()
            right = self.unary()
            value = value * right if op == "*" else value / right
        return value

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == "-":
            self.take()
            return -self.unary()
        return self.power()

    def power(self):
        base = self.postfix()
        if self.peek() == "**":
            self.take()
            return base ** self.unary()
        return base

    def postfix(self):
        value = self.atom()
        while self.peek() == "!":
            self.take()
            value = factorial(value)
        return value

    def atom(self):
        token = self.take()
        if token == "(":
            value = self.expr()
            if self.take() != ")":
                raise ValueError("expected )")
            return value
        if isinstance(token, (int, float)):
            return token
        raise ValueError(f"expected a number, got {token!r}")


def evaluate(text):
    parser = Parser(tokenize(text))
    value = parser.expr()
    if parser.peek() is not None:
        raise ValueError(f"unexpected {parser.peek()!r}")
    return value
--- hint
`postfix` has the shape of `expr` with nothing on the right: read an atom, then `while self.peek() == "!"`, take the `!` and replace the value with its factorial. `power` now reads its base with `self.postfix()` instead of `self.atom()`.
--- hint
Write the factorial as its own small function: refuse anything that is not an `int` of 0 or more, then multiply 2, 3, … up to `n` together, starting from 1. A lone `!` reaches `atom`, which already refuses a token that is not a number or `"("`.
--- check test | Factorials, and a factorial of a factorial
evaluate("3!") == 6 and evaluate("0!") == 1 and evaluate("5!") == 120 and evaluate("3!!") == 720
--- check test | ! binds tighter than **, unary minus and brackets around it

--- check test | Mixed with the other operators

--- check test | A ! with nothing before it, and other nonsense, is refused

--- check test | The lesson's rules still hold

+++ problem | A rate limiter with a clock you control
--- task
Write `rate_limit(max_calls, per_seconds, clock)`, a decorator with arguments. `clock()` returns the current time in seconds, and the wrapper calls it exactly once per call.

- A call is allowed when fewer than `max_calls` **allowed** calls happened in the last `per_seconds` seconds: that is, at times `t` with `now - t < per_seconds`. An allowed call runs the function and returns its result.
- A refused call raises `RuntimeError("rate limit exceeded")`. It does not run the function, and it does not count as a call.
- Each decorated function keeps its own history. Use `functools.wraps`, and pass every argument through.
- It must stay quick for 100,000 calls, so forget calls that have left the window.

For example, with `rate_limit(3, 10, clock)` and calls at times 0, 1, 2, 3 and 10, the call at time 3 is refused (three calls in the last 10 seconds), and the call at time 10 is allowed, because the call at time 0 has left the window.
--- starter
import functools


def rate_limit(max_calls, per_seconds, clock):
    def decorator(func):
        return func
    return decorator
--- solution
import functools
from collections import deque


def rate_limit(max_calls, per_seconds, clock):
    def decorator(func):
        recent = deque()

        @functools.wraps(func)
        def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
            now = clock()
            while recent and now - recent[0] >= per_seconds:
                recent.popleft()
            if len(recent) >= max_calls:
                raise RuntimeError("rate limit exceeded")
            recent.append(now)
            return func(*args, **kwargs)

        return wrapper
    return decorator
--- check case | The fourth call in the window is refused, and a later one allowed
(lambda t: (lambda f: ["refused" if raises(RuntimeError, lambda: f(-1)) else "ok" for _ in range(5)])(rate_limit(3, 10, lambda: next(t))(abs)))(iter([0, 1, 2, 3, 10]))
=> ["ok", "ok", "ok", "refused", "ok"]
--- check test | A refused call does not run the function, or count
(lambda log, t: (lambda f: ([raises(RuntimeError, f) for _ in range(4)], len(log))[1])(rate_limit(1, 5, lambda: next(t))(lambda: log.append(1))))([], iter([0, 1, 2, 5])) == 2
--- check case | Exactly per_seconds later is allowed again, just before is not
(lambda t: (lambda f: [raises(RuntimeError, f) for _ in range(3)])(rate_limit(1, 5, lambda: next(t))(lambda: None)))(iter([0, 4.9, 5]))
=> [False, True, False]
--- check test | Two decorated functions keep separate histories
(lambda f, g: (f(1), raises(RuntimeError, lambda: f(1)), g(-3))[2] == 3)(rate_limit(1, 60, lambda: 0)(abs), rate_limit(1, 60, lambda: 0)(abs))
--- check test | Keeps the name, passes keywords through, returns the result
(lambda f: f.__name__ == "sorted" and f([3, 1, 2], reverse=True) == [3, 2, 1])(rate_limit(5, 1, lambda: 0)(sorted))
--- check test | The clock is read once per call
(lambda calls: (lambda f: ([raises(RuntimeError, f) for _ in range(4)], len(calls))[1])(rate_limit(2, 100, lambda: calls.append(1) or len(calls))(lambda: None)))([]) == 4
--- check case | 100,000 calls, ten allowed in every 100 time units
(lambda t: (lambda f: sum(1 for _ in range(100000) if not raises(RuntimeError, f)))(rate_limit(10, 100, lambda: next(t))(lambda: None)))(iter(range(100000)))
=> 10000

+++ problem | Models with checked fields
--- task
Build a tiny data-model toolkit from a descriptor and a class hook.

- `Field(kind)`: a data descriptor. It learns its name with `__set_name__`, stores values in the object's `__dict__` under that name, returns itself when read from the class, and raises `TypeError` when a value that is not an instance of `kind` is assigned, when the object is made or later.
- `Model`: a base class with a class attribute `fields = ()`. Its `__init_subclass__` sets `cls.fields` to the parent's fields followed by the names of the `Field` attributes in the subclass's own body, in the order they are written, as a tuple.
- `Model.__init__(self, **values)` takes exactly one keyword argument per field. A missing field or an unknown keyword raises `TypeError`. Each value is assigned, so its `Field` checks it.
- `to_dict()` returns a dict from each field name to its value, in field order.
- `__repr__` gives the class name and the fields, like `"Rocket(name='Atlas', stages=2)"`.

For example, after `class Rocket(Model):` with `name = Field(str)` and `stages = Field(int)`, `Rocket.fields` is `("name", "stages")`. A subclass of `Rocket` with `crew = Field(int)` has the fields `("name", "stages", "crew")`. The checks make their own models, so you only need `Field` and `Model`.
--- starter
class Field:
    def __init__(self, kind):
        self.kind = kind


class Model:
    fields = ()
--- solution
class Field:
    def __init__(self, kind):
        self.kind = kind

    def __set_name__(self, owner, name):
        self.name = name

    def __get__(self, obj, owner):
        if obj is None:
            return self
        return obj.__dict__[self.name]

    def __set__(self, obj, value):
        if not isinstance(value, self.kind):
            raise TypeError(f"{self.name} must be {self.kind.__name__}")
        obj.__dict__[self.name] = value


class Model:
    fields = ()

    def __init_subclass__(cls, **kwargs):
        super().__init_subclass__(**kwargs)
        own = tuple(name for name, value in vars(cls).items() if isinstance(value, Field))
        cls.fields = cls.fields + own

    def __init__(self, **values):
        missing = [name for name in self.fields if name not in values]
        unknown = [name for name in values if name not in self.fields]
        if missing or unknown:
            raise TypeError(f"missing fields {missing}, unknown fields {unknown}")
        for name in self.fields:
            setattr(self, name, values[name])

    def to_dict(self):
        return {name: getattr(self, name) for name in self.fields}

    def __repr__(self):
        inside = ", ".join(f"{name}={getattr(self, name)!r}" for name in self.fields)
        return f"{type(self).__name__}({inside})"
--- check test | The hook lists the fields in order, and skips other attributes
(lambda R: R.fields == ("name", "stages") and Model.fields == ())(type("Rocket", (Model,), {"name": Field(str), "note": "x", "stages": Field(int)}))
--- check case | repr and to_dict
(lambda R: (lambda r: (repr(r), r.to_dict()))(R(name="Atlas", stages=2)))(type("Rocket", (Model,), {"name": Field(str), "stages": Field(int)}))
=> ("Rocket(name='Atlas', stages=2)", {"name": "Atlas", "stages": 2})
--- check test | Wrong types raise TypeError, when made and later
(lambda R: raises(TypeError, lambda: R(name="A", stages="2")) and (lambda r: raises(TypeError, lambda: setattr(r, "stages", 2.5)) and r.stages == 1)(R(name="A", stages=1)))(type("Rocket", (Model,), {"name": Field(str), "stages": Field(int)}))
--- check test | Missing and unknown keywords raise TypeError
(lambda R: raises(TypeError, lambda: R(name="A")) and raises(TypeError, lambda: R(name="A", stages=1, color="red")))(type("Rocket", (Model,), {"name": Field(str), "stages": Field(int)}))
--- check test | A subclass adds its fields after its parent's
(lambda R: (lambda C: C.fields == ("name", "stages", "crew") and R.fields == ("name", "stages") and repr(C(name="Soyuz", stages=3, crew=3)) == "C(name='Soyuz', stages=3, crew=3)")(type("C", (R,), {"crew": Field(int)})))(type("Rocket", (Model,), {"name": Field(str), "stages": Field(int)}))
--- check test | Values live in each object's own dict, and the class gives the descriptor
(lambda R: (lambda a, b: vars(a) == {"name": "A", "stages": 1} and b.name == "B" and isinstance(R.name, Field))(R(name="A", stages=1), R(name="B", stages=2)))(type("Rocket", (Model,), {"name": Field(str), "stages": Field(int)}))

+++ problem | A pipeline of coroutines
--- task
Build a push pipeline out of generators that receive values with `send`.

- `primed(func)`: a decorator for generator functions. Calling the decorated function makes the generator, runs it to its first `yield` with `next()`, and returns it, ready for `send`. Use `functools.wraps`.
- Four generators, each decorated with `@primed`, each looping forever and receiving one line at a time with `line = yield`:
  - `sink(out)` calls `out(line)` for every line.
  - `grep(word, target)` sends on to `target` only the lines that contain `word`.
  - `number(target)` sends on `f"{n}: {line}"`, where `n` counts the lines this `number` has received, from 1.
  - `broadcast(targets)` sends every line to each target in the list, in order.
- `push(lines, target)` sends each line to `target`, in order.

For example:

```
found = []
everything = []
pipeline = broadcast([grep("ERROR", number(sink(found.append))), sink(everything.append)])
push(["ok", "ERROR disk", "ERROR fan", "done"], pipeline)
```

leaves `["1: ERROR disk", "2: ERROR fan"]` in `found` and all four lines in `everything`.
--- starter
import functools


def primed(func):
    return func


def push(lines, target):
    pass
--- solution
import functools


def primed(func):
    @functools.wraps(func)
    def wrapper(*args, **kwargs):
        gen = func(*args, **kwargs)
        next(gen)
        return gen
    return wrapper


@primed
def sink(out):
    while True:
        line = yield
        out(line)


@primed
def grep(word, target):
    while True:
        line = yield
        if word in line:
            target.send(line)


@primed
def number(target):
    n = 0
    while True:
        line = yield
        n += 1
        target.send(f"{n}: {line}")


@primed
def broadcast(targets):
    while True:
        line = yield
        for target in targets:
            target.send(line)


def push(lines, target):
    for line in lines:
        target.send(line)
--- check case | The example pipeline
(lambda found, everything: (push(["ok", "ERROR disk", "ERROR fan", "done"], broadcast([grep("ERROR", number(sink(found.append))), sink(everything.append)])), found, everything)[1:])([], [])
=> (["1: ERROR disk", "2: ERROR fan"], ["ok", "ERROR disk", "ERROR fan", "done"])
--- check test | A primed generator takes a send straight away
(lambda out: (sink(out.append).send("hi"), out)[1])([]) == ["hi"]
--- check test | primed keeps the names
sink.__name__ == "sink" and grep.__name__ == "grep" and number.__name__ == "number" and broadcast.__name__ == "broadcast"
--- check test | Each number counts on its own
(lambda a, b: (lambda na, nb: (push(["x", "y"], na), push(["z"], nb), push(["w"], na), (a, b))[3])(number(sink(a.append)), number(sink(b.append))))([], []) == (["1: x", "2: y", "3: w"], ["1: z"])
--- check test | grep, then number: only matching lines are counted
(lambda out: (push(["go", "no", "go now", "stop"], grep("go", number(sink(out.append)))), out)[1])([]) == ["1: go", "2: go now"]
--- check test | primed works on any generator function
(lambda g: g.send(4))(primed(lambda: (yield (yield) * 10))()) == 40

+++ problem | A two-stage pipeline with queues
--- task
Images go through two stages: `fetch(name)` waits 0.1 seconds and returns `name + ".raw"`, and `decode(data)` waits 0.05 seconds and returns `data.upper()`. Both are in the starter, and they record the most calls ever running at once in `fetch_peak` and `decode_peak`. A name that starts with `"bad"` makes `fetch` raise `ConnectionError`.

Rewrite `pipeline(names, fetchers)`, a coroutine, to use two `asyncio.Queue`s:

- `fetchers` fetcher tasks take names from the first queue, fetch them, and put `(name, data)` on the second queue. A fetch that raises `ConnectionError` records the result `"failed"` for that name and puts nothing on the second queue; the fetcher carries on with the next name.
- **One** decoder task takes pairs from the second queue, decodes them, and records the result.
- Wait until the first queue is finished, then the second, then cancel every task.
- Return a dict from each name to its result, in the order of `names`.

Keep the code at the bottom of the starter. Done one image at a time, the six images take about 0.9 seconds; with three fetchers and a decoder working side by side, about 0.4.
--- starter
import asyncio
import time

fetching = 0
fetch_peak = 0
decoding = 0
decode_peak = 0


async def fetch(name):
    global fetching, fetch_peak
    fetching += 1
    fetch_peak = max(fetch_peak, fetching)
    try:
        await asyncio.sleep(0.1)
        if name.startswith("bad"):
            raise ConnectionError(name)
        return name + ".raw"
    finally:
        fetching -= 1


async def decode(data):
    global decoding, decode_peak
    decoding += 1
    decode_peak = max(decode_peak, decoding)
    await asyncio.sleep(0.05)
    decoding -= 1
    return data.upper()


async def pipeline(names, fetchers):
    results = {}
    for name in names:
        try:
            results[name] = await decode(await fetch(name))
        except ConnectionError:
            results[name] = "failed"
    return results


start = time.perf_counter()
results = await pipeline(["a", "b", "bad1", "c", "d", "e"], 3)
seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
nothing = await pipeline([], 2)
--- solution
import asyncio
import time

fetching = 0
fetch_peak = 0
decoding = 0
decode_peak = 0


async def fetch(name):
    global fetching, fetch_peak
    fetching += 1
    fetch_peak = max(fetch_peak, fetching)
    try:
        await asyncio.sleep(0.1)
        if name.startswith("bad"):
            raise ConnectionError(name)
        return name + ".raw"
    finally:
        fetching -= 1


async def decode(data):
    global decoding, decode_peak
    decoding += 1
    decode_peak = max(decode_peak, decoding)
    await asyncio.sleep(0.05)
    decoding -= 1
    return data.upper()


async def pipeline(names, fetchers):
    to_fetch = asyncio.Queue()
    to_decode = asyncio.Queue()
    results = {}

    async def fetcher():
        while True:
            name = await to_fetch.get()
            try:
                data = await fetch(name)
                await to_decode.put((name, data))
            except ConnectionError:
                results[name] = "failed"
            finally:
                to_fetch.task_done()

    async def decoder():
        while True:
            name, data = await to_decode.get()
            try:
                results[name] = await decode(data)
            finally:
                to_decode.task_done()

    tasks = [asyncio.create_task(fetcher()) for _ in range(fetchers)]
    tasks.append(asyncio.create_task(decoder()))
    for name in names:
        await to_fetch.put(name)
    await to_fetch.join()
    await to_decode.join()
    for task in tasks:
        task.cancel()
    return {name: results[name] for name in names}


start = time.perf_counter()
results = await pipeline(["a", "b", "bad1", "c", "d", "e"], 3)
seconds = time.perf_counter() - start
nothing = await pipeline([], 2)
--- check case | Every image, in the order of the names, with the failure recorded
results
=> {"a": "A.RAW", "b": "B.RAW", "bad1": "failed", "c": "C.RAW", "d": "D.RAW", "e": "E.RAW"}
--- check test | The keys come in the order of the names
list(results) == ["a", "b", "bad1", "c", "d", "e"]
--- check test | Three fetchers at once, and one decoder
fetch_peak == 3 and decode_peak == 1
--- check test | The stages overlapped (about 0.4s, not 0.9s)
seconds < 0.65
--- check case | No names
nothing
=> {}
--- check source | Uses asyncio queues
asyncio\.Queue\(

+++ problem | The busiest window
--- task
Write `busiest_window(times: list[float], width: float) -> tuple[float, int]`, with exactly those type hints. `times` is a list of event times sorted from earliest to latest, perhaps with repeats, and `width` is greater than 0.

A window starts at one of the event times `t` and covers the times from `t` up to, but **not including**, `t + width`. Return `(t, count)` for the window that holds the most events. If several windows tie, return the one that starts earliest. An empty list raises `ValueError`.

It must handle 200,000 events quickly, so count each window with `bisect` instead of looking at every event. For example, `busiest_window([0, 5, 5, 5, 9], 1)` gives `(5, 3)`.
--- starter
def busiest_window(times, width):
    return (times[0], len(times))
--- solution
import bisect


def busiest_window(times: list[float], width: float) -> tuple[float, int]:
    if not times:
        raise ValueError("no events")
    best_start = times[0]
    best_count = 0
    for i, start in enumerate(times):
        count = bisect.bisect_left(times, start + width) - i
        if count > best_count:
            best_start = start
            best_count = count
    return (best_start, best_count)
--- check case | Repeated times all count
busiest_window([0, 5, 5, 5, 9], 1)
=> (5, 3)
--- check case | A tie goes to the earliest window
busiest_window([1, 2, 3, 10, 11, 12, 13], 3)
=> (1, 3)
--- check case | The end of the window is not included
busiest_window([0, 10], 10)
=> (0, 1)
--- check test | One event, and no events
busiest_window([7.5], 2) == (7.5, 1) and raises(ValueError, lambda: busiest_window([], 1))
--- check case | 200,000 events
busiest_window(sorted([i * 3 for i in range(100000)] + [150000 + i / 1000 for i in range(100000)]), 50)
=> (150000, 50017)
--- check test | The hints are exactly as asked
__import__("typing").get_type_hints(busiest_window) == {"times": list[float], "width": float, "return": tuple[float, int]}

+++ problem | Test a window merger
--- task
`merge_windows(windows)` takes a list of `(start, end)` tuples in any order. It returns a **new** list of tuples, sorted by start, in which every group of overlapping windows has been merged into one. Windows that only touch, like `(1, 4)` and `(4, 5)`, count as overlapping. A window inside another one disappears into it. The list passed in must not be changed, and no windows give `[]`.

Write `test_merge_windows(merge_windows)`. It must pass for a correct `merge_windows`, and raise `AssertionError` for every buggy one the checker tries.
--- starter
def test_merge_windows(merge_windows):
    assert merge_windows([(1, 3), (2, 6)]) == [(1, 6)]
--- solution
def test_merge_windows(merge_windows):
    assert merge_windows([]) == []
    assert merge_windows([(1, 3), (2, 6), (8, 10)]) == [(1, 6), (8, 10)]
    # any order in, sorted out
    assert merge_windows([(8, 10), (1, 3), (2, 6)]) == [(1, 6), (8, 10)]
    assert merge_windows([(5, 6), (1, 2)]) == [(1, 2), (5, 6)]
    # touching windows merge
    assert merge_windows([(1, 4), (4, 5)]) == [(1, 5)]
    # a window inside another one
    assert merge_windows([(1, 10), (2, 3)]) == [(1, 10)]
    assert merge_windows([(1, 10), (2, 3), (4, 12)]) == [(1, 12)]
    # the list passed in is left alone
    windows = [(3, 4), (1, 2)]
    merge_windows(windows)
    assert windows == [(3, 4), (1, 2)]

--- check test | Catches one that does not sort
(lambda g: (exec("def merge(windows):\n    merged = []\n    for start, end in windows:\n        if merged and start <= merged[-1][1]:\n            merged[-1] = (merged[-1][0], max(merged[-1][1], end))\n        else:\n            merged.append((start, end))\n    return merged\n", g), raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_merge_windows(g["merge"])))[1])({})
--- check test | Catches one that does not merge touching windows
(lambda g: (exec("def merge(windows):\n    merged = []\n    for start, end in sorted(windows):\n        if merged and start < merged[-1][1]:\n            merged[-1] = (merged[-1][0], max(merged[-1][1], end))\n        else:\n            merged.append((start, end))\n    return merged\n", g), raises(AssertionError, lambda: test_merge_windows(g["merge"])))[1])({})

+++ problem | An invoice from standard input
--- task
Order lines arrive on standard input, one per line: an item, a whole-number quantity and a unit price, like `bolt 4 0.25`. Blank lines are skipped. Write a program in three parts, working in **whole cents** throughout, never with floats:

- `parse_line(line)` returns `(item, quantity, price_in_cents)`, such as `("bolt", 4, 25)`. The price must be exact: `"0.29"` is `29` cents.
- `money(cents)` returns the amount as text with two digits after the point: `5` gives `"0.05"`, and `10300` gives `"103.00"`.
- `invoice(lines)` returns the list of output lines:
  - one line per order line, like `"bolt: 4 x 0.25 = 1.00"` (the unit price and the line total, both as money);
  - then `"subtotal: <subtotal>"`;
  - if the subtotal is 100.00 or more, a line `"discount: -<discount>"`, where the discount is 5% of the subtotal in cents, rounded down to a whole cent;
  - last, `"total: <subtotal minus any discount>"`.
- The entry point, under `if __name__ == "__main__":`, prints each line of the invoice for the lines of standard input.

For the input in the Input box, the program prints:

```
bolt: 4 x 0.25 = 1.00
panel: 2 x 45.50 = 91.00
wire: 10 x 1.10 = 11.00
subtotal: 103.00
discount: -5.15
total: 97.85
```
--- starter
import sys

total = 0
for line in sys.stdin.read().splitlines():
    if line.strip():
        item, quantity, price = line.split()
        cost = int(quantity) * float(price)
        total += cost
        print(f"{item}: {quantity} x {price} = {cost}")
print(f"total: {total}")
--- solution
import sys
from decimal import Decimal


def parse_line(line):
    item, quantity, price = line.split()
    return item, int(quantity), int(Decimal(price) * 100)


def money(cents):
    whole, part = divmod(cents, 100)
    return f"{whole}.{part:02d}"


def invoice(lines):
    out = []
    subtotal = 0
    for line in lines:
        if not line.strip():
            continue
        item, quantity, price = parse_line(line)
        cost = quantity * price
        subtotal += cost
        out.append(f"{item}: {quantity} x {money(price)} = {money(cost)}")
    out.append(f"subtotal: {money(subtotal)}")
    total = subtotal
    if subtotal >= 10000:
        discount = subtotal * 5 // 100
        out.append(f"discount: -{money(discount)}")
        total = subtotal - discount
    out.append(f"total: {money(total)}")
    return out


if __name__ == "__main__":
    for line in invoice(sys.stdin.read().splitlines()):
        print(line)
--- check output | The program prints the invoice
bolt: 4 x 0.25 = 1.00
panel: 2 x 45.50 = 91.00
wire: 10 x 1.10 = 11.00
subtotal: 103.00
discount: -5.15
total: 97.85
--- check case | parse_line is exact
parse_line("x 3 0.29")
=> ("x", 3, 29)
--- check test | money pads the cents
money(5) == "0.05" and money(10300) == "103.00" and money(0) == "0.00" and money(99999) == "999.99"
--- check case | Under 100.00: no discount line
invoice(["a 1 99.99"])
=> ["a: 1 x 99.99 = 99.99", "subtotal: 99.99", "total: 99.99"]
--- check case | Exactly 100.00 gets the discount
invoice(["a 1 100"])
=> ["a: 1 x 100.00 = 100.00", "subtotal: 100.00", "discount: -5.00", "total: 95.00"]
--- check case | The discount is rounded down to a whole cent
invoice(["a 1 100.10"])[2:]
=> ["discount: -5.00", "total: 95.10"]
--- check case | No lines at all
invoice(["", "  "])
=> ["subtotal: 0.00", "total: 0.00"]
--- stdin
bolt 4 0.25
panel 2 45.50

wire 10 1.10

+++ problem | Typos that swap two letters
--- task
The most common typing mistake is swapping two neighboring letters: `teh` for `the`. Ordinary edit distance counts that as 2. Write `typo_distance(a, b)`, the fewest edits that turn `a` into `b`, where an edit is an insertion, a deletion, a substitution, **or swapping two neighboring letters**.

Fill a table bottom-up, where the cell for `(i, j)` is the distance between `a[:i]` and `b[:j]`. It follows the edit-distance rule, plus one more choice: when `i` and `j` are both at least 2, `a[i - 1] == b[j - 2]` and `a[i - 2] == b[j - 1]`, the cell can also be the cell two rows up and two columns left, plus 1. Keep only the rows you need, so two strings of 1,000 characters are quick.

For example, `typo_distance("teh", "the")` is `1`, and `typo_distance("kitten", "sitting")` is still `3`.
--- starter
def typo_distance(a, b):
    if a == b:
        return 0
    return max(len(a), len(b))
--- solution
def typo_distance(a, b):
    before = None
    previous = list(range(len(b) + 1))
    for i in range(1, len(a) + 1):
        current = [i] + [0] * len(b)
        for j in range(1, len(b) + 1):
            if a[i - 1] == b[j - 1]:
                current[j] = previous[j - 1]
            else:
                current[j] = 1 + min(previous[j], current[j - 1], previous[j - 1])
            if i > 1 and j > 1 and a[i - 1] == b[j - 2] and a[i - 2] == b[j - 1]:
                current[j] = min(current[j], before[j - 2] + 1)
        before = previous
        previous = current
    return previous[len(b)]
--- check case | A swap is one edit
typo_distance("teh", "the")
=> 1
--- check test | Swaps anywhere, and two swaps
typo_distance("ab", "ba") == 1 and typo_distance("acb", "abc") == 1 and typo_distance("abcd", "badc") == 2
--- check test | Ordinary edits still count as before
typo_distance("kitten", "sitting") == 3 and typo_distance("converse", "conserve") == 2 and typo_distance("same", "same") == 0
--- check test | Empty strings
typo_distance("", "abc") == 3 and typo_distance("abc", "") == 3 and typo_distance("", "") == 0
--- check case | 1,000 characters, every pair swapped
typo_distance("ab" * 500, "ba" * 500)
=> 2
--- check case | 1,000 characters, all different
typo_distance("a" * 1000, "b" * 1000)
=> 1000

+++ problem | How long until everyone hears
--- task
A signal starts at one node of a network and travels along one-way links, each with a delay in milliseconds. The links arrive as lines of text like `"a b 5"`: a link from `a` to `b` with a delay of 5, a whole number of 0 or more. Blank lines are skipped.

Write `broadcast_time(lines, source, nodes)`. It returns how long it takes until every node in the list `nodes` has received the signal: the largest of their earliest arrival times, where the signal is at `source` at time 0. If any node in `nodes` can never be reached, return `-1`. `nodes` may include `source`, and there may be several links between the same two nodes.

It must handle 20,000 nodes quickly. For example, with the links `["1 2 1", "2 3 1", "1 3 4"]` and source `"1"`, the nodes `["1", "2", "3"]` all have the signal after 2 milliseconds.
--- starter
def broadcast_time(lines, source, nodes):
    return len(nodes) - 1
--- solution
import heapq


def broadcast_time(lines, source, nodes):
    graph = {}
    for line in lines:
        if line.strip():
            a, b, delay = line.split()
            graph.setdefault(a, []).append((b, int(delay)))
    best = {source: 0}
    heap = [(0, source)]
    while heap:
        time, node = heapq.heappop(heap)
        if time > best[node]:
            continue
        for neighbour, delay in graph.get(node, []):
            new = time + delay
            if new < best.get(neighbour, float("inf")):
                best[neighbour] = new
                heapq.heappush(heap, (new, neighbour))
    if any(node not in best for node in nodes):
        return -1
    return max(best[node] for node in nodes)
--- check case | Two hops beat one slow link
broadcast_time(["1 2 1", "2 3 1", "1 3 4"], "1", ["1", "2", "3"])
=> 2
--- check case | A node that is never reached
broadcast_time(["1 2 1", "2 3 1"], "1", ["1", "2", "3", "4"])
=> -1
--- check case | Only the source
broadcast_time([], "a", ["a"])
=> 0
--- check case | Several links between two nodes, a loop, and blank lines
broadcast_time(["a b 9", "", "a b 3", "b a 1", "b c 0", "  "], "a", ["b", "c"])
=> 3
--- check test | Links are one way
broadcast_time(["a b 1"], "b", ["a", "b"]) == -1 and broadcast_time(["a b 1"], "a", ["a", "b"]) == 1
--- check case | 20,000 nodes
broadcast_time([f"{i} {i + 1} {i % 3}" for i in range(20000)] + [f"{i} {i + 2} 5" for i in range(0, 20000, 2)], "0", [str(i) for i in range(20001)])
=> 19999

+++ problem | A condition checker
--- task
A ground station has alarm rules like `temp > 30 and not fuel < 10`. Write `check(condition, readings)`, which returns `True` or `False` for a rule, where `readings` is a dict from names to numbers. Write a tokenizer and a recursive-descent parser for this grammar:

```
expr        := term   ("or" term)*
term        := unary  ("and" unary)*
unary       := "not" unary  |  "(" expr ")"  |  comparison
comparison  := value  ("<" | "<=" | ">" | ">=" | "==" | "!=")  value
value       := NUMBER  |  NAME
```

- Tokens: names (a letter or `_`, then letters, digits and `_`; `ch.isalpha()` is true for a letter), numbers (digits with at most one `.`), the six comparison operators (take the longest one that fits), and `(` and `)`. The words `and`, `or` and `not` are keywords, not names. Spaces are skipped.
- A name's value is `readings[name]`; an unknown name raises `KeyError`.
- Anything malformed raises `ValueError`: an unexpected character (a lone `=` or `!` included), a comparison with a missing value or operator, a bracket that is not closed, or tokens left over.

For example, `check("temp > 30 and not fuel < 10", {"temp": 35, "fuel": 50})` is `True`. Do not use `eval`.
--- starter
def check(condition, readings):
    return False
--- solution
KEYWORDS = ("and", "or", "not")
COMPARISONS = ("<", "<=", ">", ">=", "==", "!=")


def tokenize(text):
    tokens = []
    i = 0
    while i < len(text):
        ch = text[i]
        if ch.isspace():
            i += 1
        elif ch.isalpha() or ch == "_":
            start = i
            while i < len(text) and (text[i].isalnum() or text[i] == "_"):
                i += 1
            tokens.append(text[start:i])
        elif ch.isdigit() or ch == ".":
            start = i
            while i < len(text) and (text[i].isdigit() or text[i] == "."):
                i += 1
            number = text[start:i]
            if number.count(".") > 1 or number == ".":
                raise ValueError(f"bad number {number!r} at position {start}")
            tokens.append(float(number) if "." in number else int(number))
        elif text[i:i + 2] in ("<=", ">=", "==", "!="):
            tokens.append(text[i:i + 2])
            i += 2
        elif ch in "<>()":
            tokens.append(ch)
            i += 1
        else:
            raise ValueError(f"unexpected character {ch!r} at position {i}")
    return tokens


class ConditionParser:
    def __init__(self, tokens, readings):
        self.tokens = tokens
        self.pos = 0
        self.readings = readings

    def peek(self):
        return self.tokens[self.pos] if self.pos < len(self.tokens) else None

    def take(self):
        token = self.peek()
        self.pos += 1
        return token

    def expr(self):
        value = self.term()
        while self.peek() == "or":
            self.take()
            right = self.term()
            value = value or right
        return value

    def term(self):
        value = self.unary()
        while self.peek() == "and":
            self.take()
            right = self.unary()
            value = value and right
        return value

    def unary(self):
        if self.peek() == "not":
            self.take()
            return not self.unary()
        if self.peek() == "(":
            self.take()
            value = self.expr()
            if self.take() != ")":
                raise ValueError("expected )")
            return value
        return self.comparison()

    def comparison(self):
        left = self.value()
        op = self.take()
        if op not in COMPARISONS:
            raise ValueError(f"expected a comparison, got {op!r}")
        right = self.value()
        if op == "<":
            return left < right
        if op == "<=":
            return left <= right
        if op == ">":
            return left > right
        if op == ">=":
            return left >= right
        if op == "==":
            return left == right
        return left != right

    def value(self):
        token = self.take()
        if isinstance(token, (int, float)):
            return token
        if isinstance(token, str) and (token[0].isalpha() or token[0] == "_") and token not in KEYWORDS:
            return self.readings[token]
        raise ValueError(f"expected a number or a name, got {token!r}")


def check(condition, readings):
    parser = ConditionParser(tokenize(condition), readings)
    value = parser.expr()
    if parser.peek() is not None:
        raise ValueError(f"unexpected {parser.peek()!r}")
    return value
--- check test | The example rule, both ways
check("temp > 30 and not fuel < 10", {"temp": 35, "fuel": 50}) is True and check("temp > 30 and not fuel < 10", {"temp": 35, "fuel": 5}) is False
--- check test | not binds tighter than and, and and tighter than or
check("not a > 1 or b > 1", {"a": 5, "b": 0}) is False and check("a == 1 or b == 2 and c == 3", {"a": 1, "b": 0, "c": 0}) is True
--- check test | Brackets change the grouping
check("(a == 1 or b == 2) and c != 3", {"a": 1, "b": 0, "c": 3}) is False and check("not (x >= 2.5)", {"x": 2.4}) is True
--- check test | Every comparison, numbers on either side
all([check("1 <= 1", {}), check("2 < 3", {}), check("3 > 2", {}), check("2 >= 2", {}), check("_v2 == 4", {"_v2": 4}), check("4 != x", {"x": 5})]) and not check("1.5 > 2", {})
--- check test | An unknown name raises KeyError
raises(KeyError, lambda: check("speed > 10", {"temp": 1}))
--- check test | Malformed rules raise ValueError
all(raises(ValueError, lambda c=c: check(c, {"a": 1, "b": 2})) for c in ["", "a", "a >", "(a > 1", "a > 1)", "a > 1 b", "a >> 1", "a = 1", "a ! 1", "a > 1 and", "not", "a > b > 1", "and > 1"])
--- check source absent | Does not use eval
\beval\s*\(

+++ question | Functions made in a loop
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
fs = []
for n in [1, 2, 3]:
    fs.append(lambda: n * 10)
print([f() for f in fs])
```
--- answer
[30, 30, 30]
[30,30,30]
--- why
Every lambda keeps the variable `n` itself, not its value when the lambda was made. There is only one `n`, and by the time the lambdas are called the loop has finished and left it at 3. So all three return 30. A default argument (`lambda n=n: n * 10`) or a factory function would give each one its own value.

+++ question | A descriptor and the object's dict
--- ask
A class has a **data descriptor** (one with `__get__` and `__set__`) called `x`. Someone writes `obj.__dict__["x"] = 5` directly, so the object's own dict now holds `x` too. What does `obj.x` give?
--- choice
`5`, because an object's own dict is always checked first.
--- choice correct
Whatever the descriptor's `__get__` returns, because a data descriptor is checked before the object's dict.
--- choice
It raises `AttributeError`, because the name is defined in two places.
--- why
Python looks for a data descriptor on the class first, then in the object's `__dict__`, then for a non-data descriptor or plain class attribute. That order is what stops a checker like `Positive` from being skipped by a value sitting in the dict. Only a descriptor without `__set__` (like `cached_property`) lets the dict win.

+++ question | When a class hook runs
--- ask
`Base` defines `__init_subclass__`, which registers each subclass. A file defines `class Csv(Base): ...` but the program never makes a `Csv` object. Is `Csv` in the registry?
--- choice
No: the hook runs the first time an object of the subclass is made.
--- choice correct
Yes, as soon as the `class Csv` statement has run, usually when its file is imported.
--- choice
Only if `Csv` calls `super().__init__()` in its own `__init__`.
--- why
A `class` statement is code that runs, and `__init_subclass__` runs at the moment the subclass is created, not when objects are made. That is why a plugin registers itself just by being defined, and why a plugin in a file that is never imported never appears.

+++ question | What yield from hands back
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
def inner():
    yield 1
    return "done"

def outer():
    result = yield from inner()
    yield result

print(list(outer()))
```
--- answer
[1, 'done']
[1, "done"]
[1,'done']
[1,"done"]
--- why
`yield from inner()` passes out every value `inner` yields, here just 1. When `inner` returns, its return value becomes the value of the `yield from` expression, so `result` is `"done"`, and `outer` then yields it. `list` collects both values.

+++ question | A sleep inside a coroutine
--- ask
Why does `time.sleep(1)` inside an `async def` slow down every other task, while `await asyncio.sleep(1)` does not?
--- choice
`time.sleep` measures real seconds, while `asyncio.sleep` measures shorter event-loop seconds.
--- choice correct
`time.sleep` blocks the single thread, so the event loop cannot switch to any other task until it ends; `await asyncio.sleep` pauses only this task and hands control back to the loop.
--- choice
`time.sleep` starts a new thread for each call, and the threads have to take turns.
--- why
asyncio runs all its tasks on one thread and switches between them only at an `await`. A blocking call never reaches an `await`, so nothing else can run while it waits. The waiting kind of sleep tells the loop "wake me later", and the loop runs other tasks in the meantime.

+++ question | The second smallest in a heap
--- ask
This function should return the two smallest numbers of a list, smallest first. Which line is wrong?

```python
def two_smallest(nums):
    h = []                            # line 2
    for x in nums:
        heapq.heappush(h, x)          # line 4
    return h[0], h[1]                 # line 5
```
--- choice
Line 2: a heap must be made with a special constructor, not a plain list.
--- choice
Line 4: `heappush` puts the largest item at the front.
--- choice correct
Line 5: only `h[0]` is guaranteed to be the smallest; `h[1]` is just a child of the top, not the second smallest, so it should pop twice instead.
--- why
A heap is only partly ordered: every parent is no bigger than its children. Push 1, 4, 2, 3 and the list is `[1, 3, 2, 4]`, so `h[1]` is 3 while the second smallest is 2. To get the next smallest, call `heappop`, which costs O(log n).

+++ question | bisect_right with repeats
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
import bisect
print(bisect.bisect_right([10, 20, 20, 30], 20))
```
--- answer
3
--- why
`bisect_right` gives the position just past every value equal to 20. The two 20s sit at positions 1 and 2, so the answer is 3. `bisect_left` would give 1, the position of the first 20.

+++ question | A hint is only a label
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
def double(x: int) -> int:
    return x * 2

print(double("ab"))
```
--- answer
abab
--- why
Python does not check type hints while a program runs. `"ab" * 2` is `"abab"`, so the call works and returns a string. A type checker such as mypy would report the call before the program runs, but the program itself only fails if a line cannot work with the value it gets.

+++ question | Adding tenths
--- ask
What does this print?

```python
import math
print(0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3, math.isclose(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3))
```
--- answer
False True
False, True
--- why
0.1 and 0.2 have no exact binary form, so each is stored as the nearest float, and their sum is `0.30000000000000004`, which is not the float nearest to 0.3. `math.isclose` allows a relative difference of up to `1e-9`, so it says the two are close.

+++ question | The cost of an edit distance
--- ask
The bottom-up edit distance that keeps only the previous row compares strings of lengths n and m. What does it cost?
--- choice
O(n + m) time and O(n + m) memory.
--- choice correct
O(n × m) time and O(m) memory.
--- choice
O(n × m) time and O(n × m) memory, because every cell of the table is needed at the end.
--- choice
O(3^n) time, because each cell tries three edits.
--- why
There is one cell per pair `(i, j)`, and each takes a fixed amount of work, so the time is n × m. But each row only needs the row above it, so keeping one previous row of m + 1 numbers is enough. Trying three edits per call is what plain recursion does, before memoisation or the table removes the repeats.

+++ question | When a node is settled
--- ask
BFS marks a square as seen when it is *added* to the queue. Why must Dijkstra's algorithm wait until a node is *popped* from the heap before treating its cost as final?
--- choice
Because heaps are slower than queues, so marking early would waste time.
--- choice correct
Because a route found later can still be cheaper, when it has more steps but lower costs; only the cheapest node on the heap is certain to have no cheaper route left.
--- choice
Because a node added to the heap might never be popped at all.
--- why
In BFS every step costs the same, so the first time you reach a square is by the fewest steps. With costs, B might first be reached for 10 and later, through two more places, for 3. When a node comes off the heap it has the lowest cost of everything left, and with no negative costs no other route can beat it.

+++ question | Grouping from the right by mistake
--- ask
A student writes the `expr` rule as "a term, then optionally `+` or `-` followed by another *expr*", calling `self.expr()` for the right side. What does their calculator give for `10 - 4 - 3`?
--- answer
9
--- why
Calling `expr` for the right side makes everything after the first minus one group: `10 - (4 - 3)`, which is `10 - 1 = 9`. Operators of the same precedence should be done left to right, `(10 - 4) - 3 = 3`, which a `while` loop that folds each new term into the value gives for free.
